#6910. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

#6906. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

#6905. Reggie Dinkins Is Still the Funniest Show on TV

Tracy Morgan and Daniel Radcliffe return for season 2 of their hilarious NBC sitcom somehow even wittier than before.

It’s a gross exaggeration to say that television is a lost art, but we can maybe agree that it has changed. Big-time. But sadly, not all of it is for the better. The binge model pigeonholes every show into a novel, scriptwriters tragically factor in second-screen doomscrolling, and the half-hour situational comedy has been driven to near extinction. Which is why I thank the television gods for The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins and then thank them again that it’s back so soon.

Still sharp, still absurd, and still just plain funny, The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins proves sophomores aren’t always in a slump, with a new season that picks up from season 1’s high point. “More of the same” isn’t bad news here, and if the series can keep pace, we might have a proper TV MVP when other sitcoms can’t make the cut.

Season 2 of Reggie Dinkins, which premiered November 2 on NBC, catches up with Daniel Radcliffe’s anguished auteur, Arthur Tobin, as he documents the show’s hero, Reggie Dinkins (Tracy Morgan). Dinkins is a disgraced football superstar, and he’s hell-bent on rehabilitating his public profile. Arthur still lives in the Dinkins’ crowded New Jersey mansion that also houses Reggie’s levelheaded ex-wife and manager Monica (Erika Alexander), their son Carmelo (Jalyn Hall), Reggie’s younger fiancée Brina (Precious Way), and Bobby Moynihan as Rusty, Reggie’s freeloading BFF who is too integral to the family’s cohesion to kick to the curb.

This season, Arthur grows ever closer to the Dinkins clan, despite his own hyperawareness of his documentary ethics violations. (Episode 2 of this season at last interrogates how compromised the in-progress project has been since the start.) Most of the ensemble, Radcliffe and Morgan included, exists in a sort of stasis—this is a network sitcom, after all—but it’s Precious Way who shines as the much-improved standout of the bunch.

Formerly “just” the younger woman in Reggie’s life last season, Brina has grown into the soul of the show. Along with Way’s buoyant charisma, there’s a newfound depth to Brina that wasn’t present in her before. I’ll admit, I thought she was grating at first blush, and I was ready to write her off as a shallow antagonist who didn’t seem involved with Reggie or the family in earnest. But in season 2, she’s evolved into a phenomenal supporting player who instantly brightens the room and contrasts beautifully with the others, most of all Radcliffe’s flummoxed British artiste. (A throwaway gag involving her “Voice Bucket Challenge” had me cracking up at my desk.) It’s a mere single component upgrade we’re talking about here, but it elevates Reggie Dinkins to a much higher degree this time around.

Jalyn Hall, too, grows in his once-thankless role as Reggie and Monica’s teenage son, Carmelo. Through a new overarching storyline involving a forbidden romance, which becomes a linchpin for most of season 2’s first half, Carmelo crystallizes in his specific variant of “Blerd” that is more refreshing than derivative. He’s not just “The Geek,” as you’d find in most sitcoms. He’s fluid enough to perform the function without killing the buzz.

The first four episodes of the season, provided to the press, keep up season 1’s winning formula—22-minute-sitcom hijinks that unfold against an airy atmosphere with well-timed punchlines. Daniel Radcliffe and Tracy Morgan further assert their powers as TV’s unlikeliest duo, and I can’t get enough of them. I pray there are yet more improbable excuses to ensure multiple seasons of a single documentary’s production. Sure, the rhythms are familiar now, but it’s welcome fun. Reggie Dinkins is a potent reminder of what television used to be and what it still can be if we’re brave enough to let it.

#6904. This Is What Winter 2026 Has in Store for Every Star Sign, According to an Astrologer

It’s been a chaotic time, but the end of autumn finds Mercury and Venus ushering in a fresh start under the twinkling holiday lights following their retrogrades. To explain: each retrograde has a “post-shadow,” which essentially means a bit of a paradigm shift. Mercury rules communication, while Venus rules love and money, so conversations around these themes might finally feel less muddled and more smooth-sailing. Enmeshments may untangle, responsibilities may firm up, and reciprocity may finally be restored.

As ever, the winter solstice arrives on December 21, marking the shortest day of the year and the start of Capricorn season, a sign that urges us to think practically and long-term. This isn’t just about dreaming up New Year’s resolutions: the Sun in Capricorn asks us big questions about how we want to invest our time and energy, and what we want to build for our future, so there’ll be a lot of that this winter.

It’s not all plain sailing, though. We’ve got a Mars retrograde approaching on January 10, 2027, which could brew up a number of conflicts and interpersonal obstacles. Fortunately, the solar eclipse in Aquarius lands a month later, on February 6, which will usher in a sense of community, with a few new faces entering the scene. And then, a lunar eclipse in Virgo, on February 20, will bring us back down to earth, reminding us of our everyday responsibilities and commitments.

So yeah, a lot going on. But what does winter have in store for your sign specifically? Scroll on to find out.

January’s Mars retrograde is actually this winter’s highlight for you, Aries. You’re the go-getter of the zodiac, and Mars, your planetary ruler, is the warrior. But the retrograde will have you slowing down and rethinking your goals, particularly around work and ongoing projects (which will be a good thing). Mars’s reentry into fellow fire sign Leo urges you to get clear on what—and who!—you are really passionate about, and cut the distractions in your life that are keeping you from pursuing your heart’s desires.

Venus ends its retrograde on November 13 and clears its shadow at the start of the season, promoting a peaceful environment, especially in the workplace, and will perhaps find you starting some gigs that feel particularly worth your while. The solar eclipse in Aquarius later this winter could also find you embarking on a big new career path. The movers and shakers in your industry may swap places, and with so much change in the air, you might be able to position yourself in a new role.

The season opens with you getting all your ducks in a row, Gemini, but by the end of the winter, everything you have organized for yourself in terms of your workflow and everyday responsibilities might just go out the window as you embark on a big adventure abroad, or somehow otherwise step out of your comfort zone and your usual routine. But, hey, that’s okay! The organization you cultivate now will create the safe home base for you to jump off into new adventures.

The upcoming eclipses are the highlight of your winter—you are ruled by the moon, after all, so its movements suggest changes and opportunities for you, Cancer. The solar eclipse in Aquarius, for example, could be a powerful one for cutting ties: you might finally get the closure you seek, and you might settle a debt or otherwise correct an imbalance. The lunar eclipse in Virgo stands to bring communication breakthroughs: seemingly minor details may be revealed that result in major revelations.

Late winter will likely bring plenty of drama and intrigue to Leos. The solar eclipse in Aquarius could mean that someone’s secret crush on you isn’t so secret anymore! Important truths are said to be revealed during eclipse season, and this one is all about your relationships. If you’re already in love, between the eclipses and Mars retrograde, this could be a major period of transformation for you and your partner, one which could bring you much closer… or, it could mark a turning point if you decide you’ve changed too much since the start of your relationship.

The lunar eclipse in your zodiac sign, Virgo, stands to be one of the most transformative periods of the year for you—but first, the solstice! The winter solstice marks the sun’s entry into fellow earth sign Capricorn, which could bring fun, romance, and creativity your way. The lunar eclipse in your zodiac sign could mark a tremendous period of change, both personally and within your relationships. Something about your identity, how you see yourself, and who you want to be in your partnerships could undergo a brilliant revolution. Intriguing introductions could take place—the new version of yourself that you see in the mirror could be the most striking!

Venus clears its post-retrograde shadow in mid-December, which could find you finally feeling like yourself again: clear-headed, cute, and ready to mingle! On the surface, you might be exploring and enjoying a new aesthetic, but on a deeper level, you might have a powerful new understanding of your values and a renewed sense of self-esteem. The solar eclipse in Aquarius later this winter could bring the relationship reboot you’re seeking, as well as creative breakthroughs. Aquarius is the zodiac sign of the future, so this period could feel challenging if you’re clinging to the past—but if you embrace change, novel opportunities may arise.

You may have struggled finding the right words this autumn, Scorpio—but early winter brings newfound clarity, and could find you experiencing communication breakthroughs, especially around the winter solstice and Capricorn season. Also, new places to explore in your neighborhood, or new friends to mingle with, are likely to enter the scene. The Mars retrograde and lunar eclipse later in the season could mark a huge shift in your social life: you might let go of old friends, or perhaps you will find a new group or community that transforms your life.

Your birthday season spans November 22 to December 21 and, as ever, this’ll be a time for exciting introductions and being on the receiving end of plenty of attention. Your focus may shift to finances as the sun enters Capricorn on the winter solstice, and perhaps you’ll receive a promotion at work or a meaningful gift from someone special! Career developments could take place during the lunar eclipse in Virgo towards the end of the winter—but first, shocking revelations and communication breakthroughs may arrive during the solar eclipse in Aquarius.

Capricorn season begins on the winter solstice, December 21, and marks the beginning of an energizing period for you. You might receive a lot of attention, meanwhile winning people over could come easily. Mars retrograde could find you rethinking travel plans or decisions about education, but ultimately, the shifts you make this winter are likely to find you expanding your horizons in an even bigger way later in the year. You’re figuring out what’s really worth your time, and during the lunar eclipse in particular, you may also be figuring out what’s worth your money.

Between a solar eclipse and a Mercury retrograde in your zodiac sign later this winter on February 26, this stands to be a significant period of personal transformation for Aquarians. Eclipses can bring surprises and big emotions: an unexpected chain of events could find you realising just how much you’ve changed, and powerful feelings about who you were, and who you stand to become, could arise. This is a time for reconnecting with yourself, whether discovering a new look or getting honest about what you need in relationships. Mercury retrograde means you may waffle back and forth before landing on decisions: give yourself time, explore all opportunities, and don’t rush through the process.

Big relationship shifts could take place for you this winter, Pisces. If passion has fallen flat, this might be a time when there is no more pretending. If things are still hot, then such shifts might be more about the two of you working through how you’ll help each other achieve your goals. The lunar eclipse also stands to be a big period in your relationships: surprising feelings and shocking news could surface, but so could opportunities to show each other how unshakable your love is. The ultimate question is: is there trust and a strong sense of responsibility in the relationship? If there’s no balance between revelry and reliability, this lunar eclipse could beg you to get real and recalibrate your expectations.

#6902. Meet the ‘Black Doves’ Season 2 Cast and Characters

New assassins! New spies! New... [redacted], we guess??

The spy series Black Doves is back for a second season after two years, dropping all at once, on Guy Fawkes Day of all days, November 5. Need a refresher before you dive back into the Netflix series? Here’s everything you need to know about the new and returning cast of Black Doves season 2. Some of the new characters are such big secrets, we actually don’t have any details yet. Exciting!!

The first season, set at Christmas time, followed a politician’s wife with a double life as a spy in a covert organization called the Black Doves. When the man she was having an affair with is murdered, her handler calls in an assassin and old friend of hers for protection. The two investigate her lover’s death and discover that he was part of something bigger and more dangerous than they knew. Obviously the second season will up the already sky-high stakes, and introduce new threats. That also means new characters and cast members. So let’s get into it!

Helen is a spy who “happens” to be married to a prominent conservative politician.

Keira Knightley had a dual breakout in Hollywood in 2002 with Bend it Like Beckham and then, the next year, with Love Actually. Shortly thereafter she starred in Pirates of the Caribbean and Pride & Prejudice. The latter earned her her first Academy Award nominated. She was nominated again in 2014 for The Imitation Game and has two BAFTA nominations, for that and for Atonement. She was also nominated for an Olivier after starring in The Misanthrope on the West End. Other credits include King Arthur, Never Let Me Go, Domino, The Duchess, Anna Karenina, Colette, and Begin Again.

You might also know her from Last Tango in Halifax, Julia, Where the Heart Is, and The Paradise. She is also a stage actress with credits on the West End including Blood Brothers, Little Shop of Horrors, Guys and Dolls, and Betty Blue Eyes.

Williams is a mercenary with a grudge against Sam.

She will star in the upcoming supernatural series The Witch Farm and also had a supporting role in the 2021 film Operation Mincemeat.

Neve Campbell is a Canadian actress best known for playing Sidney Prescott in the Scream franchise. Before that, she broke through with leading roles in the series Party of Five and the film The Craft. Since then, Campbell had leading or recurring roles on shows like Medium, The Philanthropist, Grey’s Anatomy, Welcome to Sweden, Manhattan, House of Cards, and The Lincoln Lawyer. On the big screen, she was recently in Black Bag and Sacrifice. (And her Halloween costume this year was Bend it Like Beckham, which ofc starred Knightley.)

He’s set to appear in the second season of A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms. His other television credits include Shardlake, Wolfe, and We Hunt Together. His film credits include Rogue One, Free Fire, and Resurrection.

Patrick works for an unknown, new organization, and offers to help Sam.

Sam Riley starred in the 2012 adaptation of Jack Kerouac’s novel On the Road. You might know him as Mr. Darcy, NBD, in Pride and Prejudice and Zombies. Riley played a raven in Maleficent and received critical acclaim for Control. His other credits include the 2020 Rebecca, Brighton Rock, and Free Fire.

#6900. Tommy’s Take on Red–the Color Fashion Can’t (and Shouldn’t) Quit

One of fashion’s earliest street-style photographers, Tommy Ton has always had a unique point of view on what makes an outfit sing. His subjects—individuals with style and substance in equal measure—aren’t following trends. They’re expressing something deeper, and that difference is what makes his shots so compelling. Sure, the clothes are always great, but his takes capture the true essence of the wearer. In monthly installments surrounding a fashion theme, he'll explore the outfits that caught his eye and why. Here’s Tommy’s Take.

There is something about red that fashion simply cannot get enough of. Crimson, poppy, scarlet, cherry, oxblood—the shades are seemingly endless, and so is our fascination with them. What began as an easy way to inject color into an otherwise neutral wardrobe has evolved into something much bigger. At this point, I would say fashion has developed a full-blown case of scarlet fever.

Traditionally, I associated red with evening dressing. There was something inherently glamorous, perhaps even a little theatrical, about wearing it after dark. But that distinction has all but disappeared. Red has found its way into every corner of the wardrobe, from casual daytime looks to evening ensembles, and some of the most compelling ways I've seen it worn lately are decidedly understated.

Not so long ago, a red accessory was the easiest entry point: a handbag, a pair of shoes, or perhaps a flash of red socks. It was the little punctuation mark that made an outfit feel more considered. Today, however, we're embracing red as the starting point of an entire look. Take Polo Ralph Lauren's red Harrington jacket, a beautifully executed Chanel cap-toe pump, or even a luxurious red sweater casually tied around the waist. I particularly love the latter, bringing an unexpected flash of color to jeans or tailored trousers. It's a simple styling gesture that can transform an entire outfit.

And then there is the appeal of wearing red from head to toe. Who could forget the monochromatic look that came down Michael Rider's Celine runway this past season? There was an almost collective gasp. Seeing such a saturated look presented with that kind of conviction was a reminder of just how arresting the color can be. Red wasn't merely an accent; it was the entire statement.

There is a certain pleasure in committing to one color, allowing different textures, proportions, and shades to create interest. A scarlet knit paired with matching trousers, or a cherry-red anorak worn over an equally saturated ensemble, makes quite a statement. After seasons of pared-back palettes and quiet luxury, there is something refreshing about dressing with a little more conviction.

Depending on the shade, red can be romantic, provocative, sporty, polished, or even a little rebellious. Cherry has a playful quality, scarlet carries a certain theatricality, while oxblood offers a moodier, more understated interpretation. Perhaps that's why our infatuation with the color has lasted so long. It has been reinterpreted countless times, yet somehow manages to feel exciting all over again.

Whatever the reason, I'm not quite ready to give up on red. Whether worn as a subtle accent, an everyday staple, or embraced from head to toe, it has a way of making even the most familiar wardrobe feel exciting again. And judging by the way we're wearing it now, I don't see our scarlet fever subsiding anytime soon.

Want more? Shop Tommy’s October Take, below.

Is more more, or is less more? For years, we’ve been told that good taste is knowing when to stop. Coco Chanel’s famous advice about looking in the mirror before leaving the house and removing one thing has become something of a fashion commandment for many. But I’ve always wondered what happens when we do the opposite. What if, instead of taking something away, you add another necklace, another ring, another earring, another bracelet? What if the most interesting thing about getting dressed is not knowing when to stop, but having the confidence not to?

After seasons of quiet luxury and wardrobes edited down to the essentials, I find myself drawn to a more expressive approach to accessorizing. Somewhere along the way, restraint became synonymous with sophistication, and the absence of adornment was elevated into a marker of taste. But minimalism, for all its elegance, can become its own uniform. When everyone is wearing the same monogram pendant or oversized belt buckle, how much individuality is left?

Jewelry is most interesting when it disrupts that uniformity. I love the woman with rings on nearly every finger, a wrist stacked with cuffs and bracelets, necklaces layered at different lengths and crowded with pendants, and earrings that seem to be having a conversation of their own. There is something wonderfully assured about a look that refuses to be fully resolved. It doesn’t ask whether every piece matches, whether the proportions are balanced, or whether one more accessory might be too much. It simply embraces the pleasure of wearing what you love.

What strikes me most about this more-is-more approach is that it rarely feels calculated. The best examples are always a little imperfect: a delicate chain mixed with a heavy pendant; a diamond ring beside an inexpensive silver band; a charm-laden brooch that clinks with every movement. Nothing needs to match, nor should it. The most compelling combinations look as if they came together over time, collected from different places, people, and phases of one’s life. The result feels lived-in rather than meticulously styled.

Perhaps this is why the current appetite for accessorizing feels so refreshing. Not everything needs to be versatile, timeless, or sensible. Some things are worth wearing simply because they make you happy.

So the next time you reach for just one necklace, consider adding another. Then perhaps a third. Put on the rings you usually save for evenings out, stack your bracelets, and let the earrings be a little outrageous. You can always take something off. But there is a certain joy in discovering that you don’t want to.

Want more? Shop Tommy’s September Take, below.

In an industry as vast and layered as fashion, it’s worth asking: Who inspires the tastemakers? Who shapes the eye of the people who decide what is photographed and published, what earns a place on the runway, and what ultimately finds its way onto the racks of the world’s most coveted department stores?

Designers long occupied the highest tier of fashion’s hierarchy of influence. Together with editors, journalists, stylists, and buyers, they defined what we desired before we had the language to desire it ourselves. Now that we live under the influence of algorithms and celebrity and influencer culture, genuine style has become increasingly difficult to distinguish from mere visibility. True originality is quieter—and infinitely more compelling.

Several times a year, the industry converges on the world’s fashion capitals. Equal parts work and reunion, Fashion Week carries the unmistakable energy of summer camp, bringing together friends and colleagues who have spent months apart. Between the action of the shows themselves, another spectacle quietly unfolds on the sidewalks and in the queues. Fashion week remains one of the world’s greatest people-watching opportunities.

It is often here, unexpectedly, that the most memorable style reveals itself. Not on the runway, but on an unfamiliar face glimpsed again and again throughout the week. You begin to notice the subtle details: the effortless knot of a belt cinching a jacket, the unexpected layering of heirloom pendants, the confidence of someone dressing entirely for themselves rather than for the cameras. Before long, you’ve developed a style crush on someone whose name you may never learn.

This is an ode to those women: the ones who influence the influential. The insiders whose instinct, rather than their following, has earned the admiration of fashion’s most discerning insiders. They are, quite simply, your favorite fashion insider’s favorite fashion insider.

Want more? Shop Tommy’s July Take, below.

Gabriella Karefa-Johnson proves the bra top has graduated beyond the beach. Layered beneath an oversized cotton poplin shirt and paired with a lace-trim slip skirt, the look feels effortlessly city-ready, while Chanel’s cap-toe pumps add the perfect finishing touch.

There’s a reason white dominates every summer wardrobe: It reflects heat while always looking polished. Black accessories, including Prada’s pouch-shaped bag—the silhouette of the season—give the monochromatic look welcome definition.

Go beyond bohemian styling. Marta Oldrini proves a sheer skirt can feel decidedly city-ready, pairing the airy silhouette with this summer’s trending butter yellow, minimalist flip-flops, and understated accessories for a look that feels perfectly at home on the streets of Paris—even in a heat wave.

Heat waves demand a softer take on denim. Swap rigid silhouettes for a breezy chambray shirt and relaxed jeans—a combination that feels just as polished but far more comfortable.

The Row’s impeccably tailored shorts offer a polished alternative to the casual cutoffs of summers past. Finished with sleek loafers—a refreshing change from the season’s ubiquitous sandals—the result is effortlessly chic for city dressing.

The easiest way to style a statement skirt? Keep everything else understated. A simple tank creates the perfect backdrop, while snakeskin shoes—the exotics trend is maintaining its stronghold—add just the right amount of fashion polish.

Want more? Shop Tommy’s June Take, below.

Fashion experts have always emphasized investing in a wardrobe of foundational essentials: the perfect white shirt, a tailored suit, a little black dress, and so on. But once you’ve found those building blocks, the next question becomes: how do you avoid getting caught up in the constant seasonal cycle of trends just to make your closet feel relevant again?

The answer is quite simple: focus on accessories. Small accents, whether it’s a bag in an interesting color, a brooch that has a deeper meaning, or a belt that invites a closer look, can instantly give your wardrobe more clarity and a fresh perspective. Clothes come and go, but a beautifully curated collection of accessories stays in rotation for longer than a single season when you invest wisely in the right pieces. After all, no one ever said you can have too many accessories.

If there has been one brand that has sparked a renewed interest in accessorizing, it’s without a doubt Celine under Michael Rider. From the very first look of his debut collection, what immediately caught your eye was that wrist full of charm bracelets, the hand stacked with rings, and the oversized gold medallion belt buckle slightly peeking out from beneath a blazer. It was a sign that all those never-ending discussions about quiet luxury were finally going to be put to rest.

It was a clever exercise in merchandising as well, as you couldn’t help but be drawn to all those delicious accessories that perfectly accented each look. It was like a fashion enthusiast’s version of walking into a candy store. Whether it was a silk foulard effortlessly tied around a model’s jacket or half-tucked beneath a lapel, it made you want to replicate the styling and adopt it into your wardrobe immediately. You could sense that this collection was going to influence fashion at the highest level before trickling down to the masses; the true mark of an impactful collection is when its imprint can be seen on fashion insiders the following season.

Fast-forward nine months later to Paris Fashion Week, outside a show at the Petit Palais, and all those chic gestural styling cues from the Celine runway had made their way to the street in an unexpected masterclass in styling. Caught in conversation, it was quite a sight to see how three fashion professionals (Sarah Park, Ramya Giangola, and Lisa Aiken) unintentionally complemented one another and somehow captured the mood of the moment.

Everything that felt timeless yet modern was there: glimpses of bold primary colors, subtle accents of gold and silver hardware, small leather wallets and pouchettes, and classic black frames. It was very much in the French way of dressing, where the individual nuances of each look made you curious and encouraged you to reconsider what you already own. After closely scanning each look from head to toe, it became clear that each of these women had adopted elements of the Celine preppy bourgeois aesthetic. Yet it wasn’t a head-to-toe runway look that made their outfits stand out. Rather, it was the fact that each woman carefully borrowed styling cues from Celine and made them distinctly her own. You could easily sense the playfulness and joy in the way they got dressed.

What’s crucial when reevaluating your wardrobe each season is looking for pieces that offer day-to-night versatility and can be effortlessly thrown together without too much thought. Practicality matters, but it’s also about allowing yourself to step slightly outside your comfort zone. Whether it’s buying the right jewelry or embracing color, you don’t want to feel intimidated by your accessories or uncertain about when it’s appropriate to wear them. You want these timeless investment pieces to feel like lifelong treasured friends that come out to play when the occasion is right. They are ultimately what truly bring an outfit together.

With a nod to the Celine Spring 26 runway, Lisa Aiken nonchalantly drapes a bold printed Celine silk foulard off her shoulder in a way that feels modern and chic. The flashes of rouge red from her Khaite pouchette and scarf perfectly balance the gold accents.

Ramya Giangola effortlessly remixes old Celine by Phoebe Philo with new Celine by Michael Rider in her own unique way that ticks all the boxes. From the large gold medallion belt to the silk foulard tied with small leather pouchettes, they instantly give her vintage Celine skirt from spring 2010 a playful update.

Sarah Park’s monochromatic layers of red knits are a classic nod to the American Parisian preppy trend but with a more refined elevated take as she perfectly pairs the right amount of costume jewelry completed by her signature belted duo of mini wallets from The Row.

#6899. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

As threat actors adopt more targeted and convincing tactics, education leaders need practical ways to reduce risk without undermining the open, connected environments their institutions depend on.

This is a very interesting development if it survives this election cycle and if it isn’t dependent on uniquely charismatic candidates like Talarico. If the Democrats can reckon with a lost source of political power, then that cannot be a bad development.

#6879. Our Favorite Loafer Now Comes in Menswear’s Favorite Leather

A store I worked at in the 2010s used to stock dozens of styles of well-known shoe brands, and while our inventory was well-stocked, the rare cordovan loafers that popped up were almost mythical. There would be murmurs of a possible restock and by the time I showed up for my shift they’d be gone. In other words, cordovan loafers aren’t always easy to come by. I bring this up because Meermin’s 25th Anniversary Unlined Penny Loafer is now available in a gorgeous burgundy shell cordovan—one that, considering the nature of both the material and the build, should hold up for another 25 years of consistent wear.

First, a quick little primer on Meermin: the Mallorca-based, family-owned shoemaker started in, you guessed it, 2001. They decided to put decades of luxury industry experience into their own, less-marked-up, dexterously-made products. 25 years later, and the Unlined Penny Loafer is the catalog’s crown jewel—tenaciously timeless, super versatile and as comfortable as loafers come—and thus the bedrock the brand has riffed-upon for the anniversary.

We should also note that shell cordovan leather is very much not like the other leathers. For starters, it can only come from horsehide, and specifically from the underlayers of the rump, which is the (actual) technical term for the ass and also a very finite region of hide. Cordovan is notoriously sturdy. Like, damn-near-indestructible, will-outlive-the-cockroaches, kind of durable. And, like many long-lasting materials, it develops an inimitable character with age, such that one could argue it gets more beautiful with each scuff.

And if you’re worried about the bottoms of a pair of shoes designed to last decades—and millions of steps—the flexible chestnut bark-tanned leather outsoles Meermin employs are affixed with a resoleable Goodyear welt, so you won’t need to fret about the inevitably asymmetrical wear-and-tear. If there was a single nit to pick with the endeavor, it’d be that these are a limited release and don’t allow for a whole lot of waffling. So, if you’re in the market for some forever loafers, your search could be over in a matter of minutes. Head on over to Meermin to check ‘em out in all their glory.

#6875. The Real-Life Diet of Christian McCaffrey, Who Thinks Cold Plunges Are a Metaphor for Life

The last time Christian McCaffrey was featured in this magazine, in 2020, he posed poolside, ogling the camera in nothing but a pair of crisp white Calvin Kleins. The All-Pro running back still looks like Adonis, but a lot has changed in six years. For one, he's a 49er now, and at the midway point of the 2026 NFL season he and quarterback Brock Purdy, a popular MVP candidate, have San Francisco sitting pretty atop the NFC. McCaffrey, now in his 11th NFL season, also recently became a father, a development that's thrown the demands of professional football into stark relief. “I want to spend all day with my kids,” he confesses. “But obviously I play football, so it's not always like that.”

When McCaffrey's not chasing after his springy 15-month old daughter, his days consist of morning prayer ("it puts me in the right head space"), film study (he goes through “a bunch of notebooks every year”), cold plunges ("a good metaphor for life, actually"), and meditation, a practice he embraced this past summer under the auspices of the movement coordinator and spiritual guru Ido Portal. “Some days were great,” McCaffrey says. “Some days were crippling.”

But the week we spoke, the 49ers happened to be on their bye, which meant the typically scrupulous 30-year-old could permit himself that rare indulgence: a slice of his wife Olivia Culpo's carrot cake.

Christian McCaffrey: It's funny, even on the bye week, you can never just take a week off. You always have to do something. For me, you get a good week in the middle of the season to just get your body back, get your mind back. I think a lot of it is just being with my family. It's so good for your mind, your body, your soul. And then in the mornings, I'll do whatever workout—the team gives us different workouts to do throughout the week so that you stay up to date or you're at least moving a little bit. And then recovery work, any treatment you have to do, and later in the afternoon you'll do another mobility recovery session just to stay moving so you don't get stale.

I think the first thing that I did right was marry correctly. When you play in the NFL, it just takes all of your energy and time, frankly. I mean, I want to spend all day with my kids, but obviously I play football too, so it's not always like that. So to be able to still do my job and know that they're taken care of—my wife's a super mom, so I still do everything I need to do. And when you come home, to be able to look at my daughter's face and hold my son and just spend time with them, it makes those moments so much better.

My son is a month old, but my daughter—she's 14, 15 months now, and she's all over the place. If there's something to play with, she'll play with it. She is obsessed with that little Roomba. She won't stop. She'll just click the button on the Roomba 24/7, and then when it starts moving, she gets scared and runs to me. She likes climbing and moving, and we just try to keep her moving as much as possible, and she loves it. Just watching her smile and enjoy life is the greatest thing ever.

For me, the staples are nutrition. Those are things you can control, always. So if I'm eating healthy, if I'm getting the correct amount of sleep, if I have my Dymatize shake after my workout or my prehab session or whatever it is, getting those positive calories into your system at the same times every day is something everybody can control. That's why partnering with them was easy for me, because it was a product that actually helped me and also tastes good.

I like the Fruity Pebbles. I'm still a kid at heart, so anything that tastes like an old cereal, I'm going to go ahead and do that. But I mean, there's so many things out there now. It's almost overwhelming how many modalities and recovery tools and different things you can do. But I think the thing I've learned most, as somebody that's experimented with all of that stuff, is your mindset and your ability to just stay moving is critical. I think you can try all of the different tools, but if you're not doing the staples—moving, recovering from moving, eating correctly, having the right nutrition, sleeping correctly—then a lot of this stuff is not going to move the needle as much as you think.

I used to, and then I got way too obsessed over it. But I had an Oura ring for a long time, and I'll track my sleep in the offseason with that.

Well, it's fascinating. After the season, you think you're sleeping well, but your body and your mind go through a lot over an NFL season. So you think, “Oh, I can rest now.” But I was looking at the sleep scores early in the offseason and they were horrible. Maybe I slept eight hours, but it's the worst sleep ever. You're detoxing… I mean, the reality is that you take a lot of drugs to help you play. You have to do all these things to help you get back on the field on Sunday, with all the hits you're taking. So it takes a while to get back to a good sleep score. But then once I get the ball rolling it’s like, "Okay, I'm in a good place now.”

I don't think anyone loves getting into the cold plunge. But everyone loves it after. The cold plunge is a good metaphor for life, actually. The things that are usually the most challenging are usually the most beneficial. So yeah, I've learned to like it. And again, when you're playing and it's in season, you're so focused on the practice and the game and getting your body right that the things that you think just become routine and habit.

I always start my morning with prayer. That's been a staple, and if I can connect with God as much as possible, it just puts me in the right head space. It reminds me why I'm doing this and it eliminates a lot of distractions. I meditated a lot this offseason, especially in the summer. I worked with a guy, Ido Portal, who's a legend and has had a massive influence on me. And for six weeks straight, we did one hour of stillness every morning. That was a huge challenge, but a really moving experience. I still do that all the time. I don't have time to do it for an hour anymore, but if I can get 20 minutes in the mornings, or even throughout the day at a random point where I have a little window, I try to do that as much as possible. That just helps your nervous system get back to where it's supposed to be, especially in a high-stress week.

Yeah, I'd never done that before until this summer. And Ido, he's a character, man, but he's a beautiful person and an unbelievable human being. It was very moving working with him for a summer, and it wasn't negotiable. We were doing it, starting at seven o'clock every morning, one hour straight, complete stillness, can't move, nothing. It's only something you can explain through experience. It wasn't about whether or not you turned your brain off. There was no shot. For six weeks, I didn't shut my brain off one time. It's more about, can you focus and refocus on the task at hand as best as possible? Some days were great. Some days were crippling. Some days you're cramping because you're in a seated crisscross position and you're like, "I'm in so much pain." And then you get done and you're like, "Wait a second, I wasn't in as much pain as I thought." It was such a moving experience because, again, you said you were going to do something, therefore you need to do it. You're going to find out what your purpose is through this six-week experience. It might be different than the guy next to you. It might be different than Ido's, but you're going to go through it, and you're going to learn a lot about yourself and you'll be better for it.

You watch so much film with the team all the time, but people think we just go out and practice and play. But really, we have a job. We're sitting in meetings taking notes all day. I mean, you go through a bunch of notebooks every year in the NFL and you're watching film all the time. I've watched less film as I've gotten older, but I'll watch a lot of blitz tape to make sure I'm prepared for different tells, when they're blitzing, when they're not. But running back is such an instinctual position, so I try to train the instinct more than predict things based on what you're seeing. There are certain things you can pick up on, but that can also be a crutch, so I try to find the middle ground.

Look, I try to eat as clean as possible, but there's a time to indulge. If you're feeling great and you have a couple of days off—if my wife's going to make a dessert, I'm going to eat it. It's good for the soul.

She makes a carrot cake and, to this day, there's not a better one I've had. Or my mom will be in town and cook something. Or her mom will be in town and cook something. So I'll definitely indulge every once in a while, but throughout the week I eat clean.

In Real-Life Diet, athletes, celebrities, and other high performers talk about their diet, exercise routines, and pursuit of wellness. Keep in mind that what works for them might not necessarily be healthy for you.

#6874. The Remarkable Election-Night Story We Should All Be Talking About

Don’t let the sound of the blue tsunami drown out what happened in Wisconsin.

Drowned out by the sound of the blue tsunami making landfall everywhere, a remarkable story took place in Wisconsin. The state’s Democratic party, almost gerrymandered out of existence a few years ago and helpless, succeeded in electing not only a governor but also majorities in both houses of the state legislature.

The Democrats already enjoy a majority on the state supreme court, so I feel confident in saying that the state is now completely free of the dead hand of the administration of Scott Walker, the goggle-eyed homunculus hired by Koch Industries to manage their subsidiary once known as the state of Wisconsin. They also flipped two congressional seats: Paul Ryan’s old seat, now held by one of his old aides, and that of the inexcusable Derrick Van Orden, famous corrector of lounging congressional pages.

(What is truly delicious is that Van Orden was dimed out on that rampage by his fellow Wisconsin congressman Mark Pocan, who was reelected Tuesday night, though he ran unopposed.)

Wisconsin’s new governor made a visit to the shebeen a few years back when he was Milwaukee County executive, which is a position of some importance in state politics. (It also served as Walker’s jumping-off point.) His pathway to the governor’s office was, shall we say, eccentric. From the Milwaukee Journal Sentinel:

But his campaign for governor was something of a roller coaster.

(Hong, it should be noted, turned out to be the anti–Haley Stevens, campaigning vigorously for Crowley throughout the general election campaign. Good on her.)

It was political activist Ben Wikler who turned the shebeen on to David Crowley, and Wikler is going to go down in Wisconsin political history as the guy who rebuilt the state’s Democratic party almost from scratch. And he did so in keeping with the state’s proud progressive history and with the state’s one-word motto: Forward.

#6871. After I Kicked Drugs and Alcohol, I Found a New Addiction: Cheating on My Fiancée

Thirteen years of sobriety taught me that the most dangerous thing for an addict isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And I keep getting away with it.

Ethical nonmonogamy relies on a foundation of total transparency. Partners openly agree to terms and continually negotiate them together. Betrayals such as infidelity can take many shapes and break the agreement a couple has made, monogamous or not. For people in recovery, however, the adrenaline of secret keeping can feel familiar. Clinicians call this behavioral, or process, addiction—in which compulsion lies in the thrill of the chase rather than the payoff. It doesn’t excuse betrayal, but it can help explain how a person committed to honesty ends up living a lie.

For this installment of the Secret Lives of Men, we spoke to Owen,* a 36-year-old brand strategist with 13 years of sobriety who spent most of the first two years of his relationship cheating on the partner he plans to marry. His story begs the question: What does it mean to carry out “living amends,” an Alcoholics Anonymous term for permanently changing your behavior for the better, when you aren’t ready to confess?

*Names and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

Once a week, my fiancée has a therapy session over a video call that she takes from our bedroom. We both work from home, so while that happens, I’m usually barricaded in my office with noise-canceling headphones on, keeping up with work. Recently, I came out to the kitchen to pour a coffee after her hour was up, and she wasn’t home.

She takes walks, so there was no reason to panic. But my mind went where it has gone a lot lately: She found out. By whatever cruel trick of fate, she knew, and she was out there walking it off, letting the anger curdle. When she came back, she would bring hell with her.

I called once. She didn’t pick up. I didn’t try again. For half an hour, I paced the apartment unable to get a single thing done. I checked my phone every few seconds. I rehearsed what I’d say when she walked through the door: which parts I’d confess, which parts I’d still try to save. I worried myself sick. When she finally walked back in, it turned out she’d just been on a long phone call with her sister. She took off her headset, kissed me hello, and asked what I had in mind for dinner. My heart kept pounding long after she’d walked into the kitchen.

Until two years ago, I could say with real pride that I had never cheated on anyone. Not once. Not even when I was drinking and doing all those drugs, back when I lied about nearly everything else. I’d had opportunities, but infidelity was the one line I never crossed.

Advanced 12-step work taught me a strange paradox: Sometimes you have to sit with the agonizing truth that you are doing something wrong. That you aren’t ready or able to stop. I’ve built 13 years of sobriety on the idea that we are only as sick as our secrets. But I should admit something up front: I’m good at telling stories, especially about myself. Even now, I catch myself deciding which parts to share. So here it is: For the better part of two years, I cheated on the woman I’m going to marry. She still doesn’t know.

In recovery, you learn that the most dangerous thing that can happen to a person with an addiction isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And every time you skate by, the lie gets a little easier to tell. The disease gets a little more room to grow. I first learned that at 23. I had to learn it again in my 30s.

I come from a long line of talented, tortured people. My father sold commercial real estate and spent years in and out of a quiet, high-functioning addiction to painkillers. He went away for treatment twice. My mother was raised by an alcoholic and carried a lot of anger. My parents divorced when I was young, both remarried badly, and neither of their houses was a safe place emotionally.

At 19, while in college, I fell into a relationship I can only describe as wickedly codependent. We went through two miscarriages together and became trauma bonded. I didn’t know how to grieve, so I was drinking heavily and taking almost anything I could find: stimulants, psychedelics, dissociatives. Just before I turned 21, I caught my girlfriend in a lie so profound it ripped the wool off my eyes: She had been sleeping with my best friend for nearly the entire time we’d been together. One of the miscarried babies was actually his. I ended things the day I found out and took the situation as the perfect excuse to keep killing myself slowly.

I didn’t make it a year out of college before the consequences caught up with me. The fall after graduation, I was driving home from a concert when I got pulled over for a dead taillight. I was relatively sober and polite and was sure I’d talked my way out of it—right up until the officer asked me to empty my pockets. Inside were a bag of powder that I’d been promised was Molly and a few pills I didn’t have a prescription for. They booked me and sent everything off to the state lab. For three months, nothing happened. Meanwhile, I painted myself further into the corner before the floor ultimately fell out. When the charge finally came, I began a year and a half of court dates and probation.

As a first offender, I pled into a court-mandated, recovery-focused diversion program with regular drug tests. I followed the program to a T, at least for a while.

One weekend, when house-sitting alone for a neighbor, I took a drink out of boredom and pure spite. No test caught it. Then I lied about it to my counselors, the court, and my family. A month later, guilt finally won out, and I admitted it to my sponsor. A few days later, he brought it back up and told me I had to come clean to everyone.

“The fact that you were willing to conceal that from everyone for this long,” he said, “is the only evidence you need of how sick you are.”

The problem wasn’t the drink. It was how easily I hid it. I surrendered to the program and handed my life over to a higher power. I haven’t had a drink since.

I went celibate by choice for three years. I did a lot of therapy and learned that I had swung from codependent to avoidant. I had finally regained control, and I didn’t want to hand myself to anyone. Then, in my late 20s, I decided to give dating another shot.

Before long, I leaned into ethical nonmonogamy and dated as “solo poly,” which means I was seeing multiple people without a primary partner. Casual dating became my primary hobby. Some connections were purely physical; some were friends with benefits; some went emotionally deep. At my peak, I was sleeping with six people, two or three times a week. I felt I was winning at something. I was always honest with every partner about who I was, sobriety included. But I wasn’t honest with myself about why I needed so much of it.

Then, later in therapy, I came to understand that the dopamine that keeps people in an addiction loop is driven less by the thing itself than by the anticipation of getting it. Dating apps are built on that anticipation. For me, there were three hits: the match, the first nudes, the first hookup. In the program, we say the disease of addiction wears as many faces as it needs to. The chase was one of those faces.

I matched with Maya as she was getting ready to leave on a six-week work trip to Thailand. We messaged far longer than usual, trading long voice notes across the 11-hour time difference—her from hotel lobbies at night, me on morning walks. By the time we finally met, I already knew how she laughed. Our first date was electric, the best of my life. We sat at the bar until they started stacking chairs, talking about family, grief, books that had saved us. She was self-sufficient and emotionally deep. She was great at conversation. She was beautiful.

A few months into our relationship, we curled up on her couch on a Sunday afternoon and talked about ethical nonmonogamy. We’d both dabbled in it, but neither of us had done it inside a primary relationship. I told her I wanted it. She was open to it. We agreed to start monogamous and build the foundation first, but we never said for how long.

The truth is, our sex wasn’t great. Early on, I suggested something new in bed, and she went quiet and turned toward the wall. Neither of us brought it up the next morning. I internalized the idea that I couldn’t ask for what I wanted, that this was just how it would be until we opened things up. Instead of communicating, I let the resentment build.

Then, one afternoon at a coffee shop across town, I ran into Bree, a woman I used to hook up with. She had been the perfect friend with benefits: incredible chemistry but no desire for a relationship. We stayed and talked for an hour. When she mentioned she lived ten minutes away, neither of us pretended not to know what that meant. By that evening, I was at her place. It was the first time in my life I had been unfaithful in any way, and I felt awful. When I went home, I waited for the consequences. They never came.

A big part of addiction is justification. We were planning to be nonmonogamous, I told myself, so the way I’d just conducted myself was basically equivalent. It wasn’t that bad. I can see now how thin that logic was. Nonmonogamy runs on transparency and consent. I was keeping a secret.

Over the next year and a half, my infidelities came and went. A few more times with Bree. A handful of times with anonymous women in other cities. Trans women, sex workers. I traveled for work, and I took advantage of the distance. I kept my phone face down, hid dating apps, used end-to-end-encrypted texting. For each time I followed through, there were five times I stopped myself. Each close call made me feel like I had it under control.

Strangely, the woman I cheated with most also kept me from making worse mistakes. Bree became my confidante, the only person I was fully honest with about any of it. One night, I arranged to meet a sex worker. I was sitting in my car outside the hotel when clarity hit. The only thing I could think to do was call Bree. She talked me down. She and I ended up having sex that night. The program has a phrase for that kind of thinking: I want what I want when I want it.

Meanwhile, the relationship I was betraying kept getting better. We slowly began to practice nonmonogamy, and it took us far longer than either of us expected. We listened to the audiobook version of Polysecure by Jessica Fern together. We’d pause so often to talk that a half-hour chapter took two hours. It gave me a way to ask for what I wanted without it feeling like an attack on her. One night, Maya paused the book and asked me, plainly, how she could do better. For the first time, I believed she meant it, and I told her. The sex got better. Then, much better.

The final close call came in June. Bored at my desk, I connected with someone on an anonymous hookup site, and we moved to texting. A few messages in, after I’d sent a couple of nudes, my phone lit up with a long blackmail threat. Attached was a screenshot of Maya’s Instagram. This kind of thing had happened four or five times before, back when I was single. Go ahead, I would say. I never considered paying, and no one ever followed through. But this time, I closed my laptop, walked to the bathroom, sat on the edge of the tub until my hands stopped shaking, and blocked the number. This time, I had something to lose. I was forced to face how stupid I was being.

I shared the story in therapy. My therapist, who usually lets me do most of the talking, leaned back, looked at his clipboard, and said, “What the fuck are you doing? This is like the 20th time something like this has happened.”

I didn’t have an answer. Nothing had ever happened to me. That was the whole point.

This past August, on a weekend trip to the Catskills with friends, I proposed to Maya. She said yes before I could finish the question. I meant every word I said that weekend.

Maya and I have now officially opened our relationship. We’ve mostly played as a couple. When she sees someone on her own, she tells me everything the next morning. Last month, I sexted with a woman for days and chose not to tell her. Though I have permission, I can’t seem to give up the secrecy.

Growing up in the houses I did, I hid the truth to keep the peace. Recovery taught me to say it anyway, but I’ve only ever practiced with people who can’t leave, like a sponsor or a therapist. Telling Maya is different. Sometimes, I reverse the question: What if she were the one carrying a secret like this? I like to think that I’d find a way to accept it, because through everything I’ve done, I have never loved her any less.

In the program, when you can’t make amends without causing further harm, you make what’s called living amends. It starts with stopping the behavior. Then you try to love better, act cleaner, and stay present day after day, without asking for absolution. That’s where I am now. I pray regularly that this pain is my consequence. If that’s my penance, I’ll carry it.

#6869. What to Wear to an Interview for Every Industry and Budget

Looking for a job can be a grueling marathon of resumes, cover letters, and HR acronyms—and that’s before you ever get into the room with your would-be future employer (or their AI proxy, as may be the case). Once you’ve secured an interview, the finish line may feel tantalizingly close, but the race is far from over. Before you get the chance to wow them with your articulate and highly considered insights into your personal strengths, weaknesses, career goals, and salary expectations, however, you’ve got to make a good first impression, and that starts with what you wear. So what, exactly, should one wear to a job interview in 2026? Aside from “probably not pajama pants,” the right answer depends on the job. Despite the loosening of office dress codes across the board in recent years and the rise of remote work in the post-pandemic era, every industry has its own answer to that question, from the traditional suit and tie to “whatever makes you feel the most creative.” For advice on getting the right look for the job, we consulted a bunch of folks who ought to know.

“The interview and the job are very different things,” explains Atlanta-based personal stylist Joshua Davis. “A tech founder might wear a T-shirt to a meeting with a VC, but even if all your research says the office is pretty casual, you should still go more conservative for the interview.” Davis suggests researching the company on LinkedIn to see how employees present themselves and even perusing their individual social media accounts for further insights into their style and presentation. “People expect that a lawyer will arrive in an E-Class and wear a black suit. A trial attorney might, but an attorney who specializes in tech IP will usually dress more like their client,” he adds. “The same is true for most bankers, consultants, and accountants who are client-facing – they have learned to research their client and make sure their image is usually only slightly more conservative than the person they are trying to sell to.”

Whether you’re applying at a disruptive startup or a firm with centuries of history, your clothes are an important means of determining how well you’ll fit into the corporate culture and represent the company to the world. “We get a fair number of calls from HR departments that have found a great candidate, but they don’t feel like they can fully recommend that person because of their wardrobe,” Davis says. “The most common reason is that a candidate is dressing too casually or they don’t dress for their body. To translate, that means too sloppy or too sexy.”

At the most formal end of the spectrum are law firms, and the longer a firm has been around the more traditional its dress code is likely to be. “Law firms in New York are still conservative workplaces and interviewees hoping to land a position as an attorney should still wear a suit with leather shoes,” says Jessica Chin Somers, Esq., a managing director at Kinney Recruiting, an NYC-based legal recruiter. “For men, the uniform of a blue suit with brown shoes or a gray suit with black shoes exists for a reason, but more important than color is the fit.” Aside from that, Chin says, there’s only one big rule to remember: “Lawyers do not show up to interviews wearing sneakers, no matter how “cool” you think the firm is or how expensive the shoes.”

Unless it’s a firm specializing in crypto, the finance field’s dress code remains similarly conservative. “First impressions are important, especially for a job interview in the finance industry, and the candidate needs to convey a sense of seriousness, competence, and trustworthiness,” explains J. Daniel Chi, Ph.D., the chair of the finance department at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas. “The dress code has relaxed quite a bit over recent years, however, as a job candidate, unless the firm gives clear instructions on dressing casually, dressing formally is a safer bet.”

The tech world’s reputation for preferring hoodies and sneakers over suits and brogues turns out to be well deserved. “What I see with a lot of different [tech] companies is a more casual approach to being at the office,” says Sam Fricker, the head of recruiting at Philo, a streaming service with offices in the Bay Area. “Because our company is bi-coastal, employees who are based on the East Coast sometimes are sometimes dressed more formally,” adds Maryland-based Philo Recruiter Alexis Fagin. “Departments may differ as well, like finance teams might require a little bit more of a buttoned-up look for meetings, for example.”

At Toast, a tech company serving the restaurant industry, things are similarly relaxed. “We encourage employees to wear whatever makes them feel most comfortable and confident to do their jobs, and the same goes for those interviewing,” says Toast's chief people officer Mike Gutner. “That said, our recruiters will suggest something in the realm of business casual. The bulk of our interviews are virtual at this point, so I’m sure I’ve talked to more than a couple of applicants in formal tops who are also wearing sweatpants and slippers below the camera. I won’t blame them; I do the same from time to time.”

At the other end of the spectrum are creative agencies, whose commitment to out-of-the-box thinking extends to personal presentation. “[Your] experience and portfolio far outweigh [your] presentation,” says Matt Johnson, the founder and chief strategy officer of LA-based creative agency Haymaker. “At the end of the day, we’re investing in the person and their abilities, not their appearance, so I’d say dress to represent your best and most true self. The more authentic you can be, the better.”

“NGOs value humility and authentic alignment with the cause, and your outfit should communicate authenticity, cultural sensitivity, and reliability,” says Sam DeMase, a career expert at ZipRecruiter in NYC. In other words, the safest play at your NGO interview is business casual, i.e. a blazer and button-up shirt with trousers or dark jeans, or a sweater, a collared shirt, and dress pants or chinos. Depending on the role and the organization, you should also steer clear of anything too flashy or obviously expensive-looking, the better to reinforce your focus on the mission.

Jobs in media—a range that covers everything from journalism to social media management to advertising planning—are extremely diverse, as are the dress codes at media organizations, but most media companies fall on the more casual end of the spectrum. “You want to wear something you feel genuinely comfortable in so you can focus on the conversation and be yourself,” advises Rich Kleiman, the CEO and co-founder of Boardroom, a sports, business, and entertainment network. “But comfort shouldn’t come at the expense of fit. Whatever you wear, make sure it fits well and feels intentional.” Despite this latitude, Kleiman cautions against confusing casual with careless, and advises investing in alterations to nail the fit. “Sometimes, investing a little in your presentation is worth it, especially if it helps you walk into the room feeling confident.”

Dress codes can vary widely between academic institutions and the departments within them, so the best course of action is to act like an academic and do your research, says Jocelyn Coalter, senior director of career and leadership development at St. John’s University in NYC. Beyond that, she advises candidates to err on the side of formality when in doubt. “It’s a lot easier to remove a blazer that you may not need than to put one on that you didn't bring,” she says. “I also recommend professional yet comfortable shoes for academic interviews, as there’s often a component that involves meeting different people on different parts of campus, or getting a tour.”
