#6866. Esquire First Look: How Bad Monkey Season 2 Brews Up Even More Trouble in Paradise

Creator and showrunner Bill Lawrence reveals how John Malkovich’s love of the Apple TV crime series starring Vince Vaughn landed him a role as the “comedic and dangerous” new villain.

For Bill Lawrence, producing some of TV’s biggest comedies is serious business. Once the architect of cable juggernauts Scrubs and Cougar Town, the 57-year-old producer is now at the helm of several streaming-era darlings, including Ted Lasso, Shrinking, Rooster, and Bad Monkey. But of all his shows, Lawrence tells Esquire that Bad Monkey scratches a specific itch.

“My favorite movies, books, and TV shows that grabbed me as a kid melted genres,” Lawrence says. A self-professed diehard for buddy-cop classics like 48 Hrs. and Beverly Hills Cop, the two-time Emmy winner says that the films’ signature mixture of humor with grittier themes and settings made them more than just funny movies. “People remember them as raucous comedies, and they were. But there were real emotional stakes,” says Lawrence. “And the first author that got me into that stuff was Carl Hiaasen.”

A political journalist and author from Florida (where Lawrence’s family originates), Carl Hiaasen wrote the satirical crime novel Bad Monkey in 2013. The book was adapted in full in 2024 as the Apple TV series’ first season. Lawrence was, and is, a “massive fan” of Hiaasen’s. “Then I got to know this guy,” he says. “That one of my heroes turned out to be like I hoped he would be, and that he let me take one of his best properties and turn it into a little bit of throwback TV—it’s one of the most fun rides of my career.”

Now Lawrence is ready to unpack the upcoming second season of Bad Monkey—and its Emmy-worthy newcomers—which returns to Apple TV on December 2. A new trailer, launching today, is embedded just below.

Bad Monkey season 2 sees Vince Vaughn returns as Yancy, a former Miami detective turned reluctant restaurant inspector who lives in the Florida Keys. With severed arms and medical fraud deep in his rearview mirror, Yancy finds himself caught in a new case involving a shady restaurant—an obvious front for a criminal enterprise—and the underworld boss behind it. Three-time Emmy Award winner and Oscar nominee John Malkovich joins as a new antagonist, Spencer. While Lawrence resists revealing too much about Spencer, he describes the role as a “comedic and dangerous” villain who drives Yancy to the brink.

“One of my favorite things about Carl’s books is that they’re really, at their core, character stories,” Lawrence says. “Spencer” is a role that Lawrence and his team “reverse-engineered” for Malkovich when they caught wind of the actor praising Bad Monkey while promoting his 2025 film Opus. “Randomly, Malkovich said, ‘My favorite show this year was Bad Monkey,’ ” Lawrence recalls. “This story started from a place of [thinking], If John Malkovich was kind and silly enough to bring him in this world, who would he be? You will meet him as our other fearless leader this year.”

The second season of Bad Monkey breaks from Hiaasen’s books (with the author’s blessing) to tell a new story that, in a spiritual sense, takes place between the original Bad Monkey novel and its 2016 sequel, Razor Girl. “We took massive liberties expanding certain characters, making the world bigger,” Lawrence says about collaborating with Hiaasen. “My nightmare would be to do anything he didn’t feel was true to Florida or his voice. I’m not overstepping when I tell you he helped come up with the main issue that Rosa’s chasing down [in season 2], because it was something he had written about when he was a journalist.”

Adds Lawrence, “It was nerve-racking. One of the scariest things, if you’re doing an IP [series] and it’s your favorite author, [is when you] have to make up things, but I’m very lucky he’s become a close friend and is candid with me to tell me if we hit or miss on it.”

Malkovich isn’t the only new face in season 2. A slew of stand-up comics, up-and-coming actors, and notable names fill out the ensemble. Sam Jaeger, Zavior Phillips, Peter Billingsley, Sofia Boutella, and Mo Amer all appear in the second season, to name a few. The Handmaid’s Tale’s Yvonne Strahovski also joins as a series regular in a role Lawrence hints has “something much bigger and deeper going on.” Returning characters from season 1 include Rosa (Natalie Martinez), Ro (John Ortiz), and Caitlin (Charlotte Lawrence, Bill’s real-life daughter).

But Bad Monkey is still all about Vince Vaughn’s Yancy, the endlessly witty, if also damaged, soul of the whole shebang. Yancy has changed a little since season 1—“He realized that he was a catalyst for change in other people,” Lawrence says—but he’s also the same dude who knows how to relax in his own backyard. “He was still drifting around and ended up very alone. Were we to have him be the same loner throughout season 2, it would be a mistake. He is the same Yancy. But watching that character decide what it means to let people into his life—whether it be Caitlin or Rosa—that is his personal journey. I wouldn’t want to watch the show if it weren’t Yancy being Yancy on some level.”

Yancy’s big story for season 2 involves his father, Jim, played again by Scott Glenn, whom Yancy invites to stay with him in his home. Lawrence says this story aims to highlight Yancy’s internal struggle as a budding father figure. “We all have dynamics with our fathers—me, Vince, [co-executive producer] Matt [Tarses]. You become your parent’s ... I don’t want to say caretaker, but the roles switch. That’s a role that comes your way. The person who always could take care of you, you need to step up and do that for them and embrace taking their role for everyone else.”

“If you ask me what [season 2] is actually about,” says Lawrence, “It’s about Yancy stepping in to be the patriarch of whatever his found family is, and whether or not he is ready to do that.”

If one thing sets Bad Monkey apart from Lawrence’s other works, it’s action. Lawrence aims to pay proper homage to the movies that influenced him with “feature-level” set pieces this year. “In season 1, we were flying by the seat of our pants, seeing what we could do, especially in Florida,” says Lawrence. “The one thing we wanted to upgrade in season 2 is the action. It’s stuff I haven’t shot in my normal 30-minute comedies before, and I think we pulled it off.”

The action will be felt through the characters—the ones standing, anyway. Bill Lawrence teases that the sheer amount of newcomers means one or two or several won’t make it to the end. “One of the things about Carl’s books is that when you reach the end, a lot of the regulars have died. Knowing the cast will never get too big, it’s fun to see the smoke clear and [find out] who’s left.”

This is serious business. But it’s still a blast.

#6864. Can Hermès Make a Watch as Iconic as the Birkin?

I arrived in Miami expecting to see the latest watches from Hermès. But as representatives of the historic Parisian luxury house led a group of us through the Rubell Museum, there was no sign of the timepieces. After passing through rooms showcasing Basquiat, Keith Haring, and Anselm Kiefer on the walls, we came into a room that looked more like an art classroom than a luxury-watch launch. Instead of being shown new editions of the H08, which Hermès has been slowly sculpting into an icon, I was handed a paintbrush.

Present in the room was Cuban American artist José Parlá, along with tables covered in paint, brushes, and scraps of paper torn from street advertisements. On the walls hung canvases Parlá had already begun and which would serve as the starting point for our own creations.

Delighted by the surprise arts-and-crafts, I happily donned my apron. Later that day, Hermès would unveil two new versions of its distinctive H08, the sporty model it debuted in 2021 and has been carefully building up. The latest pair introduced into the catalog both feature ceramic bezels, one in blue and another in white. They share a shimmering fiberglass case coated in aluminum that gives the watch the look of a tumultuous, gray sea.

When it was first released, the H08 garnered immediate praise from the collector community and broader market. Such rave reviews out of the gate are nearly unheard of for a completely new collection. The H08 is defined by its vaguely rectangular case with soft, sloping edges; the straight lines of the watch are contrasted by a round concentric dial. Paired with colorful, complementary rubber straps or the occasional bracelet, the H08 is a collection clearly intended for an active lifestyle without veering into the realm of the tool watch.

Hermès is a Goliath in the luxury world with a massive multibillion-dollar business across leather goods, handbags, and silk scarves. But amid all of its exceptional offerings, watches were never a primary focus; they were something one might add in while buying a Birkin. The H08 is an opportunity for Hermès, a maker of icons, to establish one in a category where it’s still establishing its reputation.

Inside the museum workroom, I set about the task of customizing my canvas. I chose one reminiscent of a rich, red sunset. I sorted through the sheets of torn paper that resembled craggy mountaintops, glued them down, and began to paint. I felt like I was transported back to my childhood art classes.

I realized that this was not simply an activity to fill the time but was actually a sophisticated metaphor for how Hermès views the creative process itself, especially as it applies to the development of pieces like the new H08s. The work is collaborative, iterative, and builds on the concept of what came before to create something new. The base is familiar enough to collectors at this point, but new colors and approaches round out a growing army of H08s so everyone can choose their flavor.

“We understood that our way to make this line grow would be to animate it and go deeper into materials,” Laurent Dordet, CEO of La Montre Hermès, the house’s Swiss watchmaking subsidiary. “It’s part of the playground of H08.” While the H08 already comes in a host of colors and accents, from bright teals and oranges to blues, greens, and yellows, the new bright blue and white variants bring a fresh, almost resortwear vibe to the collection.

H08 has given Hermès a foundation to build its limitless creativity on top of. The fundamental design hasn’t changed at all, but Hermès has continuously renewed it through new colors and materials. Hermès has played with satin brushing, DLC or colored PVD-coated titanium, and carbon composites coated with graphene powder. There are also skeletonized versions of the watch as well as ones with a chronograph (or a stopwatch function). As the H08 was always intended to be the brand’s far-reaching sport offering, opening up Hermès to new clients and lifestyle applications, the collection quickly became the vehicle for such innovation.

But even if Hermès isn’t primarily a watch brand, the house’s world-class artisans never passed off second-rate design. And the playfulness that pervades the house is essential to the watches too. “That’s what our clients expect from us. Hermès is obsessed with making a beautiful object that will bring comfort and pleasure to its owner,” Dordet says. “We are not obsessed with having something fun or playful every time. It's a balance between style, elegance, sobriety, subtlety, colors.”

Later that evening, at Hermès’s Miami Design District boutique, I encountered another expression of the company’s creative imagination: a smaller version of Jean-Simon Roch’s kinetic sculpture Mysterious Mechanics, which had appeared at the industry’s flagship trade show Watches and Wonders earlier in the year. Its elaborate system of ropes, pulleys, and moving frames was a theatrical interpretation of mechanical timekeeping, part of the broader cabinet of curiosities that informs Hermès’s creative universe.

As I stood before Mysterious Mechanics, I realized it would be all too easy for Hermès to tap into this same type of whimsy to create an H08 that was loud or multicolored in some obvious embodiment of abstract art. But that just isn’t how Hermès works. The brand understands that a signature watch is forged over generations. Look at a model like the Rolex Submariner—one of its most valuable assets is that the base design is largely unchanged over 70 years. There’s a beautiful juxtaposition between the very serious, capital-L luxury done at the highest levels of capability and concept, and the fun-loving, whimsical approach to how Hermès creates. Perhaps that’s why the brand has endured for nearly 200 years. And also why the H08 may just be its next iconic creation.

#6858. Why Are These Huge Arc’teryx Boots Suddenly Going Viral?

​Where were you during the Great Arc’teryx Jacket Phenomenon of 2022? If you’ve forgotten, the cult Canadian outdoors label went viral when young men on TikTok began filming themselves in the shower to demonstrate the hypnotic water-repelling ability of their Gore-Tex jackets. The attention only seemed to further cement Arc’teryx’s techy outerwear as an inescapable part of the menswear landscape. Now, the Vancouver brand appears to have another crossover hit on its hands: a hulking beast of a winter boot.

The Postour is a two-in-one design that combines a weatherproof outer boot with a removable insulated slipper tucked inside. In what the brand calls “full-boot mode,” the high collar cinches shut with a utility cord, sealing you off from the elements. The idea is that you can spend the day stomping through snow and slush, then shed the outer shell and lounge around in the slippers. Though it almost looks like a chalk bag sprouted a foot, the silhouette still carries the sleek, hyperfunctional minimalism the brand is known and loved for. The oddness of the design seems to be part of the appeal.

Naturally, it’s equipped with all the technical bells and whistles you’d expect from Arc’teryx: a gnarly Vibram Arctic Grip outsole, weatherproof Gore-Tex shell, and the label’s proprietary Coreloft insulation. Perhaps most covetably, there’s also a giant skeletal bird logo—known to diehards as the “deadbird”—on the side. The same logo has helped make the brand’s beanies a favorite of everyone from Timothée Chalamet to Frank Ocean.

Much like Arc’teryx’s Gore-Tex jackets before it, the Postour has become unmissable on the men’s side of the fashion algorithm. It’s a viral object of desire for a certain type of menswear guy fluent in microtrends. (This is hardly the first time we’ve seen a polarizing boot take over the algorithm, after all.) The is-this-ugly-or-cool discourse only adds fuel to the fire—and the fact that the boots resemble a sought-after design from Yeezy Season 8 certainly doesn’t hurt. TikTok and Instagram are chock full with videos of dudes excitedly unboxing their boots, blasting them with hoses, and customizing them with colored wax. As a result of all this digital buzz, the $300 boot has been drifting in and out of stock on the official Arc’teryx website. At one point last week, it was fully sold out; as of press time, only a couple of sizes remain in stock (though REI currently has a full size run).

Of course, winter footwear has its own pantheon of heavy hitters, from L.L.Bean’s iconic Bean Boot to Sorel’s felt-lined stompers and Timberland’s “40 Below” Super Boot. Whether the Postour will earn a place alongside those classics remains to be seen. For now, Arc’teryx has made the rare winter boot that’s as hyped up online as it is useful in a snowstorm. Good luck getting your hands on a pair.

#6852. Is 'The Only Living Pickpocket in New York' Immediately the Best Watch Movie Ever?

There are plenty of great movie watches: James Bond’s OG Rolex Submariner, Hans Gruber’s Cartier Tank, or even Hamilton’s made-for-Interstellar Murph. But there are very few great watch movies that dig into the myriad layers of meaning that compel real-world collectors. In that way, The Only Living Pickpocket in New York, which rolled out to theaters nationwide on October 30, is a timepiece-lover’s dream movie.

In the film, John Turturro gives an Oscar-buzzy performance as Harry Lehman, an aging New York hustler who has scraped by for decades without evolving with the world around him. He doesn’t have a phone, dances to vinyl records, pays at diners with cash, and rents Barbara Stanwych movies (“tapes,” he calls them) from the public library for his wife.

Naturally, Harry is drawn toward watches, both for his personal use as items to steal. Along the way of The Only Living Pickpocket in New York, there’s a cornucopia of watches and Easter eggs for collectors to appreciate: Director Noah Segan meticulously selected a watch to fit every character’s personality. It matters deeply to Segan whether a character is wearing a vintage Rolex Explorer 1016 or a modern Hublot; and the movie even gets deep into the inner workings of timepieces. A pawn shop owner, played by Steve Buscemi, deems a Patek Philippe “fugazi” and shows Harry the lack of jewels in the movement. Watches, as totems of an analog world imbued with personal and cultural history, aren’t just a detail, but a vital motif.

It’s clear that The Only Living Pickpocket in New York could only come from a writer-director embedded in the hobby themselves. And, in the future, Segan, along with the dealer Adam Golden who sourced the pieces for the film, plans to bring these essential watches to auction for charity. GQ talked with Segan about how he chose the film’s array of timepieces, the state of his own collection, and why he would make a terrible watch salesman.

Noah Segan: That’s a great question. I wouldn’t presume to jump on anybody else’s intent in terms of watches. I think obviously there are a lot of watches in movies that have become iconic. The first kind-of-nice watch I ever got myself was a TAG Heuer Monaco because I was such a huge fan of Steve McQueen and Le Mans. There are obviously a lot of watches that are worn. I don’t know how often they get woven into a story, but in our case, our story is not just about time, but time changing, time running out, and that in combination with very physical things, tactile things. We’ve got a pickpocket, he’s tactile. He touches you. The idea of a totem and an object representing those things started feeling very important to me, and watches do that for me.

I would probably be a very bad watch salesman simply because I would want to keep all my favorites. So I think maybe collector is where I should stay, but I’ve always been a very, for lack of a better term, sort of analog guy. I still shoot film with a camera. I spin records. I live in an old house. The classic car in the movie is my old car.

I was lucky enough to grow up in an environment that appreciates the marriage of engineering and art and history. To me, that Venn diagram is a solid circle when it comes to wristwatches. These are objects that can be beautiful, they can be sophisticated, they can represent personal historic endeavors and also the macrocosm of historic feats.

The idea of what a watch means when you think of it in terms of The Dirty Dozen [a WWII-era field watch for soldiers commissioned by Britain from 12 iconic Swiss watchmakers] being there for the military or [Paul] Newman’s Rolex Daytona and its engraving on the back that says, “Drive Carefully Me.” All of a sudden you’re talking about something bigger than us, a celebrity, probably one of the biggest celebrities of all time who did something absolutely crazy, which was not being a movie star, but being a race car driver. And yet his wife still said, “Drive Carefully Me.” So there’s the personal, there’s the promotional, there’s the macro and the micro, and they all are fascinating to me.

Well, a close friend of mine is Adam Golden, who has a company called Menta, is actually a watch dealer who deals in very specific, unique vintage watches of great provenance.

In considering the watch for Harry, I really wanted something that could bridge the gap between a very useful tool and something of value. And that watch represents a lot for people in that respect. There was a time when Harry was a younger man, and that would have been a really good, valuable, useful tool for him to have. It’s an understated watch. It’s easy to read. It can last through whatever you throw at it on the streets in New York.

And yet today it’s something of incredible value financially and also incredible value to him because it is his watch. It has been a tool for him. And I couldn’t think of a better example than that Rolex 1016 to represent that combination of factors. And so Adam found us that watch and loaned it to us to wear.

We built the other watches in the movie around [the 1016]. Giancarlo Esposito plays Detective Warren, [Harry’s] foil and mirror image. He both helps Harry and is keeping an eye on him. He wears a Tudor Tuxedo, which shares a lot of, as we say in the watch game, DNA with something like a Rolex Explorer. In fact, it was made by the same company, and yet it’s just a little different.

Steve [Buscemi’s character] wears a thin, gold Rolex Cellini on a gold bracelet that, again, is something that would have been definitely a luxury item back in the day and is an even bigger luxury item today, but still represents something of an older time. Meanwhile, the watch Dylan [a young mobster played by Will Price] has, the Hublot, is considered today to be a very flashy, modern watch. It has a modern chronograph movement. It’s made with very present-day materials. And in fact, he’s not wearing it. It’s stuffed into his gym bag, and he says, “I only wear that when I go out to flex,” because it doesn’t represent a daily tool to him.

All of these things were designed and lent to us by Adam Golden to talk about who these characters were and to reflect something about the characters in a world where the physical items and the physical representation matter.

I can walk you through all of it! We also had some interesting newer watches. We worked with an incredible team at Oris, a brand that’s been around forever, but remains really relevant. Victoria Morolis, who plays Eve, wears Oris. She’s a sort of younger hero in the movie and a punk computer hacker. She’s wearing the Oris Roberto Clemente, which is a watch that is very old-school in a lot of ways, but made with very 2026 technology.

She was wearing her very own gold Cartier tank. She said, “I am going to do this.” And we all said, “Of course you are, because you are the boss.”

Well, I have a personal feeling about that because I have a lot of knowledge of this stuff. This is my hobby, right? So to me, when I look at a 1016, I see history, I see Tenzing Norgay summiting Everest, whether that’s the actual truth or not. I am applying that to my perspective. And I’m also thinking about elegance in terms of what I like to wear. I’m thinking about how it sits on my wrist. I’m thinking about the simplicity in the design. And I can also see how someone else would appreciate the exact same thing about the Hublot: the work that went into skeletonizing that dial, the complication of a chronograph movement, and the mixture of metals and materials to make it. So I see both sides of it. I think because it is something so personal, it goes on your body. I think what ends up mattering is how you interpret that and whether you believe that one is of more value to you than the other stylistically.

Well, I think it sort of speaks to Harry’s world quite a bit. Harry comes from a world where if someone looked like they had money, they probably had money, right? And if they had nice shoes, they probably had a nice watch. And if they had a nice watch, they probably had money in their pocket. And you could do that math, and maybe it wouldn’t always be right, but these were educated guesses. And now we live in a world where a gold watch is not necessarily a gold watch. A Patek Philippe, presumably one of the most recognizable objects of value, is not necessarily an object of value. And to put that on Harry, to put that on a guy whose job it is to steal watches is, among other things, really a statement about him. And I guess in a wider sense that the world is not always what we think it is.

I think for the businessman at the beginning, he obviously has lots of nice suits and lots of nice shoes and he lives in a tall building on the Upper East Side and he’s got a camel hair coat and works in a fancy office. There are all these reasons to think that this guy is fancy and makes money. He doesn’t carry cash. You know what I mean? He wears Aldens, as we know, a fancy shoe, but he wears a fake watch. I think it speaks to where the world is right now, which is that it isn’t about honesty or dishonesty. There are shades of gray, and we exist in a bit of a gray world.

Great question. I think he knows that it’s fake. I think there is a world now where people—and I don’t think it’s just for watches, I think it’s for a lot of things—where people believe that getting most of the way toward something is enough. So somebody says to this guy, “Hey, you made partner. Isn’t it time to buy a $20,000 watch?” And then the guy next to him goes, “You can buy a $400 watch that looks just as good as a $20,000 watch.” And this guy goes, “Great.” And that’s it—that’s as far as they go.

“Too many” is the answer to how many watches I have. But most of what is in my collection that’s important to me are watches that are not [necessarily] of significant financial value. I have a watch that belonged to each of my grandfathers that I had restored. And I did not have fancy grandfathers. These are watches that cost more to restore than they were worth, but are of incredible value to me. And a couple of my friends who I’ve worked with many, many times, my friends and mentors in movies have given me watches, and those are important to me because of that, because of where they came from and what they mean and the consideration that went into them.

Watches like Benrus, which is, again, not a Rolex-level name, but Benjamin Lazarus founded that company in New York, and that means something to me as a guy from New York. So there’s something significant there. There are a lot of newer watches that I’m fascinated with, whether it’s companies like Oris or smaller brands like Haven or Bernhardt, which I appreciate.

I mean, listen, I don’t own an Omega Speedmaster. I’d love to own a Speedmaster. I love what they’re doing with modern watches now, actually. I’d love to own a gold Rolex. I think there’s something really cool about a gold Rolex.

I don’t know what I would do if I had a Rolex 6542, a Bakelite Bezel GMT [from the ’50s], because those are the coolest-looking watches. But if you sneeze at it wrong, does the bezel crack? I can’t worry about that.

Yeah, and I don’t believe in that! I want to wear my watches.

#6848. Shooting Matt Damon in the Desert and Leaning Into Discomfort

It was a beautiful day to be indoors. In barren Lancaster, California, the temperature had hit the mid-90s, and the feels-like figure in my weather app topped triple digits. We were professional. We were stoic. But at a certain point, you just give in to the heat of the desert. At a certain point, you wilt a little.

Matt Damon, mercifully, never did. Damon may be the least-debated pick in GQ Men of the Year history—he’s still riding the billion-dollar wave of The Odyssey, one of the great cinematic accomplishments this century. Damon was also, by some distance, the most unbothered man on that desert set, and all while being the only one wearing heavenly black tailoring (a peak-lapel Norton & Sons jacket, and glorious mohair Celine pants). It was Bourne-esque. Odyssean, even! Three decades into being a film star, Damon has familiarity with endurance, both creative and literal. In his cover story, he walks our Frazier Tharpe through the physical cost of making Christopher Nolan’s epic, up to and including a night in Scotland on which he didn’t sleep at all. The piece is a portrait of a movie star who still treats the job as a job, most of all when it is inconvenient, unfair, or uncomfortable.

Discomfort has been something of a theme for me this year. I will confess that I knew 2026 would be an administrative ordeal from the moment I accepted this new role at GQ. There was a move from London to New York (including a cat, whose opinion was not consulted). There was a home to find. And there was the discovery that the land of the free has impressive disdain for a grown man with no credit score. None of it was enjoyable. All of it, I think, is good for me.

I have the good fortune to work with many brilliant editors and writers. A recurring note I like to give is: Where is the tension? Without tension, there’s no story. I am not a confrontational person, but I find confrontation on the page irresistible. I want you to stumble, in every issue of GQ, onto something uncomfortable, or uncomfortably new. A magazine that agrees with you from cover to cover is a magazine that you have already read. And I would hate to do that to you.

This issue does not let you off lightly. After some life advice from an honest-to-God astronaut who went deeper into space than anyone that’s ever lived, you’ll read about Chris Rock’s unvarnished thoughts on divorce, discomfort, and reclamation, and Andrew Garfield’s very real anxieties in playing Sam Altman, the complicated man who’s been described as the Oppenheimer of our time. From there, we hang out with the men risking the unknown to get hot on peptides, which is, for better or worse, the defining male-vanity story of 2026.

A few months ago I started putting my email address in these letters (adam@gq.com). You’ve been generous. You’ve been thoughtful. A handful of you have been unhinged. But one note showed up way more than I thought it would: NO POLITICS, PLEASE.

I understand the impulse. I know These Modern Times are stressful, and wouldn’t it be nice if GQ were a place of apolitical tranquility? Alas. GQ has always taken men seriously as full human beings, and full human beings can handle tough conversations, opposing viewpoints, and a little gray area. There’s nothing noble about steering clear of the uncomfortable.

Which brings me to our special package on Cornell, anchored by a dispatch from campus by Savannah Sobrevilla. It is about an alleged sexual assault. It is also about the men on the sidelines of sexual assault. Some of the young men we meet through the story are students who are earnestly and inelegantly trying to grapple with how serious the alleged crimes are, with the crude and cruel culture that surrounds them, and with what role, exactly, they play in exacerbating that culture—or could play in diminishing it. It’s not easy reading, but not everything should be. ✦

#6842. Are Young Men the New Kings of the Swing Vote?

Two years ago, it seemed that the young men of Gen Z were shaping up to be hardcore conservatives. More than half of them voted for Donald Trump’s second presidency, refuting the idea that young people tend to lean progressive. Liberal media panicked about finding “a Joe Rogan of the left” who could reach them, and conservatives took a victory lap. “Gen Z Has Turned Right!” Fox News’s Greg Gutfeld announced, citing a report from the Harvard Kennedy School that highlighted, among other insights, the ways social media has led to an increase in conservatism among young folks. A Turning Point USA Poll from around the same time declared Generation Z “the most conservative generation in decades.”

On Tuesday night, though, as a blue tidal wave flipped states from Iowa to Texas, it was young men who comprised a critical segment of the new Democratic coalition. As reported by CIRCLE, a non-partisan research organization at Tufts University, white men between the ages of 18 to 29 voted blue by a 10-point margin; in 2024, exit polling indicated that 56% of all young men voted for Donald Trump. Even more remarkably, young people across the board voted for Democratic congressional candidates by a 40% margin, according to data from NBC.

“In the wake of 2024, there was this whole catastrophizing panic,” says Aidan Kohn-Murphy, the founder of Gen Z for Change (formerly known as TikTok for Biden) and a prominent political influencer. “Oh my God, everything we thought about Gen Z is wrong. It's actually a conservative generation. And no, that's not what was happening. I mean, a huge part of Gen Z just didn't show up to vote because they didn't feel like they had a reason.”

For Democratic strategist Eric Stern, connecting with men on an emotional level was critical in getting them out to vote. Stern has worked on Ruben Gallego and John Fetterman’s campaigns; his political ad agency, FIGHT, has produced spots for Mayor Zohran Mamdani, Senator-Elect Abdul El-Sayed, and Governor-Elect Gina Hinojosa, to name a few. This election cycle, Stern sought to change voters’ perception of what a Democrat can look like.

“Our most notable ad of the general election was Bob [Brooks] standing in front of a literal dumpster fire and then putting it out because he's a firefighter,” he said. Brooks, now the Representative-elect in the Lehigh Valley, flipped his seat on Tuesday. “A ton of Latino men are really into boxing and MMA fighting, and so Ruben [Gallego] held events around boxing,” Stern told me. For Texas’ Hinojosa, who'll become the state’s first Democratic governor in 35 years when she's sworn in in January, that meant running an ad during Mexican boxing champion Canelo Álvarez's match against Christian Mbilli, an event with a massive Latino viewing audience.

Still, Kohn-Murphy and Stern both argued that the main issue on the ballot this cycle was affordability. “If you look at polling, far and away the top issue for voters is cost of living,” Stern said. “And so you can run a million ads saying that ‘Jane Doe is for they/them,’ but it's not going to resonate with people in the same way if they can't afford to keep their lights on.”

When I asked Republican political consultant Kirsten Kukowski about young men voting blue, she brought up affordability too. After all, nearly nine months into the Iran War, a barrel of oil still costs $100. “Men and young men voted with their bank accounts,” she offered. “And for the last several years, that's how they've been voting. And I think that's how Donald Trump won that group two years ago, because he was talking about affordability and inflation.”

Kukowski, the former Communications Director for the Republican National Committee, now runs her own firm, K2 and Company, out of Minneapolis. “At some point here, people expect results,” she continued. “People expected results from Donald Trump, and they didn't see that. So now we're on the clock again for another two years.”

#6839. 13 Rugby Shirts Under $150 for Credible Old-Money Cosplay

Historically, rugby shirts are a somewhat conflicted character in the never-ending tale of menswear shirting. On one hand, they’re a prep staple on par with OCBDs, loafers, and boat shoes. On the other hand, they were quite literally designed to handle the rigors of full-contact rugby and were later embraced by folks who proudly call themselves dirtbags.

There’s an interesting duality to rugby shirts: they can feel twee or they can feel traditionally sporty. Thanks to this polarity, it makes it even easier to fit into your wardrobe. Regardless of which camp you fall in, rugby’s crush. And thanks to a recent resurgence, we thought it would be a good idea to highlight some rugby shirts under $150.

Not only are they traditionally as comfortable as any polo you own, they’re also refined enough to take into stuffier settings, rugged enough to handle some serious outdoor exploration and flexible enough to wear from Friday to Monday morning (but don’t do that, probably). And while we love an expensive version of, well, anything, we also love it when we can find some killer alternatives for prices we’d like to think of as reasonable. There wasn’t a paucity of options either, so we rounded up 13 of our favorites, right below.

One of the least expensive options on this list is also one of the best—the loose, shorter cut makes wearing it with jeans, some chinos or virtually anything else in your closet as easy as any polo you have in the arsenal.

J.Crew has a few louder options in the catalog, but the pared-down, solid-bodied iterations are even more affordable and play a supporting role with as much skill and charisma as your favorite character actor.

There is an actual science to color combinations, and Gramicci deserves an Einstein Award for this one.

When done right, a rugby can be one of the best outdoors shirts on the market, and this one is just that: super comfortable, a little hardy and delightfully reminiscent of the ones you would've seen in Camp 4.

There's simply no way that the company founded by an original Camp 4 denizen could ever make a rugby not worthy of a Half Dome assault, and it's also improbably still under $150.

Alex Mill makes a few killer monochromatic, garment-dyed variations of this striped number, but this one is more fun than those and we could all use a little bit of fun when the days are as short as they are now.

It's odd to call something a rugby if it doesn't have a contrasting placket, but this one gets a pass because everything else checks out and it's also made out of a hardy-ass jersey fabric.

One of the best rugby shirts we've found also happens to meet the rigorous price qualifications of this list—and now it's half-off—and it's not all that often that the best of anything is also a value play.

Putting "Iconic" in the name is one of those things that can backfire on you if it doesn't live up to the billing, but this one does and remains both a standard of the genre and, against all odds, under $150.

Very few brands appreciate the rugby—or the possibilities that exist under the rugby banner— more than 18 East, and not only is the Harold reimagining the notion of stripe spacing within the genre, but it's also the only short-sleeve option on this list.

This one is going to keep working its way into lists as long as its available because sometimes a brand just nails it. In this case, the cocktail of perfectly-faded brown and black is radiating Leatherheads (complimentary) and the cut is the comfy kind that can layer on either side.

Some big ol' stripes, a woven collar and a cool little oxford cloth hit at the inside of the neck are just a few details elevating this one from "not bad" to "oh hell yeah." (It's also on sale, which always helps.)

Hear me out: I've made a habit of finding stuff in the women's section that fits better than anything on the men's side, and this rugby has all the trademark elements of those past hitters. Also, it might be the softest one on this whole list.

#6836. One of My Least Favorite Politicians Lost an Election This Week. I Can’t Stop Laughing About It.

This guy was an ur-Trumper before Trump, and I’m so glad we’re rid of him.

This one is for me. Pardon me while I bust a gut.

Kris Kobach of Kansas—and never has a phrase with three K’s been more apt—is one of the founding weasels of the modern voter-suppression effort. If there is an odious policy position concocted since 2008, Kobach has run the laboratory that produced it. Imaginary voter fraud? Kobach led the farcical election-integrity commission during Trump I that fell apart after a contentious 2017 hearing in New Hampshire at which Kobach’s contentions were disemboweled by Maine secretary of state Matthew Dunlap. Draconian immigration policies? As Kansas secretary of state, Kobach tried to initiate a “proof of citizenship” law that lined him up on the wrong side of the League of Women Voters. Outright racism? Kobach told a radio program that the Obama administration was not far from a call for an end to all prosecutions of African Americans. If there was an ur-Trumper before Trump, it was Kris Kobach.

I thought we were rid of him when he ran for governor in 2018 and lost. I thought we were rid of him when he ran for the Senate in 2020 and lost. I thought we were rid of him when he got tangled up with Steve Bannon’s Build the Wall scam. But he was like the shingles virus in the body politic. He got elected Kansas attorney general in 2022.

And, on Tuesday, and extending into Thursday afternoon, it looks as though Kris Kobach might have lost another election. May it be his last.

#6834. Godzilla Minus Zero Is a Sequel Over 50 Years in the Making

The secret to the terrifying monster melee film? “I want the audience to feel that anxiety,” director Takashi Yamazaki tells Esquire.

However much Takashi Yamazaki loves Godzilla, he might love his rival more. In 2023, the filmmaker wowed the world with Godzilla Minus One, an emotionally stirring disaster epic that told a deeply human story about sacrifice, self-forgiveness, and the power of found families, against the backdrop of postwar Japan. At the 96th Academy Awards, Godzilla Minus One clinched the Oscar for Best Visual Effects, which not only righteously recognized its handsome design but lent credence to its rep as a prestige-level event film.

But many years ago, its director was just a seven-year-old boy eager to imagine his hometown wrecked by Ghidorah, one of Godzilla’s most prolific enemies. “In the theaters, fifty-five years ago, was the first time I saw Ghidorah,” Yamazaki tells Esquire via English interpreter. It was at a revival screening of the 1964 film Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster, when a school-aged Yamazaki saw a gold dragon with three heads stomp over Matsumoto City, where his family lived. But instead of glee, Yamazaki actually felt crushing ... disappointment.

“I was looking forward to seeing the utter destruction of my hometown by that kaiju,” Yamazaki says with a laugh. “They didn’t go as far as I wanted them to. So what I’ve done here is my childhood dream come true.”

On Friday, November 6, the King of the Monsters returns to the screen in Godzilla Minus Zero. Set in 1949, the sequel continues the story of Godzilla Minus One’s ex-kamikaze pilot Kōichi (Ryunosuke Kamiki). It’s been two years since Godzilla’s rise, and now he and his wife Noriko (Minami Hamabe) are raising their adopted daughter Akiko (Sae Nagatani). But when Ghidorah shows up, Kōichi regroups with his colleagues who’ve since formed the Large-Scale Disaster Response Bureau. While trying to stop Ghidorah’s terrible rampage, they cook up a desperate, if inevitable plan: Convince Godzilla to fight him.

Although Yamazaki spent the promotional cycle of Godzilla Minus One insisting that he had no plans for a sequel, the director had actually drummed up a story outline for Minus Zero. Yamazaki tells Esquire that he wanted to keep following the characters he introduced in his previous film to see how their traumatic circumstances could bring them closer together.

“In this series, Minus One and Minus Zero, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker says. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge. I wanted to see how they were going to deal with that. I love this family. I’m very attached to this family. I just wanted to see how they march forward.”

Below, Yamazaki reveals how surprised he was by the Western reception toward his first movie, why he included Ghidorah in the sequel, and whether a third movie is in the cards.

This interview has been edited for clarity, with Japanese-to-English translation performed by an interpreter.

ESQUIRE: Let’s rewind to the release of Godzilla Minus One in 2023. The movie was acclaimed outside Japan, particularly in the United States, while critics in Japan were less enthused. Did this response surprise you?

YAMAZAKI: The way Minus One was received in the United States was a surprise to me. We made Minus One without international audiences in mind. Step by step, it grew larger; first there was the L.A. premiere, which was received with a passionate reaction. That made me happy. Then the release came, and we started to see all of these comments and reaction clips on YouTube. That was surprising. I feel grateful because it was something I had not foreseen or imagined.

Why do you think there was such a stark difference towards Minus One from U.S. audiences versus Japan?

U.S. fans are so expressive. We tend to be more reserved. We don’t wear our excitement on our sleeves. I guess this is just how American people are versus Japanese. But to experience that kind of reception was wonderful. I remember watching American movies and imagining how great it would be to put a film out there and have that kind of reaction. That is what came true. So I’m very grateful.

When you first revealed Godzilla Minus Zero, it was with a teaser that showed Godzilla stomping into New York City. What compelled you to make this the very first look at the movie?

I wanted the first [look] of Minus Zero to be impactful. To have people wondering, “What the hell is going to happen?” Because the first film was so wonderfully and warmly received among American audiences, this was a way for me to communicate to the American audience, “Your country is part of the story.”

Between these two movies, you make the point that just because you aren’t related by blood or marriage doesn’t mean you can’t be a family. Where does this story come from? Do you have any similar relationships in your life?

This does not come from my personal experience, but it is something I touched on in a film I made about twenty years ago, Always: Sunset on Third Street. I feel that, if a parent and child are non-biological, sometimes their connection is stronger than biological parents and children. One of my inspirations is Shuhei Fujisawa. One of his novels is a story about a ronin who accepts a mother and child, albeit reluctantly at first, as his own family. I’ve been inspired and affected by that.

Godzilla Minus Zero continues the postwar period setting you introduced in the first film. What about Japan’s status in the immediate aftermath of World War II makes it fertile ground for the stories you want to tell with Godzilla?

With Godzilla Minus One, I attempted to tell the story of, What do people do when they are lost? When they are stripped of all means to fight? With this one, it’s a continuation of that story, about people who have to fight without anything to fight with when an even greater catastrophe than Minus One befalls them. I also wanted to tell the story of people not using weapons, but their brains. Their wits and wisdom. In this film, you see how they have to resort to means no human being should resort to. Trying to put an end to it all, but having to resort to taboo means.

Out of all of Godzilla’s other rivals, what excites you most about Ghidorah?

I was born in the Year of the Dragon, so it just so happens I love dragons. But it’s also the form factor of this kaiju. It straddles the line; if it were any weirder as a form, it would be really weird. But it’s cool visually. It’s a strange kaiju you can’t compare with anything. And it’s been Godzilla’s archnemesis for years, and the strongest nemesis of Godzilla. I don’t know how many more Godzilla films I will be invited to do, so I didn’t want to save Ghidorah for later.

Sometimes the spectacle of Godzilla fighting another monster undermines the dramatic weight he’s meant to carry. How did you strike the balance between maintaining Godzilla’s terrifying aura and the blockbuster thrills of watching him fight?

The way I approached this, in what we call a versus film, I wanted to portray how mankind relates to Godzilla and Ghidorah. Godzilla is a wild beast that humans have to deal with. Mankind harbors fear and awe in the face of Godzilla. But as I mentioned, they have to step into a taboo [solution], which mankind must never step into, to deal with this godlike beast. It is that fear and awe and conflict I wanted to depict. It was never my intention to have Godzilla as a friendly kaiju.

Godzilla has appeared in many different shades over the years. He’s been a monster, a folk hero, even a mascot. How do you, personally, see Godzilla? Is your depiction of him in your films reflective of how you think the monster should be?

I see Godzilla as a very angry god, or the wrath of God, because it is a metaphor for nuclear weapons and war. If we are going to tell a story set in the 1940s, after World War II, relying on Godzilla’s powers is tantamount to relying on nuclear weapons. Godzilla has that much of an immense power. There’s no way we can utilize his power without suffering repercussions. There is no happiness on the other side.

There have been a lot of Godzilla movies made in Hollywood lately. To your view, what are American filmmakers getting right or wrong?

I have no complaints about the different ways in which Godzilla is depicted. That’s actually one of the things I like about Godzilla. There’s room for avenues and perspectives. You also have Showa Era Godzilla that is quite funny. Then you have the Hollywood Godzilla, the monster battle films. They’re all wonderful. It’s great Godzilla can encompass all of these forms. I don’t intend at all to say, “This Godzilla is good” and “This Godzilla is not right.”

When Godzilla Minus One first released, there was so much talk about how it achieved so much with a limited budget. With Godzilla Minus Zero having more money than its predecessor, what were you able to accomplish on a technical level that wasn’t possible before?

We put all of that extra money into the visuals. I don’t want you looking at a movie, I want you in the story. There are a couple scenes where it’s longer takes, in which we show the kaiju coming and the people are scrambling, trying to escape but there’s nowhere to go. I want the audience to feel that anxiety. It is also the first Japanese film filmed for IMAX. So we were able to realize that immersive in-theater experience. It’s not a film, it’s an experience.

What are the chances of a black-and-white “Minus Color” version of Godzilla Minus Zero?

I don’t know. Personally, I want to make it. It’s of the times, isn’t it? It feels like a documentary. It’s something I want to do. I don’t know if I’ll do it yet.

You said during Minus One you didn’t plan on doing a sequel. So I’m asking now, after Minus Zero, do you have plans for a third movie?

It would be great if we could do a third film. But we have to have an offer before we embark on a new project. I’m also curious what other people might do with Godzilla in other films. Nothing is planned yet.

If there’s a third movie, do you imagine including other enemies from Godzilla’s history? I ask as a big Destroyah fan...

[Laughs]. I’ll refrain from any comments that might be misconstrued as huge news. But it would be interesting to have the option of a completely new kaiju appear. Already existing kaiju would be great, but new kaiju too.

You’re now working on your first English-language Hollywood movie, Grandgear. How are you adapting after a long career making movies in Japan?

It’s like I’m starting all over again. It makes me feel the way I felt when I was making my debut in Japan. I don’t really care about making films in Hollywood versus making films in Japan, as long as I can embark on a project that I truly want to make going forward. No matter the territory.

#6827. Inside the NYC Members' Club Where Republicans and Democrats Party Together

A half-dozen of us are squeezed into a small service elevator, our various colognes clashing with the signature fish-and-rubber scent of Lower Manhattan. The elevator operator blares Chinese ballads, and the young man next to me bobs his head in appreciation. An older man shifts uncomfortably. “Could you actually make it louder?” he snarks. Over the course of one excruciatingly slow ride to the sixth floor, we’re trapped in our private discomfort or pleasure. But we’re all headed to the same place: a midterms watch party at The Bench, a members’ club for people who are into politics but feel like political outsiders. It’s a place, according to its website, where “restless organizers, creatives, misfits, sneakerheads, and policy nerds collide.”

Since its founding two years ago, The Bench has hosted insiders ranging from CBS News editor-in-chief Bari Weiss to New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani. Monthly dues, “just to keep the lights on,” run as little as $50. The space is regularly used for events unaffiliated with The Bench: readings by literary magazines, climate week pop-ups, splashy debates. Michka Bengio, the social club’s owner and co-founder, is an earnest believer in dialogue and, if his programming is any indication, a little bit of a chaos agent. He likes to produce scenarios that underscore political tensions, like when he had Weiss’s The Free Press, co-host a watch party for Mamdani’s 2025 election. According to those who attended, it was a somber evening for the pro-Cuomo crowd, though some of them nevertheless returned to The Bench for this week’s midterms viewing.

On the night of the midterm elections, partygoers had the option of viewing the election results in a spacious, orderly room displaying CNN’s broadcast or in a lounge-y, narrower space screening Fox News’s coverage. In a cozy hallway between the two screens, guests could buy cocktails and popcorn or engage with people whose political views differ from their own—arguably The Bench’s most exclusive offering.

“There’s just a whole splatter of different opinions that come in here,” says David Azoulay, who works with the content agency Fixer Media. “The Free Press, the Manhattan Institute, and then I also get to meet a lot of people who are working in the Democratic space, on the Biden or Kamala Harris campaigns.” Azoulay helped produce events for an “America 250” tour in collaboration with Young America’s Foundation, a conservative youth organization, and has worked with provocateur Ben Shapiro and former Prime Minister Boris Johnson. Though he used to be a registered Democrat, he “grew up” and now identifies as a proud Republican. While Azoulay engaged in a playful back-and-forth with a progressive 21-year-old over what he considers to be the fallacy of socialism, Democratic councilmember Christopher Marte milled about wearing an “I Voted” sticker.

Over the sound of people screaming about the James Talarico-Ken Paxton race, Marte told me he met Bengio 10 years ago through Arena, a New York-based political organization that took off in the wake of the first Trump presidency. “It was all these young people trying to figure out how to get involved in politics. A.O.C. was on the couch, Alessandra Biaggi, all these people who were just curious, post-Trump election,” he says. At the time, Bengio was working as a campaign manager for Michael Basch, a techno-optimistic Democratic NYC mayoral candidate, a gig he secured by asking the only person he knew in politics to introduce them. He’s also worked as a consultant for NBA Africa and managed downtown restaurants like Regina’s Grocery and Miss Lily’s. The Bench is one of four Bengio ventures; he also co-founded the supper club Shtick with his wife, Jacqueline Lobel, and partners with the Hancock Foundation and the Hancock Cafe. On any given event night, he can be found buzzing around the room, excitedly facilitating introductions for his guests.

The son of a Moroccan father and Algerian mother, Bengio grew up in Strasbourg, France. When I ask what motivated him to move to New York in 2001, he deadpans: “Ever heard of the American dream?” More specifically, though, he was jealous of a friend who moved to the States for six months and returned speaking fluent English. “He was the coolest kid,” Bengio says. “And I was like, ‘I want to go to America, too.’ I love basketball,” he says, reorganizing a pile of books so that the edges aligned just so.

Bengio, a first-time voter, is coy about his own politics. Instead, he’s driven by the promise of America “that you can coexist,” he says, and a desire to nurture civil, democratic debate. “I want to be part of the project.”

“We need to really recommit and reaffirm what we mean when we talk about free speech—expression that is unencumbered by political interference in any way, shape, or form,” says Josef Palermo, a former curator at the Kennedy Center who drove up from the nation’s capital to attend this party. Earlier this year, Palermo wrote about the politicization of the Kennedy Center by both the Biden and Trump administrations in an article for The Atlantic. “We need spaces like this in D.C. Everybody’s in their own camps and certain venues are definitely coded one way or the other. I think having something like this would be huge in our nation’s capital.”

Then again, it’s partly New York’s distance from the D.C. swamp that makes talking about politics at The Bench feel cool. The space is decorated with Bengio’s enviable collection of NBA and political memorabilia. (He jokes that he stays up late scrolling vintage sites and bidding on Americana rarities.) Alongside Michael Jordan Wheaties boxes and a slew of rare basketball sneakers, there’s a framed copy of Emile Zola’s 1897 letter to the French youth during the Dreyfus affair, a portable Florida voting booth from the Bush vs. Gore election, a framed paper shift dress from Richard Nixon’s 1968 presidential campaign, and nearly every issue of John F. Kennedy Jr’s George magazine. The one with a Donald Trump cover gets taken down from the wall often, Bengio says, but he always puts it back up.

On Tuesday night, there appeared to be other collectors of rare political merch. Vintage Obama-era crop tops caught my eye, and a speechwriter for a New York City council member waxed poetic about his favorite vinyl record: a collection of John F. Kennedy speeches. Two guys wore t-shirts they designed themselves that cheekily quoted Michigan turncoat Haley Stevens: “Israel cums to me in my dreams.” (One of them requested anonymity, fearful of being cancelled by the Canary Mission, an anonymous organization that tracks critics of Israel.)

A good number of attendees who were professionally invested in the election outcomes couldn’t stop checking their phones. Anthony DiMieri, the man behind Mamdani’s winning social media strategy, told me he’d just come from Brad Lander’s party. Max Altman, the brother of the OpenAI founder Sam, also stopped by. He’s excited about a recent move to New York from Austin, Texas, and if he hadn’t forgotten to request an absentee ballot, he says, he would’ve voted for James Talarico.

As the results turned progressively blue, the room began to clear out. Before leaving, I chatted with Azoulay’s “libertarian friend” Hunt Beaty. In the hallway between broadcasts, Beaty tells me he’s not terribly moved by the midterms. “Everything interesting that is happening in the world right now, to me, is happening outside of politics,” he says. He rejects the libertarian designation and lives by the credo “free minds and free markets,” citing Reason magazine. And yet, Beaty finds that The Bench still has something to offer him.

“It’s fun to be in a room filled with buzzy, interesting people who at least are able to try and talk about ideas, while also being like, ‘Maybe we’re doing coke in the bathroom, too.’ I want there to be a New York City where those things could coexist.”

#6825. The Apple Watch Deals You Need to Look for This Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We’re expecting record-low deals in the coming weeks—but for now, we’ll take $250 off.

It’s no secret that Apple has been dominating tech headlines this year. But between the news of its recent price hikes on MacBooks and iPads and the release of the iPhone Duo (the tech giant’s first foldable), not much fanfare has been made about the new generations of the Apple Watch. As far as we’re concerned, that’s a damn shame, because they’re well worth the upgrade—especially if you can find one on sale like we just did.

Apple Watches usually go on deepest discount during Amazon sales, Black Friday included. And while only two models are marked down right now (the Ultra 4 and Ultra 3), we know a thing or two about how Apple deals work. In the coming weeks, prices will drop and these wearables will sell out like hotcakes, which is why you’ll want to plan what you’re going to buy now.

Below, we’re breaking down the differences between each model—and peeling back the curtain on our price-tracking prowess—so you’re all set come Black Friday. If you don’t take the plunge on one before then, that is.

Commerce editor Luke Guillory argued that compared to other fitness trackers on the market, Apple was lagging behind with its lack of readiness score functionality—until now. The Apple Watch Series 12 marks the debut of the brand’s very own readiness score thanks to a revamped tracking system. Plus, the watch now measures your heart rate more frequently to deliver Heart Rate Variability (HRV) readings every five minutes. If you’re trying to pay closer attention to your health this year, it’s a smart buy, made all the sweeter when it inevitably goes back on sale for Black Friday.

For October Prime Day, this brand-new watch surprisingly dropped to just $350, so we’re expecting a similar—if not deeper—discount in the coming weeks.

While the Apple Watch Ultra 4 isn’t necessarily reinventing the wheel, it did get the same advanced health tracking and readiness score features from the Apple Watch Series 12, so if you’re looking to get your first Ultra model, it’s worth grabbing—especially when it’s $100 off. Not to mention, Guillory is partial to the new ocean-inspired, semi-translucent rubber watch bands.

That said, if you’re not someone who needs the shiny new gadget immediately or who won’t take advantage of these souped-up health features—you’re not going to miss out on much if you score an older model on sale, like this $250 discount on the Apple Watch Ultra 3. Associate Commerce Editor Bryn Gelbart went so far as to dub it the “best Apple Watch ever” when it was released last fall.

“I was never tempted to upgrade to the premium option, but the Apple Watch Ultra 3 changed my mind,” Gelbart said. “It turns out I didn't quite know what I was missing.” It has a bright 49mm display, satellite messaging features, a durable titanium case, and a long lasting battery (an impressive 42 hours under normal use and 72 hours on Low Power Mode). Gelbart recommends it for cyclists, runners, or the adventure-prone.

At 28% off, this is the best pre-Black Friday deal to shop as of publication, although this model has dipped as low as $600 before.

If you’re looking for Apple’s most affordable watch, we suggest turning your attention to the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3. It was actually the watch that converted Gelbart to Apple wearables in the first place, arguing that its beauty is in its simplicity.

“It's an accurate and predictive workout tracker. It counts my steps and activity and connects to my phone to play music and receive messages and calls,” he says. “If you’re new to the Apple Watch and want to dip your toes, this is where I recommend you start.” It has the same sleep tracking features, like nightly sleep score and sleep apnea notifications, but lacks the ECG app or hypertension notifications of its newer siblings. It also has an 18-hour battery life so you’ll need to charge most nights.

This dropped to a record-low $199 during October Prime Day, so we’re hopeful that a similar price cut will emerge for Black Friday, but this is by far the most affordable Apple Watch on the market, even at full price.

Our philosophy when it comes to shopping for Apple tech during Black Friday? If you find the watch with the features you need at a price you can stomach, it’s worth grabbing quickly. During Prime Day, a handful of watches sold out completely—and you won’t want your wearable of choice to meet the same fate before you check out.

#6824. All the Wardrobe Essentials Every Guy Should Own in 2026

When it comes to getting dressed, we often rhapsodize about the virtues of “wardrobe essentials for men” as if everyone knows what we mean. So, let's actually talk about it.

On Maslow's hierarchy of menswear needs, wardrobe essentials might begin the perfect white tee, a killer pair of jeans, and the shoes you can wear with them. But in compiling this list, our goal wasn’t simply to meet your needs—you are, frankly, a grown-ass man, and hopefully have those covered—but more to remind you of the fundamental pieces of menswear that have helped men look hotter and feel better for generations. And in a world of non-stop trends, we need these reminders now more than ever.

In aggregate, our essentials constitute the foundation of a rock-solid men’s basic wardrobe. Below, a highly specific starter pack of everything you’ll want—nay, need—to wear in the year of our lord 2026.

A plain white T-shirt can be worn as an undershirt, standalone with some vintage jeans, or—if you want to get DIY—screen-printed into a work of art. Looking for an indie version no one else has? Check out our guide to the best white tees.

Slim, abbreviated trunks or boxer briefs that keep everything in place are the foundation of every rock-solid wardrobe—whether other people can see ‘em or not. Even if you’re in the minority that’s committed to boxer shorts, it pays to spend a little more on something that’s, you know, holding your balls all day long.

We love the zany knitwear options flooding the internet these days. But if we're honest, there will always be a spot in our closets for a classic crewneck sweater. They're timeless and endlessly style-able, even if you only manage to drape one across your shoulders.

There's a reason that the Levi's 501s is at the very top of our guide to the best men's jeans: it has been, is, and always will be the jeans you come back to again (and again, and again). If you dig your denim light and a little looser, though, Gap's Loose Jeans are a close second. Can't think of what to wear? Here's your answer, seven days a week.

If there’s another theme here, it’s that menswear essentials shouldn’t be unitaskers. They should be useful in a variety of situations, and when it comes to tailoring in particular, the navy blazer is a Swiss Army Knife. It works in almost any situation, from job interviews (with a tie) to weddings (someone else's or your own). Bonus points if you opt for a double-breasted number with strong shoulders, brash peak lapels, and an unmistakable sense of what Italians—and menswear guys—call sprezzatura.

The menswear-sphere admittedly overloaded on loafers, and we’re now seeing a slight shift in the shoe zeitgeist. But that doesn’t take away from the fact that the right pair of loafers can still be the most versatile kicks in your closet. They’ve got more range than Daniel Day-Lewis and can go from casual to dressy to punk in a flash. Wear 'em with socks or without, and count yourself lucky when they get truly worn in—then keep wearing them.

Sure, everyone knows a guy who refuses to wear shorts. But he's overpowered every time by his greatest foes: Those months of the year where, honestly, wearing anything but a short becomes a compromise. Our favorite pairs merge style with practicality, and only look better the more thrashed they get. (Except for the Champion shorts—maybe keep those a little cleaner.)

They may have emerged from the sweaty, stylish world of sportswear, but hoodies have long since transcended their athletic origins. These ones have the stamina to take you from a coffee run to casual Friday at the office (just put it under one of those jean jackets we're always telling you about).

We get that simple sneakers aren’t exactly the type of kicks you lust after on StockX, but there’s no denying the rush of dopamine you get when you break out a fresh pair, or how well they go with literally everything. At less than a Benjamin there's the Converse Chuck Taylor, a mainstay of our sneakers coverage, since, uh, always.

Not ready to take the plunge on a serious diver from Rolex or Omega? You're in luck: Seiko's much-heralded 5 series gives you everything you could want in a dive watch—an automatic movement, a day-date display, water resistance, and a near-indestructible case—at a price that won't require a second mortgage. (If you've got a few more dollars to play around with, Hamilton is no slouch, either.)

Like a plain white tee or simple white crew socks, a white Oxford shirt is a true workhorse. It can take the 9-to-5 like a champ, sharpen up for a fancier occasion, or kick back wide open over your favorite vintage tee. If you only have one shirt with buttons, let it be this.

Remember chinos? The wardrobe staple of preppies and, inexplicably, grandpas everywhere? The pant that taps in when your jeans need a break? The ones that Gap viral-ized in the late-90s? These days chinos are back and bigger than before. The chinos of today are surprisingly familiar and look more like the military-issued pairs of the midcentury—high rise and hella roomy. Some even have a pleat or two. We're all for going wide and pleated, but these both present a happy-ish medium.

While people usually start talking about them in April, sunglasses are a year-round necessity—overcast January skies be damned. They’re relevant whether you’re soaking up the summer rays, trying to cut the wintry glare, or simply trying to navigate life with a little more stealth.

Headwear trends come and go—we see you, crochet knit beanies and Trucker hats—but the baseball cap is eternal. You wear one, your dad wears one, your grandpa wears one. The solution for everything from a bad hair day, to an impromptu work Zoom, to forgetting to pack your sunglasses, they're also just plain cool, whether brand new or a little worn out. Always have been, always will.

Sweatpants should be cozy, sure, but the best of the genre are so much more than mere lazy-Sunday garb. We’ve moved past the calf-gripping elastane-pumped versions that have defined the closets of so many Lulu bros and dudes who have skate decks hung up as art. Instead, sweatpants now should be closer to the originals with a baggier fit and even wide-open hems. Spring for a pair that oozes Rocky-level charm, and they'll become the bed-to-bodega bottoms you can't stop wearing outside of the house, too.

There were a few years where the collective menswear consciousness leaned into “fun socks,” and further away from God. How easily we are led astray… Thankfully, we all now agree that the best socks are really the ones you forget that you're wearing, because they're so quietly consistent and comfortable. And in all of our years of reporting on savvy men's style choices, we've never heard a bad word said against any of the four contenders below.

Sometimes clothes can make you feel like you’re living a movie, and a good trench coat is like you’re aiming for an Oscar. (Those trucker jackets with the sewn-in hoodie are more like Hallmark). It’s the most dramatic garment you can have in your closet, and while wooden top coats are great when it's freezing out, a lighter-weight trench coat works the other nine months of the year—and can be paired with a heavy sweater for the other three. You may not be starring in any blockbusters coming up soon, but, boy, will you look like you're ready to.

In cooler temperatures, a reliable flannel shirt is more than a wardrobe essential: it's a no-brainer. Most versions will be plaid, but all of them should feel substantial enough to double as a light jacket, thanks to their lightweight wool or thick cotton make.

It's hard to think of a jacket more useful, more handsome, more versatile—heck, more all three at once—than the canvas jacket. These days, the original designs still retains their utilitarian charm, but the hammer and nails that used to accompany them have long since been replaced by a cell phone and ear buds. And thanks to fashion's ongoing workwear fixation, there's never been a wider range of luxe designer versions available.

Black derbies can pull through when you need shoes for more formal occasions—and also elevate a quick-and-dirty jeans-and-tee outfit. Dr. Martens are a great entry-level choice, and you'll get a lot more mileage out of the Mono pair, which forgoes the brand's signature yellow stitching for discreet black thread.

When your dress pants are this good—roomy and comfortable, statement-making but versatile—there's no sense in saving them for special occasions. Wear them all the time, with anything, the way you would your jeans.

Belts are often an afterthought, but they really shouldn't be. So don't waist (sorry) the opportunity. Much like cowboy boots, the western belt is riding high in fashion right now—but for this story's purposes, we'd like to guide you back to more classic styles, even if the two belts up top have a certain western flair.

By now, you're probably getting the idea that the name of the game here is versatility. While you could opt for Chelsea boots—the right pair will be able to withstand the elements, and any sideways glances from your office nemesis—you'll feel so much calmer in a pair truly designed to stomping through snowy slush.

We love that iconic North Face puffer as much as the next guy (you know the one we're talking about). But don't let its objectively perfect design distract you from the avalanche of stellar, lesser-known alternatives on the market.

A good duffel bag needs to do only one thing: hold stuff. A great duffel bag, though, needs to check off a few more boxes. It should still carry your clothes, kicks, chargers, toiletries, and everything else in the most space-efficient, easy-to-find way possible. But it should also be designed to outlast your passport—or better yet, you—despite years of being stuffed in an overhead bin and thrown in the trunk of your car. We've tested hundreds of 'em over the years, and the ones below offer all that and more.
