#6817. Kevin Costner Is Particular About Scents—So He Made His Own Colognes

Eight years ago, Kevin Costner was in Germany on a press tour. His hotel room was chock full of fruit baskets and other fancy accouterments that come with international fame. And also a handful of bath products. “I’m taking a shower and I’m thinking, ‘Man, this body wash smells good,’” he tells me from the couch of his modern white compound on a seaside street in Carpinteria, California. “And I’m thinking, ‘I don’t really use those words. Why would I even think that to myself?” Maybe two more times on that 15-day junket, he found himself thinking about the scent of that body wash.

“Can’t tell you why, don’t even know what it was,” he says. “I came back home to America, and probably over the next year, periodically, I actually thought about it. But then it faded in like a song you can’t remember.”

A few years later, he found himself in Scotland, scoring his Western epic Horizon. After a jetlagged detour to a countryside castle, he hopped in a hotel shower in Glasgow, and there it was: the smell. “I looked down and there were the bottles and I go, ‘That’s that smell from five or six years ago. That’s that smell,’" he recounts with his English lab, Bob, curled up at his side. “And I did something incredibly stupid. I went out of the shower and I got my phone.”

He got water everywhere in the process, but at that moment he was singularly focused. “I go back in the shower and I take a picture of these bottles as if they were going to disappear. I could have just really finished my shower, honestly. Dried off like a normal human being. But what I’m saying was it fired my senses again, imagination. And I sent those pictures back and I said, ‘See if you can get a case of this. I’m never going to forget it again.’”

It turned out to be 19-69’s Rainbow Bar, a scent inspired by both the ‘80s glam rock bands that frequented the Rainbow Bar on the Sunset Strip, along with general notes of California: there’s bourbon alongside basil and vetiver, creating a fragrance that’s simultaneously woody and green. That kickstarted Costner’s career as a perfumer. Now, he’s debuting a collection of fragrances with the same Milan-based niche perfume house. The collection consists of three fragrances (or extrait de parfum, to be precise), chicly advertised on 19-69’s website with a photo of Kevin Costner’s star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame. They retail at $80 per 7.5 milliliter bottle (a 30 ML bottle will run you $300, and the really giant 95 ML version costs a cool $800), and each fragrance—called After Hours, Heritage, and Pacific Coast Highway—reflects something of Costner’s life and history.

Kevin Costner first discovered 19-69 after using its body wash during a press tour in Germany, but it wasn't until years later he figured out the brand's name.

He tells me this story from his living room, amid coffee table books on design and the American frontier, and a homemade scrapbook dedicated to Bob’s first year of life (the dog peed a little bit on my foot, but I took it as a compliment). I visited on a foggy day in June, walking into his home to be greeted by a view of the Pacific. Costner, who has an endless string of massive, monocultural hits—Field of Dreams, The Bodyguard, the tv show Yellowstone—has the aura of a Paul Newman-type, his bright blue eyes undiminished by the trail of drool and dog hair that Bob left on his feather-light black sweater. The peculiarity that we are talking about his collaboration with 19-69, and not an upcoming film of his, is not lost on him. “I bring just a lot of feeling to things because I can’t always explain why I like something,” he says. “It’s like wine. I think, ‘oh, it smells like wood.’ But does it, really? The fuck it does.”

After his second encounter with 19-69 products in Scotland, Costner’s assistant eventually tracked the brand’s co-founder Johan Bergelin down, and 19-69 sent him a case of the product. In 2024, he called Bergelin, to congratulate him on a job well done. They met in person for the first time that August in Cologne, Germany, which was not on purpose but did make them laugh. Shortly after that first meeting, they went ahead with a partnership, spending two years developing a collection of fragrances, produced in France.

As a child growing up in a small town in the Skåne region of Sweden, Bergelin had, much like Costner, been seduced by a scent he encountered in the wild. His was a perfume at the local hair salon, a mass market scent from Karl Lagerfeld. An artist and designer, he eventually became a self-taught perfumer, and launched 19-69 in 2017 with his wife, Catharina, who handles communications and writes the colorful copy for each scent. The Milan-based brand—initially inspired by the culture and societal upheaval of 1969, the year of Bergelin’s birth—is known amongst self-described “fragheads” for its offerings tied to specific eras and places (Christopher Street, the Rainbow Bar in Hollywood, the island of Capri): their Miami Blue scent has a note of cocaine; the American Psycho perfume, a collaboration with Bret Easton Ellis (which just won an Allure “Best of Beauty” award), contains hints of palate-cleansing sorbet and harkens back to the products in Patrick Bateman’s prophetic 1980s beauty routine; Female Christ references a 1969 performance piece by Danish artist Lene Adler Petersen, in which she walked naked through the Copenhagen Stock Exchange carrying a cross. For the Costner collection, they’ve developed a group of three scents, titled After Hours, Pacific Coast Highway, and Heritage, all based on the actor’s life.

After Hours, the signature scent of the collection, has notes of cypress, bourbon, and nutmeg; Bergelin describes as a version of 19-69’s Rainbow Bar fragrance “on steroids,” and says that for him, it evokes the image of someone in a glass house in the Hollywood Hills at sunset, surveying the city. It reminds Costner of shooting one of his first major magazine covers, the May 1989 issue of Vanity Fair, with the louche, legendary Helmut Newton. “So, I was in a tuxedo but my tie was off and my jacket was on the couch,” he recounts. “It was supposed to be a sexy shot with a glass of whiskey. And Helmut kept saying, ‘take your shirt off, just leave the bow tie on or something’... I said no. I’m not an exotic dancer!” He carefully notes that Newton was “so sweet” and did not make this suggestion in any sort of predatory fashion. He was merely opposed to looking like a Chippendale.

Costner (left) first met 19-69 co-founder Johan Bergelin (right) in 2024 and together they spent two years developing a trio of fragrances inspired by the actor's life.

Costner once wore After Hours to one of his son’s volleyball tournaments. “There were about 15 teams of teenage boys,” he says. “So the gym smelled really bad.” He says a pregnant woman kept staring at him, with her friend tugging her arm, urging her not to approach. “She walks all across the gym towards me,” he says. “And I wasn’t sure what this was going to be about. And she just turns to me and says, ‘Look, I’m married, I’m not [trying to bother you]. But what are you wearing?’” She did not ask for an autograph.

“The scent doesn’t have to be obvious,” he says. “But I love that it somehow becomes noticeable on its own merit, period.”

Pacific Coast Highway is lighter, with notes of clementine, juniper berry, and jasmine, Bergelin’s favorite. It smells like going to the beach in California (on a day without an algae bloom or whatever) and then taking a long hot shower after. It is clean without being boring, and I love it. Costner says the PCH scent reminds him of Tommy James and the Shandell’s 1968 hit “Crystal Blue Persuasion,” a lysergic earworm inspired by the Books of Isaiah, Ezekiel, and Revelations that most listeners and critics instead took to be a song about acid, amphetamines, or meth. “It’s easy,” he says. “A light sensory moment.”

Heritage is a rich, masculine scent that nods to life on Costner’s sprawling ranch in Aspen, Colorado, with notes of cherry, violet leaves, amber, and vanilla, scents that sound sweet and girly on paper but add up to something whiskey-ish (“It’s on the richer side,” says Bergelin. “It has volume.”) Costner says it reminds him of “a place that’s very masculine, carpets and wood and guns and there’s a deepness to it like the wood of a place. It has a forest-y feeling to me.”

Costner, an avid hunter, is eager to talk about guns as we sniff Heritage. He both believes in laws that limit them and sees them as heirlooms, like jewelry. “If you misuse a gun, you should never have one again,” he says. “But I have my father’s gun, and he got it when he was young. And so, when I put that gun to my cheek, I can feel him. When I was a boy, I would just ask him to let me hold it and it was much too heavy for me. And now it feels almost light as a feather. And my sons will one day put the gun to their cheek when I won’t be here, and they’ll remember those times in the field with that dog and everything else.”

19-69’s tagline is “bottling counterculture.” But Costner, born in 1955, had a different experience of the hippie years. He was raised in a conservative household without much money in Southern California; his late brother left to serve in the Marines in Vietnam while other kids his age protested the war. He rigorously studied classical piano with an elderly instructor who had been around long enough to teach FDR. “You can imagine where the politics were with my mother and father, who wanted to support their son in Vietnam,” he says. “At the dinner table, long hair was bad. The music was bad. They didn’t like anything about it. And I was sitting there eating at that same table thinking I liked a lot of it.”

He was too young to go full Woodstock. But he remembers what it was like to be around the culture. “A whole generation here went north to the Haight-Ashbury, to San Francisco, the Summer of Love,” he remembers. “I would watch the last exit out of Santa Barbara when there was no freeway. It was just a highway with lights, and the last one was like a last man out of Saigon moment. There would sometimes be as many as 60, 70 kids at that light with their thumbs out.”

Costner’s best-known roles are visceral and sexy, and in watching them, it’s easy to conjure up scents: saddle leather, the grass of the Great Plains and baseball parks, sweat, campfires, gunpowder. Millions of people have formed their own relationships with him by going to the movies, surrounded by the scents of melted butter and spilled soda. One 2016 study examined the olfactory experience of going to the cinema, in which chemists asserted that audiences emitted different compounds, like carbon dioxide and isoprene, depending on the genre of film they were watching, unwittingly creating new scents for emotional or romantic scenes. If that’s true, which seems awfully nice to believe, Costner has, in one small way, been in the fragrance business for a long time.

He says his favorite smells are lemon, cinnamon, and sage (“I knew [sage] as a hunter, not as an in-crowd thing”), and his house smells like cleanliness, or nothing. I asked if he likes the smell of movie theaters. “Not particularly,” he says. “I think what I miss in a theater is the curtain. But I know that smell of popcorn. It’s almost ceremonial, in a way, and it’s always lousy. It’s better when you cook it at home, it just is.”

#6812. Crying in the Club (and Everywhere Else) With Jordan Firstman

“This is what you’re keeping me from right now,” Jordan Firstman says, and pivots his laptop screen to face a high hotel window overlooking a long, tempting stretch of Brazilian beach.

Firstman is in Rio de Janeiro for work, presenting three film-festival screenings of Club Kid, his directorial debut, which he also wrote and stars in as Peter, a dissolute downtown New York club promoter whose own extended adolescence ends abruptly when he’s called upon to parent Arlo, the 10-year-old son he’s never met.

The Brazilian audiences “really got it,” Firstman reports. It seems like everybody who watches Club Kid really gets it. The film received a seven-minute ovation at the Cannes Film Festival back in May, and sold to A24 for a reported $17 million after a brief, buzz-stoking bidding war. Preview screenings in LA, where Firstman lives, have been packed to the rafters.

Shirt and pants by Brioni. Bow tie and cummerbund, stylist’s own. Vintage watch by Rolex from Foundwell. Vintage cufflinks by Cartier from Foundwell. Earring, his own.

But when it’s suggested that this all bodes well for Club Kid’s broader appeal when it finally hits theaters, Firstman says, “Only time will tell. I hope it translates to the gen-pop.”

What we do know: Club Kid is hands down the most heartwarming film ever made about a man raising a child conceived during a blotto bisexual threesome—a supremely confident comedy-drama, raw and honest about gay sex, party drugs, and parenting in a way that’s excitingly and delightfully at odds with the sweetness of its tone. It’s an extremely likeable film, and its director-star is extremely likeable in it. And, I’ll say—at the risk of being rude—this means it’s kind of a departure for Firstman, who up until now has cultivated an almost proudly polarizing comedic persona online and onscreen. “I think, when you’re annoying and gay, people can’t get past that, in our culture,” he says, “and it’s interesting to me.”

Firstman is 35, and has not been famous particularly long, but if his public life so far has taught him anything, it’s how quickly the wind can shift. He broke through doing silly front-facing video sketches during COVID—playing banana bread’s publicist, yass-ing up his new favorite client, that sort of thing—and before long found himself apologizing for unearthed edgelord tweets he’d written in his early 20s. (He said in a statement that he’d “grown a million lifetimes” since those posts.)

His run on Fox’s English Teacher ended with the show’s cancellation in 2025; around the same time, he popped off to Vulture about the sex scenes in Heated Rivalry, suggesting the hockey bromance didn’t have a clue about “how gay people fuck.” (He is perhaps the only person ever to have pissed off that show’s fan base and lived to tell the tale, which may be evidence that nothing can stop his rise.)

Now ensconced once again as a TV-series regular—on HBO’s I Love LA—and with a buzzy movie in the pipeline, he’s able to look at all this with sunrise/sunset serenity.

“There’s ebbs and flows to whether people fuck with you or not,” he says. “Everyone loved me during the pandemic, and then everyone hated me for a little bit, and now it feels like people like me again.”

Still, he remains, by his own admission, “too online,” unable to not read things written about him, and it’s hard not to internalize some of that stuff, a little bit. He was gay and annoying in 2023’s Rotting in the Sun, playing “Jordan Firstman,” a dick-swinging, clout-chasing imp who torments a depressive artist played by the film’s director, Sebastián Silva. No less an authority than Pedro Almodóvar saw the movie and told Firstman he was a “real actor,” but his was one of the kinder reactions.

“I kind of assumed audiences would understand it was a performance,” Firstman says, “and I think many didn’t.” He thinks the movie is brilliant and calls Silva a genius. But he’s also convinced that playing a maximally annoying caricature of himself “really did affect the way people saw me, and then it affected how I saw myself. The year after Rotting was actually very tough for me, because I felt very hated.”

The new film, he says, is partly a response to that experience, he says. “I think Club Kid came from a place of, ‘I know how I feel, and I know how I’m being perceived is so different than how I feel, and I want to show a more true side of myself through this work.’”

That wasn’t how it started, of course. It started with Firstman at a dinner party, doing bits with his friends’ kid. That was the spark—Firstman realizing that putting a Jordan Firstman–type character, talking the way he does, in situations with an impressionable minor who maybe shouldn’t hear the kinds of things Firstman tends to say out loud, could be a great comedic setup.

Club Kid is in conversation with a long line of films about characters thrust into a parental role they’re ill-equipped for; Firstman can rattle them off, from Chaplin’s The Kid to Auntie Mame to Kramer vs. Kramer to Big Daddy. But as he wrote, bigger ideas emerged—about the “arrested development” of a certain cohort of childless gay men, about what it actually means to be an adult. And in working out how Peter would show up to his newfound relationship with Arlo, he thought about his own parents—two Newsday reporters who raised him in the New York suburbs—and what he got from them, as well as what he wished he’d gotten.

“I feel like no one taught me what life was going to be like. We just got by in a pleasant-ish way. But I don’t think I got major insights,” he says. A beat. “It is what it is. I am grateful that I went looking for those insights in my early 20s, through drug trips.”

This is what it’s like to talk to Firstman these days; he’s reflective, but he’ll still spring a provocative overshare on you like a trap door. (Boing! Drug trips!) The first time we met, at a photo shoot a few weeks before the call from Brazil, he told me that although the standing ovation thing at Cannes is overblown (“It felt great, but it’s such a ruse. It’s like, you can keep it going if you want. You just don’t let it stop. [From the stage] You keep pointing at different people in the movie”), the experience of showing the movie to an appreciative audience at the most prestigious film festival in the world had been transformative, that it felt like “20 years of validation that I didn’t get,” and that he no longer feels the same need to “say the edgy thing” all the time.

But at the very beginning of that conversation—as I remember it, we were still in the process of shaking hands—he started telling me an off-the-record story involving, let’s say, an adult situation. Today, over Zoom, I ask if that anecdote (explicit, slightly alarming) was the old Jordan briefly reasserting himself by saying the edgy thing.

“It probably came from a place of, maybe, nervousness, if I’m being honest,” he says. “But I think, probably, there was some sort of provocation that was a bit of a shield in that story.”

And it’s more complicated than that, he notes—because the guy who says the edgy thing is a part of him, too, and who’s to say it’s not his best self? He really does feel, after Cannes, like he doesn’t have to prove himself in quite the same way, he says.

“But then I’m like, ‘Oh, am I changing into something that’s less honest, or more honest? Are the gritty parts of me honest, and I’m letting go of them to be more palatable?’” Or is it a defense mechanism, “and the real me is something softer and sweeter and more put-together?”

The goal, of course, is to amalgamate all our disparate selves. Wouldn’t that be great? Firstman has tried his best.

“I’ve done a lot,” he says. “Everything mystical, I’ve tried.”

He’s tried past-life regression, and he’s tried ayahuasca, and he’s the second person I’ve interviewed—the first being Mike Tyson—who has tried bufotenin, a hallucinogen found in the venom of the Sonoran desert toad. (He doesn’t recall much about the first time he tried it, except a feeling “like something really bad happened”; the second time was “a way more positive, beautiful experience.”)

“I think I’ve always been a very curious person,” he says. “They say with ayahuasca, it finds you when you’re supposed to do it.”

When he heard about ayahuasca—a traditional psychoactive tea brewed from two plants native to the Amazon—on comedian Duncan Trussell’s podcast, he says, “It felt like I got shot in the stomach. I was like, ‘I have to do this.’” The next day he was at a party and somebody told him they’d just done it, under the supervision of a shaman in Topanga. Firstman went on to do it nine times over the course of his 20s.

“By the end, it kicked me out,” he says. “God, literally, was like, ‘You can’t come back here. This realm is not for you, ever again.’ I still did it one more time after that, and it was like, Bitch, are you serious? It almost really fucked me up.”

So he was pushing it, he reasons, with the toad venom. “I maybe shouldn’t have even done that, but I think it’s a different thing.”

He has this secret spiritual tool kit now, of stuff he’s learned from these experiences, about his own perceptions, and he thinks it’s protected him. He wasn’t looking for a way out of anything or relief from any particular suffering or struggle. He wanted to learn about life and what there was to experience—which, as it happens, is also how he’s approached his working life.

“I do it through film, I do it through music. I do it through social interactions. I think life is about learning in any way you can and experiencing everything you can.”

Instagram videos. Sitcoms. Directing a movie. Each another trip: What happens when I do this? He figures he could probably have built a more stable career, “if I had a more diligent respect for one medium, maybe.” But he can’t help where his creative impulses take him. Was anybody clamoring for Firstman to make 2025’s Secrets, a full-length comedy album of oddly well-executed genre-parody songs with titles like “I’m Hoarding Cum” and “Pennies in My Pussy”?

The answer is no. (The actual answer is probably “not at all.”) But once he started making the record, Firstman says, “there was no stopping me. I was in the studio, like, ‘What am I doing in a music studio?’ But I couldn’t not. I was there every day. I was begging any producer I knew to help me, because I just needed to.”

Chances are you could not make a movie like Club Kid, whose realness stems in part from a clearly lived-in understanding of partying, without having done some wild shit. But it would be difficult to make this movie if you were still living that way. Peter has Arlo to drop-kick him out of suspended adolescence and into adulthood; I ask Firstman what the catalyst was for his own growth.

“I think it was a slow process,” he says, “that’s still happening, to some degree.”

Some of what happened was professional: In 2025, he put out the album and shot the second season of English Teacher, the first season of I Love LA, and Club Kid. The practical demands of that schedule forced him to lock in: “I didn’t have Saturdays off to party, and I couldn’t afford, on a Monday, to not feel like my best.”

He also went through a breakup “that was really, really, really sad and just really unexpected, and someone I never thought I would break up with.”

He doesn’t want to be too specific about, y’know, the specifics. But it was a split he initiated.

“It came to a point where I was like, ‘I know the life I want to live and I don’t think this love is aligning with that life.’ I had to choose my life over love—which is such a mind-fuck, because you’re like, ‘Love is everything. There’s nothing more important than love.’ But then, your life is also—on a day-to-day level, it’s pretty important. And so yeah, I think it was a really tough, bizarre out-of-body experience almost, because it was a love I had never known the likes of before, and then it was over.”

By the time they broke up they had been together for four years. When it started, Firstman says, he couldn’t believe they’d found each other and the depth of what he was feeling for this person. “And I felt the exact same way with the breakup,” he says. “I was like, ‘I cannot believe this is happening.’ It feels so unreal. It just doesn’t make any sense. As much as the love didn’t make sense in the beginning, how powerful it felt, the breakup didn’t. It was the exact same feeling.

After their last conversation they agreed to stay friends, and then they didn’t talk for a year. “I think he was protecting himself and maybe protecting me too,” Firstman says. When his ex finally responded to a message, Firstman was on set, about to shoot a climactic scene in Club Kid—he’d scripted, as it happens, a big emotional moment for Peter, a moment where two characters connect on the phone after a long time apart.

“The first time, in a year, he had responded, was the moment before they called ‘Action,’” Firstman says. “That’s why I cried so hard in that scene, just like a huge release.”

His ex hasn’t seen the movie yet, Firstman says, and maybe he never will. It’s dedicated to him, though—his name is in the end credits. “He doesn’t know that either,” he says. “I’m a little scared. I’m a little scared, because to me, the movie is a love letter to him.”

He starts crying here, a little bit, apologizing even as it’s happening.

“All of that extra love that I still have for him, I put it directly into the movie. If he does see it, which I don’t expect him to or need him to, I hope he sees that’s where all my love went, and that I still have it for him and I always will.”

He has not started writing his next movie yet, but he’s started thinking about it. He knows that like this one, it’ll be a love story—and that after writing, in Club Kid, about familial love, community love, and loving yourself, he wants to write about friendship next.

In the past year, he’s been blown away by two movies of vast scope, Paul Thomas Anderson’s One Battle After Another (“I was making my movie when that came out, and I think that is a movie, for him, about growing up, and not feeling like a grown-up as an adult”) and the equally sprawling queer epic La Bola Negra. I ask him if he’s looking to go similarly big with his next project. Maybe, he says.

“But I think I’d like to take incremental steps and learn more as a filmmaker before.…”

“Yeah,” he says, laughing. “My war movie. The Firstman war movie. I have zero context for war, so I would be completely making everything up.” ✦

#6808. 22 Killer Watches I Saw at One of New York’s Most Exclusive Collector Hangs

This is an edition of the newsletter Box + Papers, Cam Wolf’s weekly deep dive into the world of watches. Sign up here.

I knew the watch collector meetup I was going to was legit when its organizer sent this text to the group: “We’ve engaged an off-duty NYPD detective to work security for our dinner. So, now really no reason not to bring your best!”

Last Wednesday night, at a fancy steakhouse in Midtown Manhattan, I was reminded why I love these enthusiast-organized events. This one was relatively small in comparison to blowouts like Rolliefest, but the idea was the same: A dozen or so collectors brought along their most prized timepieces and plunked them down on the table unattended (besides the off-duty NYPD detective, of course). When I asked one attendee if I could take his Breguet—similar to one heading to auction this weekend with a $73,000 estimate—across the room to take a photograph, he reacted like I’d asked if I could breathe oxygen: Of course! What a silly question. Events like these genuinely reflect the best of the hobby: deeply passionate collectors who really just want to share their watches with like-minded obsessives.

Between bites of miniature beef wellingtons and medium-rare steak, the guests passed around and ogled the dozens of grails on display. There were new classics on the table—including Vacheron Constantin’s Ultra-Thin, a leading contender for watch of the year—alongside plenty of old ones, including every flavor of vintage Rolex, from GMT-Masters to Daytonas to Explorer IIs. I was standing with one collector as he examined a pretty vintage Rolex GMT-Master. He stopped and looked around. So-and-so must be here, he muttered aloud. How did he know? Because he recognized the shade of the patina on the dial.

This particular group started getting together a couple of years ago, after the collectors @HorologicallyMinded, @s_h_lemon, @null_islands, and @jmsfisk (everyone at the event asked to remain anonymous) met at another watch-related retreat. @HorologicallyMinded, or HM, says that collecting was at one point something he did in solitude. “People generally judge when they learn how much watches cost,” HM tells me. “You paid how much for that!?” But he and the other three founders began meeting up for monthly dinners before eventually taking turns to organize these larger events. “This group has been the most rewarding part of this hobby,” HM says. “It’s been so great to have a group of people that have a common interest, but it’s not work-related and no one judges.”

Although the group has grown, the core four are still instrumental in making sure the functions go off without a hitch, with just the right spread of watches on the tables. “It is very deliberate and the four of us generally coordinate on it,” HM says. “We specifically make sure there is something for everyone.” That means ensuring there are at least a handful of watches worth less than $10,000—this time, that included several Rolex Datejusts, a Vulcain Cricket, and a gold Breitling from the 1940s. “We knew we were having some relative newbies and different people have different budgets, and we want everyone to be comfortable.” On the other end of the spectrum, some grail-level pieces were special requests from other members of the group.

All the watches wound up getting plenty of TLC. “I love the fact that we’re a group that can, and did, totally geek out about a vintage Longines Legend Diver, while a Doré dial Patek is on the same table,” HM says.

Below are some of the very best pieces I saw that night.

A Patek Philippe ref. 3970, part of the house’s historic lineage of pieces that combine a perpetual calendar with a chronograph. The dial on this one is so tasty.

A skeletonized Breguet Ref. 3355PT00PA0—maybe the watch of the night for me—on a show-stopping platinum bracelet.

A few years ago, MB&F started making more approachable versions of its sci-fi-ready watches and allocating them through a lottery system. This is the M.A.D2, with an outer ring on the dial that spins around very quickly. It’s really fun.

The aforementioned Rolex GMT-Master is a ref. 16760, a model produced during the ’80s that’s nicknamed the “Sophia Loren” thanks to its curvaceous profile.

Omega has incredible stuff in its archives. This is a chronograph from the ’30s that’s controlled entirely through that single button at 2 o’clock.

A couple of real bangers here. The Vacheron on the left is known as the “Cioccolatone,” because it resembles a square of chocolate. At the center is the Patek Philippe ref. 3417, an antimagnetic watch with just about the prettiest typography you’ll ever see on a watch. Zohran Mamdani should adopt that cursive for signs in the city, because it can make just about anything look nice: Warning: Rats. Or maybe, No Dumping Here.

A cloisonné dial from—of all brands—IWC! I am such a sucker for a cloisonné, which refers to the intricate images carefully fashioned from wire and colorful baked enamel.

A beautiful example of one of the Seafarers that Heuer made for Abercrombie & Fitch from the ’50s through the ’70s, back when the latter was still a rugged outdoor outfitter. The shades of orange, yellow, and blue are dreamy.

A monochromatic take on the Audemars Piguet Royal Oak Perpetual Calendar, which was produced in the ’80s and ’90s but looks incredibly modern.

Universal Genève finally relaunched last year after decades on the shelf, but it’s hard to beat the originals. This Polerouter comes on an extremely cool “wave” bracelet, which basically uses a repeating chevron pattern to make the slinky bit of metal appear to be in motion.

During World War II, a dozen watch manufacturers came together to make models following a strict set of specifications for the British Ministry of Defence. One of those brands was Longines, which produced this strapping piece.

You didn’t think we’d wrap this list up without a gorgeous yellow-gold Daytona, did you?

The ultimate fusion of sport and luxury watches. Heuer supplied this ’70s-era Carrera to Ferrari’s racing team, including legendary drivers like Jacky Ickx, Niki Lauda, and Clay Regazzoni.

This Patek is cool, but the bracelet is insane.

This isn’t your average Patek Philippe Nautilus. PP began producing this reference 3800 in 1981 as a smaller counterpart to the original. The few times I’ve gotten to try one on, I’m always struck by how perfect the proportions are. It’s nice and thin and looks just right on the wrist.

The United States Merchant Marines’ logo really punches up this Day-Date.

The ref. 6541 is the most beloved version of the Rolex Milgauss. It was produced in the ’50s and features a signature honeycomb dial. One sold for $2.5 million at auction in 2023.

Is there a better way to time your yacht race than with this Heuer Skipper? Probably not.

It’s good to have doubles, especially when it comes to vintage Rolex Explorer IIs.

#6802. Martine Rose Is Balenciaga’s New Creative Director

Martine Rose is Balenciaga's new creative director. The London-based designer will start on November 16, taking over the French-Spanish label from Pierpaolo Piccioli, whose departure was announced yesterday after three seasons at the house.

It's the first time Martine Rose has taken charge of a major fashion house, but it isn't her first Balenciaga gig. She worked as a menswear consultant under Demna from 2015 to 2018, helping shape the oversized proportions and left-field clothes that defined his early years at the brand. Now she's returning with control of the whole enterprise.

“It is a true privilege to be appointed creative director of Balenciaga, a house with an extraordinary heritage and a fearless creative spirit,” Rose said in a statement. “Balenciaga has been shaped by remarkable creative talents before me. I am committed to honoring this legacy and look forward to creating the next chapter in this rich and beautiful story.”

Rose founded her eponymous label in 2007 and has built it into one of the most influential brands in British fashion. Her collections pull from soccer and nightlife club culture, music, and the everyday wardrobes of London dwellers: suits get stretched out or squeezed in, tracksuits become tailoring, loafers turn into mules. It's menswear, on her terms.

Through her career, Rose has collaborated with Nike, Clarks, Supreme, Stüssy, and Tommy Hilfiger, and her clothes have been worn by everyone from Kendrick Lamar to Lewis Hamilton to Timothée Chalamet. But despite her idiosyncratic influence over what menswear looks like now, Rose has yet to have worked the top job at a house of Balenciaga's size. Her appointment makes her the first woman to lead the brand in its 109-year history. Her appointment makes her the first woman to lead the brand in its 109-year history.

“Martine Rose embodies this spirit with a fresh, fearless and deeply contemporary vision,” said Kering CEO Luca de Meo. “Her connection to the new generation and her instinctive understanding of culture bring a new dimension to the house.”

It's a big appointment, and one fashion people have wanted for a long time. Rumors that Rose had signed with Balenciaga have been swirling for weeks, but now it's official. The house hasn't yet said when her first collection will arrive, but whenever it does, we'll all be watching.

A version of this story originally appeared in British GQ.

#6799. Can Rian Johnson’s New Camera App Make Your iPhone Photos Look Like His Movies?

Shooting beautiful photos with an iPhone isn’t always the simplest task. Though the cameras are serviceable for a phone, Apple’s complex, built-in processing usually spits out vibrant but flatly lit images. Enter Knives Out and Star Wars director Rian Johnson and his longtime cinematographer Steve Yedlin. With the help of programmer Eric Johnson, the filmmakers recently introduced Chromagraph, a new camera app for iOS built on the same color science Yedlin developed over many years for their movies.

Open up the app, and you’re met with a relatively simple interface—in some ways, even simpler than Apple’s stock camera app—that serves an even simpler purpose: taking great pictures. Snap a photo, and the app captures an image in RAW format, containing the unprocessed data directly from the camera’s sensor. Then it applies Yedlin’s color science, creating a very “filmic” look with more contrast, grain, halation, and an overall color curve resembling classic film stocks. The user can then adjust the look of their pictures any which way they want using the provided sliders.

Chromagraph is hardly the first replacement camera app for the iPhone—and yes, it’s iPhone-only for now. Apps like Halide and Adobe’s Project Indigo offer a lot more options and might be more fit for professional use. But to Johnson and Yedlin, Chromagraph is built to reflect the more point-and-shoot quality of a smartphone camera, keeping its UI simple and its feature set relatively small, giving the average user an easy new tool for taking beautiful photos with the device they carry around every day.

A week after it went live, Johnson and Yedlin sat down to talk about what inspired them to build Chromagraph, reverse-engineering Apple’s own processing techniques, the often misguided debate between film and digital, and the magic of taking a beautiful picture.

Rian Johnson: With love. [Laughs]. I take a lot of pictures with my phone. I'm an amateur photographer, and I have a film camera that I use. I have a few other cameras, but the reality is, this is what I walk around with all day long. I had gone through different versions of taking regular pictures and pulling them into photo editors and trying to get them to look the way I wanted them. And then I started messing around with third-party apps to take RAW photos and then taking those into editors, and that gave me a little more control. But I realized what I really wanted was to be able to take a picture with my iPhone and have it, at least to start, look the way that our movies look, which are done by taking digital cinema files and Steve applying color science to them. I asked Steve, "Could we do this on an iPhone?" And I think Steve was a little hesitant, just because the iPhone sensor, while it's very good for a phone sensor, it's still a phone-sized sensor. It's still pretty noisy. There's a lot of limitations. But come on, let's just try.

Steve Yedlin: The reason that you can get better quality images out of the phone—even though, as Rian said, the sensor is great for a phone—is this burst-merge methodology, where you're actually taking several shots at different exposures and merging them to reduce noise in the shadows and extend latitude in the highlights. And while it's computational, it's real photographic data. It's not generated or artificial. It's photo data. Since Rian wanted to make it into a camera app instead of just processing. As Eric, my coding partner, and I dug into it, we realized on the Pro iPhones, as a developer with the API, you can request Apple's ProRAW that's already done that burst-merge thing. So with the Pro phones, it was a little bit easier, but with the non-Pro phones you can’t, as a developer, request that from the camera. It just won't do it. So we made our own burst-merge for non-Pro phones. We knew that if we were actually gonna give this to other people, and it's not just for us, we know more people have non-Pro than Pro. It would be too bad if every non-Pro phone has reduced latitude and higher noise.

Yedlin: That was about nine or 10 weeks. We got 20 years of color science and then nine or 10 weeks to bundle it for iOS.

Johnson: Well, I guess we use the word "look" kind of liberally. One of the fun things about the app, I think, is that it starts out just looking good. It does have a perspective on what looking good means, and starts out like that. What I love is you're starting out with an image that doesn't look like an iPhone picture. It doesn't have the flattened HDR dynamics. If there's some kind of garish light source in the frame, it handles that the way, to my eye, a film camera would. It doesn't do any kind of digital freaking out with it. It just looks like a beautiful, mellow, very filmic image. And then, it's not even necessarily that it looks like our movies. What I've been enjoying recently is seeing how far I can push it using the different controls in the app and how many different quote-unquote looks I can actually achieve starting from the base.

Johnson: Well, I mean, again, there's a little bit of a thing right now in movies and in visual culture where “filmic look” has come to mean a very specific thing.

Johnson: The thing is, when we say “filmic look,” the reality is, movies that for nearly a century existed on film had so many different looks to them. I guess just the notion that film looks a certain way is part of a bigger thing right now that I enjoy pushing back against. But I'll say, with the app, it looks like you started with a medium like film, not an iPhone sensor.

Yedlin: If you think about the pre-digital days where there were only a couple of film stocks that were barely different from each other, and maybe one or two print stocks that were also barely different from each other, but you got all these movies look totally different ways. You're never going to say that a Gordon Willis movie looks like a Vittorio Storaro movie. There's so much craftsmanship that goes into it. And this thing isn't the photographer; it's just the photo processing.

It’s just like, we’re not going to use a different recipe if Wake Up Dead Man is more dark and gothic with horror movie elements, and Glass Onion is more sun-drenched Greece. That's in the content. That's the artist, the craftsmanship of shooting it, so they look totally different. The whole thing about a processing system is that it looks artful no matter what that content is. It's not making the “look,” or the craftsmanship, or the content, but it's always going to look artful. The app does very much use the model of how film dyes work.

Johnson: And the reality is we both grew up watching movies shot on film. We came up together shooting stuff on film. We love that look. So that's a personal subjective thing.

Johnson: On the most zoomed-out level, what you just described, “you shoot it and it looks good,” it describes when I just shoot with my Leica M6, when I shoot with a film camera. If you get the exposure right then it's kind of dialed in. It also, though, describes how I've gotten used to shooting our movies when we shoot them digitally. We don't have a digital technician on set tweaking all the stuff. What Steve does is, he's created his color science and a LUT that basically interprets what you shoot, that's preloaded on the camera. So we can really just shoot with the Alexa [digital camera] as if we're shooting with a film camera. We can just meter it, and we can go, and we'll know that what comes out is gonna be in the pocket of what we want.

The other thing about shooting with your phone: very often, you just see something, you pull your phone out of your pocket, you want to be snapping as soon as you can. So the simplicity is really reflective of what you use it for. Honestly, one of the things that we kept coming back to was how smart Apple's UI design was in their camera app, which makes sense. They're great designers. We would try to do something clever, and then it would be funky, and then we’d go back to the camera app and say, "Well, how did Apple do it?" We're also not UI designers. We're still tweaking this. We're still taking any suggestions anyone out there has. This is very much still a first pass at it.

Johnson: For me, I open it up and it works just fine. For me, it's literally just my thumb naturally tends to be here when I do this, so I want it to be there when I press this. It's things like that, until you really start spending hours using the app, or put it out there and have people using it, that you know what you need to fix.

Johnson: First of all, it's been extraordinary and really emotional for both of us to see the beautiful photos that people are taking with the app. We really made this just so we would have it. It was almost an afterthought, putting it out there. What I love, day to day, is I'm taking more photos with my phone and it makes me think about light differently.

Johnson: It's not just capturing everything. Suddenly, when I see a scene and I'm taking out my phone to shoot it, I'm thinking about the contrast of the sunlight versus the dark areas of the frame because I know there's going to be more disparity between them in this finished image. I'm thinking more filmically and photographically, which is fun.

Johnson: It’s a point-and-shoot camera. The thing is, even though we want it to look good, there's a very specific use for your phone in taking pictures, which is exactly what you describe. You don't want to be sitting there fiddling with a bunch of settings. You want to pull your phone out and snap what's right in front of your face. We weren't aiming to make a tool on the level of a SLR camera, a DSLR, or even a good mirrorless full-frame camera.

Yedlin: I think that simplicity—we don't have a million looks, the simple UI, the point-and-shoot—it's all part of the same thing, which is the joy of photography. It’s what's so fun about seeing everybody enjoying it and snapping these pictures. It's not a professional camera, and it also admits what the iPhone can and can't do. We've had some people ask for more professional, manual controls over exposure and stuff like that, but the bottom line is that at the core of it, there's only certain things that camera can do. There's both the physical restrictions, but also the restrictions of what Apple even allows you, as a third party, to request from the camera. So part of it is just stripping all that out and not pretending that it's this pro camera.

Johnson: No, we haven't really considered that. But I'm all for anything that's fun. Somebody else asked me a question, phrased in a different way: could you have a “Wes Anderson” look? Or noir, black-and-white film, or this or that? You can achieve any of those looks with just a little bit of messing the sliders around. The idea of thinking of it as this kind of magic filter you put on that instantly makes it look like that is… I don't know, that seems less fun to me.

Yedlin: Also, I think it's a misattribution. I mean, Wes Anderson shoots on the same film stocks that everybody else shoots on. It's not like when you snap the camera, it's going to make everybody stand square to the wall and make Luke Wilson be in your shot.

Johnson: That's one of the things I enjoy about it. And actually the same way that I do with film, I love the little gap between [looking through] the viewfinder and then opening up the photo. I develop my own film, and it’s a little bit like that magic moment when you pull it out of the final rinse and go, “What did we get here?” That's a kind of a feature, not a bug.

Johnson: Yeah, but also I would say there's a difference between how I think about it when we're shooting our films professionally—we want the entire film to have a consistent look, and we talk about what that look is—and as an amateur, just shooting pictures for my own pleasure, where I much more enjoy the thing I just described. I don't want to sit there in a DI suite and tune it. The notion of having it be kind of, snap it, and then, “Ooh, what did I get?” And then, “Ooh, what can I do with it?” It’s a bit more like opening a Christmas present as opposed to engineering a toy from the ground up.

Yedlin: And the other thing is, the fact that it's possible doesn't mean that you know how to do it. The reason we have all of our own complicated math is because we can't do this with any of the available sliders that would be in a color corrector or a photo editing app, or anything like that. When we shoot our films, we're seeing it live. We don't have to wait for the process. To me that's really satisfying. There's a lot of movies where the color grade is such a heavy lift, where they're inventing every shot from scratch. We save a lot of time because the overall look starts with what we want, and then we can use that limited time to really finesse it and for the colorist to do his magic.

Johnson: Yeah, it's not a moral stance. The first Knives Out was the movie where we really switched over to shooting full digital, and it was a long, long, long discussion to the point where Steve and I only made the decision because our line producer came into the office and was like, "You have to make this this decision because we have to order a camera package."

Johnson: That's a big part of it. Steve said during that discussion, "It's honestly easier for me to make digital look like film than to make film look like film.” Because it has to go through a digital backend process regardless, and all you're doing is adding steps in terms of the scan and the limitations of all that. With The Last Jedi, where we shot a combo of film and digital, we had some frustrations with both the physical film stocks and also the cameras themselves. Work was being put in jeopardy because this is literally antiquated pieces of equipment that you're working with.

I don't want to just shit-talk film or the process. It would be fun to accept some limitations and say, “No, for this one we're going to shoot on film again.” But I guess the other thing that I really fundamentally agree with Steve about, an argument that he made that really landed with me, is just to let go of the notion of giving up your control and your artistry, in terms of what you want it to look like, to a brand name. “It’ll look that way because I'm shooting on X or Y or Z.” To kind of take control back. “I want it to feel like this, I want it to look like this. How do we achieve that?” People who shot on film for years and years and years, for decades, for the past century, did that. They would bend it to their will. And I think today there's a little bit of a magic thinking that started to permeate visual culture, which is you shoot on film to get the “film look.”

Johnson: I’ve taken beautiful shots with the stock app, absolutely. As somebody who loves cameras, who loves tech, who loves amateur photography, that just goes with the territory. There's a thousand different ways to do it. There are people taking vibrant, large format digital shots that I think are astounding, and there are people taking glass plate, eight-by-ten photos with a camera from the 1800s that I think look absolutely amazing. It’s just enjoying the process and getting something you think is beautiful.

#6794. Bob Dylan Just Dropped a 39-Disc Box Set. I Listened to Every Single One

It showed up in my inbox on a Tuesday afternoon without any particular fanfare, just an email from Legacy Recordings with a link to a stream. Artist: Bob Dylan. Title: Rolling Thunder Revue — The 1976 Live Recordings. Total Tracks: 381.

The link brought me to a bare-bones Sony Music web page, a couple light grey boxes arranged against a dark grey background. A small window in the middle of the page occupied maybe 10% of the available space. Inside the window were lines of text with lengthy, baroque file names, one on top of another. You could see about five at a time, starting with the first:

Bob Dylan_The Rolling Thunder Revue The 1976 Live Recordings_G0100060414779_01_01_Visions of Johanna_USSM12603067_CT122.

Bob Dylan_The Rolling Thunder Revue The 1976 Live Recordings_G0100060414779_01_02_If You See Her, Say Hello_USSM12603068_CT122.

This, it hardly needs to be said, is not the ideal way to familiarize yourself with the 31 hours and 18 minutes of new music included in the latest box set from Bob Dylan Industries. Like previous installments of the Live Recordings series, 1976 is an exhaustive accounting of (nearly) every second of (nearly) every show and concert given by Bob Dylan on a legendary tour. If a document of a performance from this tour exists, it is here, whether you want it or not.

There’s a certain thrill to a release like this, especially for the Dylan mujahideen, those of us willing to engage in the struggle. The 1976 Live Recordings is best thought of as a choose-your-own-adventure set, allowing you the opportunity to ramble along with the Rolling Thunder Revue at whatever pace you find comfortable. You can dip in on St. Petersburg (4/23/76) for the first recorded edition of “Idiot Wind,” then mosey on to Gainesville (4/25/76) for the only-ever performance of “Dink’s Song,” then settle in for a long date with Oklahoma City (5/18/76), one of the strongest complete shows. And if you want to summit the whole mountain—well, hit “play” on Lakeland (4/18/76) and let it rip. Just remember to stay hydrated.

As exciting as the sheer volume of new Bob Dylan music is, these ultra-complete tour retrospectives can be an intimidating listening experience. This is especially true in the case of The 1976 Live Recordings, which captures not the crackerjack bonhomie of the inaugural Rolling Thunder Revue, but the second, flabbier incarnation—halitosis and head scarves rather than whiteface and roses.

Dylan caught lightning in a bottle with the first Rolling Thunder, trucking around northeastern college towns with his ramshackle rock carnival while simultaneously 1) shooting an autofictional art film, and 2) trying to piece his broken marriage back together (partially by forcing his wife to act in said art film). The looks were immaculate, the vibes heady, the performances electric. Just peep a few minutes of a 1975 “Isis”—talk about the ghost of electricity howling in the bones of a face. The music press tailed the show from town to town throughout, breathlessly reporting on the latest feats of derring-do from Bob and co-conspirators. Rolling Thunder became a viral sensation, a meme, an object of FOMO decades before the term was invented.

For Dylan, it was the culmination of a thrilling 24-month period in which he’d returned to touring, recorded three brilliant albums, and reclaimed his reputation as the most vital force in rock songs. Taken together, Planet Waves, Blood On The Tracks, and Desire, along with the Before The Flood tour in ‘74 and Rolling Thunder in ‘75, seemed a clear statement of intent. You can’t repeat the past? Whaddya mean you can’t, of course you can.

Riding high on good will and good press, Bob got the band back together the following year. It was only natural. The first Rolling Thunder had been an experiment of sorts, a burst of creativity fueled in large part by spontaneity and esprit de corps. There was a real “let’s put on a show!” energy to the proceedings. Tour dates were announced only a few days in advance, the itinerary unfolding according to the whims of the man in the wide-brimmed hat, and concerts were given not in theaters, but in New England’s stuffiest gymnasia and rotary halls.

The mercurial, DIY approach was a big part of the magic, but it also limited the tour’s reach. The sequel would be a professional operation: fully produced, well-rehearsed, hosted in cavernous coliseums across the American South. Better, boozier, browner.

Those latter two descriptors certainly applied to Rolling Thunder ‘76. As for the former, it depends on who you ask. The energy of the ‘75 shows was dialed back considerably, the wiry charge dissipating somewhere between the action on stage and the tens of thousands in the stands. It would have been impossible to reproduce the energy of a room like Plymouth’s War Memorial Auditorium (capacity 1,500) in a place like Fort Collins’ Hughes Stadium (capacity 30,000), and Bob didn’t even try. Instead, he and the band dialed up the cream quotient, turning the sound into something thicker, a little less wild. If Rolling Thunder ‘75 was a bantamweight, dodging and dancing around the ring, ‘76 was a heavyweight—slower, steadier, with a brutal left hook.

Some of the best Desire cuts (“Hurricane,” “Sara”) were stripped from the ‘76 setlists, replaced with Blood On The Tracks material that neatly mapped the emotional geography of Dylan’s crumbling marriage. Most shows included some, if not all, of the following: “Tangled Up In Blue,” a tale of missed connections and cruel fate; “You’re A Big Girl Now,” a bitter, disillusioned sequel to “Positively 4th Street;” “Idiot Wind,” the breakup song to end all breakup songs, a kiss-off to both a former lover and a former self; and “You’re Gonna Make Me Lonesome When You Go,” a sweet little number written, not coincidentally, about the young A&R rep Bob had been seeing on the side. To the surprise of no one, Sara Dylan would file for divorce early the following year.

Listening now, it’s clear that the live Blood material is the lifeblood of Rolling Thunder ‘76. Bob is invested in these songs like they’re written in his soul: performances of “Idiot Wind” and “Shelter From The Storm” highlight every show in which they appear, and “Tangled” is always interesting, even if it’s occasionally more tuneless than it ought to be. The other stuff is more hit-and-miss.

Take the 4/22/76 taping for the Midnight Special television program, which notably lacks any Tracks tracks. Instead, Bob and company meander through an abbreviated selection of greatest hits in a vain attempt at pleasing the crowd (and, presumably, network execs hoping to sell ad spots against the programming). It’s the Dylan pupu platter: a few “protest” songs, a few mid-Sixties rockers, and a couple contemporary cuts as a sop to the actual fans present. Several Rolling Thunder cast members make appearances—Joan Baez, Bobby Neuwirth, Roger McGuinn—but no one’s heart seems to be in it. Dylan was dissatisfied with the results at the time, and he refused to allow the performance to air. Now here it is a half-century later, available for all of us to judge ourselves. Turns out Bob made the right call.

I can’t help but compare The 1976 Live Recordings to last year’s salvo from BD Industries, Through The Open Window: The Bootleg Series Vol. 18 1956-1963. That volume covered plenty of well-trod ground, tracing Bob’s earliest days in the Greenwich Village folk scene. Still, producers Sean Wilentz and Steve Berkowitz managed to string the collection’s hundred-odd tracks into something coherent and compelling. The result is a release that is intentional, engaging, and, despite its marathon length, essential.

The same cannot be said about this latest set. On a personal level, I find 1976 Bob much more compelling than I do 1962 Bob—I like my Dylan aged, like a fine Cabernet. But the experience of listening to The 1976 Live Recordings is somewhat less satisfying than that of Through The Open Window. Any time I return to Sony Music’s cursed promo portal, I feel like I’m pulling the lever of a slot machine. Sometimes I hit a jackpot like “Going Going Gone,” with its revised lyrics about “diamonds and rust,” a nod—or a shot?—at Baez. Other times I get bupkis—yet another by-the-numbers edition of “Oh Sister.”

It’s worth noting that The 1976 Live Recordings, like previous releases of the 1966, 1974, and 1975 tours, isn’t being marketed as part of the Bootleg Series. The first Bootleg volume—released all the way back in 1991, now closer to the beginning of Bob’s career than it is to the present—set a high standard for classic rock box sets. Deep, voluminous, and impeccably sequenced, the inaugural release presented an extraordinary alternate history of The Great American Artist, one nearly as strong as the actual history. Even if selections were weighted toward Dylan’s first decade, a number of brilliant recordings from Bob’s wilderness years were also included. See “Lord Protect My Child,” a powerfully vulnerable ballad from the Infidels sessions. See also the alternate take of “When The Night Comes Falling From The Sky,” featuring the hot-rod stylings of Roy Bittan and Little Steven Van Zandt. “Blind Willie McTell,” “Foot of Pride,” “Series of Dreams”—the list goes on and on. My favorite Bob record shifts with the seasons; there are simply too many colors in the man’s mind to settle on one. Sometimes, however, the answer to that question is the last disc of the first volume of the Bootleg Series. It is that good.

Whatever its merits, I won’t ever say the same about The 1976 Live Recordings. Rather than present a considered set of highlights from the ‘76 run, Bob Dylan Industries has simply thrown open the gates of the vault. The words of William Mulholland upon the opening of the Los Angeles Aqueduct come to mind: “There it is, take it.” Those of us familiar with the Expecting Rain message boards have had it for years—most of it, anyway. I had my fingers crossed that someone would have rustled up a tape of Salt Lake City (5/25/76), which featured the only-ever performance of “Lily, Rosemary, and the Jack of Hearts,” but no such luck. If a tape of that show does exist, it’s still sitting in a tomb somewhere, waiting for someone to ride through the devilish cold and find it.

That, more than anything, is the central disappointment of The 1976 Live Recordings: there’s very little here that hasn’t already been in circulation for some time. Of course, bringing new and heretofore unknown delights to light was never the primary purpose. Neither was telling a story in the manner of Open Window. A release like this will surely boost the cultural footprint of Rolling Thunder ‘76, particularly amongst lower echelons of Dylan fans—those who do most of their listening through YouTube and have no idea what a FLAC is—but that’s more a happy byproduct than a raison d’etre.

The real aim here is purely legal: the maintenance and extension of copyright. EU law dictates that recordings published within 50 years of their creation are protected for 70 years following their publication. Miss that deadline and the recording enters public domain, allowing any interested parties to do with it what they will. This simple fact explains the last decade-plus of releases from Bob’s archives: The 1966 Live Recordings in 2016, The 1974 Live Recordings in 2024, and now The 1976 Live Recordings in 2026. There’s a lot of juice left to squeeze in these lemons, and Sony Music is intent on squeezing them.

The general attitude of Bob Dylan Industries seems to be that any scrap of music ever made by Dylan is worth preserving and exercising ownership over. There is a cold, calculating logic behind these box sets, one which prioritizes financial motives over the music itself. The 1976 material would benefit greatly from the deft hand of a curator, someone who had dedicated the time to separate the wheat from the chaff (releasing the complete Fort Worth show on streaming services is a poor substitute). This careful editorial approach is what makes the inaugural Bootleg Series release or Through The Open Window such powerful collections. In this case, however, it would yield an incomplete copyright arrangement. So here we are instead.

Fine by me. Even if The 1976 Live Recordings ends up being too much of a good thing—seriously, if I ever hear another “Vincent Van Gogh,” it’ll be too soon—I can’t say I’m dissatisfied with the finished product. If anything, it makes me excited for the years to come, as later Dylan tours approach their semicentennial.

I’ve long been obsessed with the 1978 shows, for instance. That year’s incarnation of Dylan featured radically glitzy rearrangements, powerhouse Street-Legal cuts, and a plethora of ruffled shirts. The At Budokan live album documented a specimen from the early days of the tour, but the best performances came at the end, as Bob refined the schmaltzy big band sound into something soulful and desperate. I’ll be first in line for a hypothetical 50-disc The 1978 Live Recordings set. Then there’s the gospel tours of ‘79 and ‘80, and the “Bob Is Back” run from ‘81, and the butt rock and eyeliner era from ‘84… It goes on and on.

The important thing is that this music was made, and now it is being made available. Copyright holders and Dylan obsessives can agree on that much, even if we’re getting different things out of a release like The 1976 Live Recordings. We always did feel the same, we just saw it from a different point of view.

#6793. From the Archives: Herb Ritts’s Very Glamorous Take on Cabin Life

“First-class Cabin,” by Hamish Bowles, was originally published in the December 1996 issue of Vogue.

For more of the best from Vogue’s archive, sign up for our Nostalgia newsletter here.

With his predilection for stars, beauties, and bodies on a heroic scale, it was perhaps inevitable that Herb Ritts would be drawn to the epic vistas of Santa Fe. In fact, the photographer discovered the area almost by accident when, five years ago, his private-jet-setting aunt, Allene LaPides, who has a local ranch, inaugurated her glamorous Santa Fe art gallery with her nephew’s “Men/Women” show. “Jack Woody, my publisher at the time, had bought a house there,” Ritts remembers, “and I just loved the feeling; something grabbed me. I felt very akin to the land.” On a “whim,” he set out to look for property. “I wanted something remote and not adobe’d out!”

What he found was a house high above the piñon-bristled plains and foothills of Santa Fe, on the side of a mountain that towers so high that its peak is sometimes veiled by mist. With its heart-stopping panoramic views shadowed by ever-changing cloudscapes out of Georgia O’Keeffe, he knew that this would be the perfect retreat from his hectic Los Angeles life.

Gradually, Ritts extended the property, last year acquiring 150 acres of forest land on the far side of the mountain. Here, on a rocky outcrop above the groves of towering ponderosa pines, a rugged ten-minute drive away from the main house, Ritts set an old chair so that he could contemplate the magnificent views. Serendipitously, an old well was discovered on the property. Because of this, Ritts began to consider the possibility of building a log cabin, a romantic retreat for himself and his friends.

After working with his mother, the interior designer Shirley Ritts, to transform the original house (Herb had envisioned minor adjustments, but his mother cantilevered the structure out on all sides to add volume, raised the ceilings, and installed her trademark spacious tiled bathrooms), Ritts went on to create, a little farther down the hill, the most glamorous pool house this side of San Simeon. It is here that Shirley Ritts sits discussing their latest collaboration.

A latter-day Anita Loos with vast, limpid eyes, a deep-chestnut pageboy, and dazzling reserves of energy, Shirley Ritts is slumped into a paisley velvet armchair, her Dietrich legs (clad in her habitual leggings and biker boots) slung insouciantly over one of the arms. She confesses that she is running on half-speed after working to get the cabin finished in time for Vogue’s “totally unrealistic” deadline. If this is half-speed, gale-force Shirley must be something to be reckoned with.

Flashing an irresistible smile, Ritts mère playfully recalls her son’s original concept for this, the first house they built together (she has decorated his houses in L. A. and Malibu). “Herb wanted a ‘little’ pool house,” she says with a disparaging sniff, gesturing toward the epic land and sky beyond the windows that run the 45-foot- length of the drawing room. “Well, you can’t do ‘little’ here!”

She is equally wry on the log cabin’s history. Initially, Ritts decided to take on the project single-handedly. But after weeks of attempting to juggle his pan-global professional life and on-site meetings with the contractors, he turned again, inevitably, to his mother. It was over dinner, and Mother recalls it thus: “Herb said, ‘Mom. I have the drawings—please look at them.’ With that she takes the plans out of his little folder. I said, “No, honey, I wouldn’t think of it. It’s your project, and it’s great. I don’t know the first thing about a log cabin, and I don’t want to know.” Well, he nagged and whined, so I took the plans home. I said, “I don’t know when I’m going to have the time to look at them.” I was going to bed, and I saw those plans lying there, so I just had to look. Fatal. I mean, the only door there was the front door! I found myself at two o’clock in the morning with tracing paper and scale paper, playing with the plans. Herb asked me what I thought. I’m very diplomatic. I said, “It’s horrible!”

Her son’s original desire for a “little rustic shack with no electricity and a lantern” was clearly going to have to undergo some modification. A thoroughly chic log cabin is what he got, and rustic touches have been scrupulously confined to the structure itself. The pursuit of perfection began with the raw materials. With cabinetmaker Jeff Carbine and builder John Wolf at her side, Shirley combed the local lumberyards, personally selecting each of the individual planks required.

Herb, proving he is unquestionably his mother’s son, had some pronounced ideas of his own. The two fireplaces that anchor the cabin’s design were constructed not of the pink-gashed local stone on which the building sits but from the beautiful elliptical river rocks found in the dry arroyos of Villanueva. “Herb was real particular that they all be the same color,” recalls one of the stonemasons. Ritts and his mother shopped together for the cabin’s furnishings in Los Angeles, Paris, and points exotic. (“I’m always lugging things back from my travels,” notes Ritts. “It’s great to have those things around, reminding me of adventures.”) Late-seventeenth-century chairs sit on Agra rugs as elaborately worked as Mughal miniatures. Thirties French club chairs jostle the mission-style furniture of Ritts’s friend, the local Santa Fe-based designer Randolph Laub. Laub also provided most of the gutsy wood frames for Ritts’s extensive photography collection of vintage prints.

Surprises include Man Ray’s portrait of the thirties transvestite tightrope walker, Barbette; Herbert List’s surrealist study of a figure enigmatically swathed in pale cloth and holding a mirror; and Matt Mahurin’s muzzled bull terrier—a study in raw power. Ritts’s choices of his own works reveal his fascination with improbable physical perfection and abstract organic forms, and reflect the images in his current retrospective at Boston’s Museum of Fine Arts, “Herb Ritts Work,” on view through February 9, 1997. Also featured are his iconic images of the charismatic stars of our age. It is a culture Ritts was brought up in; Steve McQueen was a Brentwood neighbor, and Shirley’s decorating clients included Abigail van Buren (Dear Abby), Bobby Darin, and Cary Grant.

In spite of her illustrious decorating past, Shirley considers the log cabin “the crowning point in my career—eighteen men working to make that deadline! It’s a labor of love. It really all comes down to one sentence,” she adds with a sharp intake of breath. “It’s great working with your child. Great that he’s lucky enough that his talent has been recognized, and that he wants to invest in his environment and appreciate how he grew up… You know what I’m saying?”
