#6789. The Real-Life Wardrobe of Jamie Campbell Bower, Who Only Wears Suits If They’re McQueen

“I don’t know, I suppose I’m an artist,” Jamie Campbell Bower says with a laugh, after I ask him to describe himself. He’s correct, in more ways than one. If you’ve been consuming pop culture for the last decade and change, it’s less a matter of if you’ve seen the 37-year-old Londoner before, but where. He’s acted in some of the biggest franchises in Hollywood—including Twilight, Harry Potter, The Mortal Instruments, and Stranger Things—while also nurturing a multifaceted music career. As of this month, he’ll be heading to Middle-earth as Celeborn in the third season of Amazon Prime’s epic Lord of the Rings series, The Rings of Power.

During a hectic week of Rings of Power in New York City, Bower and I spent a quiet early morning talking about the bands that helped him find his style as a teenager, the difference between performing as an actor and a musician, and why he loves playing the bad guy.

All clothing and accessories, subject’s own. Coat by MM6 Maison Margiela. Sweater by John Lawrence Sullivan. Vintage skirt. Shoes by John Lawrence Sullivan. Sunglasses by Persol (throughout). Necklace (bottom) by Kinraden. Other jewelry by Pianegonda.

Jamie Campbell Bower: It’s complexity. With any character, there has to be a level of complexity. Playing in the shadow self is really illuminating, to be Jungian. You have to know it in order for it to not take over. I found it particularly interesting to rationale and reason, even though this character was doing terrible things. I have always found it really interesting to dig away and find the why. So much of that character was really about isolation and pain and not feeling like they had a sense of belonging in the space for them. Obviously, they find this power that gives them this sense of safety. It becomes the wall, and it gives them purpose, and it hides them from the world and from being hurt. What draws me to any character is complexity and understanding the balance. It’s almost anthropological in a way.

It was amazing. I was so terrified on day one. I’m always nervous about joining any show, doing any gig. But I remember day one of actually shooting with other cast members, turning around to Nick Müller, our A-camera operator, and I just went, “Look, Nick, you’ve been here way longer than I have. If I’m in your way, just tell me to fucking move.”

I’d been a part of Twilight as well—joined that on the second movie after watching a cultural moment explode—and I was in that position again with Stranger Things. It was so big. I was joining this new world, but everyone was wicked and super kind. I made some amazing friends. I love Millie [Bobby Brown]. She’s just the best. She’s like my little sister, and also like my big sister at times as well. She’s got so much sage advice and is so brilliant at what she does and so driven. I am in awe of her, and I was from the moment I met her.

I’ve got the hand of Vecna. I took it out on Fallon [the other] night. I told this story, but I got my bag searched in Mexico City on the way in over the weekend. We pulled up and one of the people there looked at me—her eyes got big. She said, “Vecna.” I’ve got three massive fuck-off suitcases. They’re going through them and I’m like, “Look, I’ve got the hand of Vecna in here. Do you want to see it?” And then it ended up being the quickest bag search ever. I got some elf ears with me as well, both made by the same guy, Barrie Gower.

It was the summer when I was 15. I spent it wearing a visor with spiky hair, because I used to skate growing up in super baggy jeans. I remember turning up being like, “That’s not you.” Skating is a part of who I am, but I was looking to represent myself in some way, shape, or form.

Then we started this indie band when I was 16, 17. Devonté Hynes had this band called Test Icicles. I always used to look at those guys with their skinny jeans and their big hair, and I was just like, “That’s a bit of me, that.” How skinny can my jeans get? How drain pipe-y am I? That’s where I really started to find my own identity.

I’ve been fortunate enough throughout my career to meet and work with such beautiful creative people in fashion and find myself more within that, too. I was talking about it to somebody yesterday: When you try a fit on that is not you, it’s like having a bad haircut. You don’t want to do that. I’m even the same if I’m doing a photoshoot. I always go through the rail and I’ll say, “That’s not my vibe. I can’t say that I’m going to wear that.” Of course, I’m always willing to try, I’m always willing to push. But you know yourself better as you get older.

It was from Alexander McQueen. I loved the identity of that brand, but I couldn’t afford a jacket, or a suit, or even a scarf. But the smaller pieces, the rings and the wallets, were like 100 bucks. Those were the things that I would gravitate towards and buy.

More recently, within the last 10 years, I remember coming here to New York for a show that I did called Will, going to Bergdorf, and buying a couple of pairs of Comme des Garçons trousers. As they were being rung up, I was like, “All right.” It wasn’t loads—we’re not talking thousands—but it was still a lot of money. That was probably the first big clothing purchase that I ever made. And I’ve still got the trousers. I used to wear them on stage in the band as well, which is foolish.

Got a bunch of stuff from H. Lorenzo the other day—some great shirts, some great jackets. I really like how they curate the store. My most recent purchase is a lot of stuff from the new Ann Demeulemeester collection. I actually just bought a sick leather trench from it—brown leather, which is fire.

Jacket by Thom Browne. Shirt, jeans, shoes by Ann Demeulemeester.

Going back to when we were kids: Test Icicles, the Libertines, the Rakes, Franz Ferdinand, Editors. Got to meet a few of those guys throughout my travels, which was always pretty sick. Nick Cave. Warren Ellis. Nick has got such a sharp style, as does Warren. Warren’s a bit more unbuttoned. I met Warren in Venice five years ago. He was stood at the end of the pier and I was so excited to meet him, I just bounded up. I was like, “Hi, Mr. Ellis. My name’s Jamie.” And I just blurted out to him for ages, and then I was like, “Thank you so much.” He said, "Good to meet you, mate. Bye.”

I think fluidity and being myself is always the main thing. I’ve never felt wholly comfortable in a regular suit. I remember we were invited to do a show in Venice, and the band that we were in was pretty heavy. We wore suits in an environment that for us felt almost quite subversive. The music that we played didn’t really fit that. I quite enjoyed that.

But on the whole, I don’t love suits too much. I like to keep it more interesting and fluid. I’m not saying that they can’t be interesting for other people, but I’ve never been interested [in them myself]. Apart from McQueen, but that’s because something like the McQueen shoulder is so identifiable and creates such an incredible silhouette. That’s probably the only time I’ve ever really been like, “I can wear this suit for a long time.” But yeah, I avoid anything that doesn’t feel right for me.

It’s hard to say. I do feel like so often they can blend. I’ve been so fortunate in my career, being drawn to things that feel like me and getting to explore different parts of myself on film. That does have an effect on the music that I’m making at the time.

With a film or a television series, you’re saying someone else’s words and building a character, but also at times injecting your own personal experience into that. In music, they are your words. You can make a choice to be like, “I’m going to build a world in this song to express something.” Or I can just be like, “This is me.” So, they can scratch different itches, but I think that they work in symbiosis sometimes.

The original movies came out when I was 12, so they were pretty seminal. I remember being in Hong Kong on a trip when The Hobbit was coming out. I was on my own, and I went to a cinema in a place called Pacific Place to watch it. That made a really big impact on me—it felt like a fantastical world that I could see myself in.

I started auditioning for it in 2024. Walking into it, of course, I was elated. I felt so lucky and so happy, like I was a little kid again. Like, I was 11 or 12 on stage in some provincial town outside of London, and now I get to fuckin’ walk into this Lord of the Rings world and be an elf? Dude, that’s sick.

Three non-negotiable albums that everyone… Oh, bloody Nora!

I discovered Dylan when I was like 16, when we were reading [J. D.] Salinger and Allen Ginsberg and books like On the Road. Let’s go with a classic. Freewheelin’ is old-school enough. It’s cool, pre-him going electric, which I adore as well. Highway 61 Revisited is a great record too. Take your pick between those two. Chopin’s Nocturnes are amazing. That’s a beautiful, beautiful piece to get into. Then there’s Nick Cave. I did an interview before and I chose Murder Ballads, which is a great record. But I also love some of the more recent work, like Ghosteen or Skeleton Tree. Ghosteen is amazing.

Oh, Ghosteen’s wicked. Let’s go with that. I referenced the artwork for Ghosteen on my last release. My album cover was by an artist called Thomas Cole, who did a collection of paintings about the journey from birth to death. He also did paintings of the Garden of Eden. From what I have now seen, those paintings heavily inspired the artist that did the paintings for Ghosteen as well, which is kind of insane.

So, he’s from the East. It’s the swearing.

#6787. Meet Gayane Umerova, the Woman Putting Uzbekistan’s Arts and Culture Scene on the Map

It’s been a landmark year for Uzbekistan. The country held its inaugural biennial in the ancient city of Bukhara last fall; 10 Soviet modernist buildings in its captial, Tashkent, were named UNESCO World Heritage Sites over the summer, making them the youngest structures in the world to receive that protected status; and its first permanent contemporary art museum, the Centre for Contemporary Arts Tashkent (CCA Tashkent), opened in September.

Upon meeting Gayane Umerova—the woman behind Uzbekistan’s cultural awakening—one of my first questions is whether she was surprised by the biennial’s success.

“My expectations are always very high, so it didn’t really surprise me. It was exactly what I envisioned,” Umerova says. What did surprise her: that out of the 1.8 million visitors to the Bukhara Biennial, half were domestic. “That’s a big shift in society. For someone to travel from Kokand to Bukhara [some 460 miles away], that would never really happen unless you’re visiting relatives. Now people see Bukhara not only as a historical city, but also as somewhere exciting to discover.”

Her official titles include Head of the Department of Creative Economy and Tourism of the Administration of the President of the Republic of Uzbekistan, as well as Chairperson of the Uzbekistan Art and Culture Development Foundation (ACDF), an organization she founded in 2017 to support the country’s visual artists, writers, theatermakers, musicians, and choreographers. (We’re meeting in her office at the ACDF headquarters in Tashkent, a space tastefully decorated with sleek wooden furniture made by Levantine designer Nada Debs in collaboration with Uzbek artisans—another one of Umerova’s ideas.) She additionally serves as Chairperson of the National Commission of Uzbekistan on UNESCO Affairs under the Cabinet of Ministers, was awarded France’s Order of Arts and Literature in April 2025, and is a LACMA Global Ambassador. It’s no wonder that when I ask how she spends her free time, she retorts that she has none—though she does relish family time with her husband and three daughters.

“Because Uzbekistan is not a country where you have a lot of private collectors building museums or architectural sites, everything has to start with the state support and then rise within society,” Umerova explains, crediting the flurry of activity in Tashkent—like that in Doha or Riyadh—to the attention and investment of its leadership, namely Uzbekistan’s president, Shavkat Mirziyoyev.

Uzbekistan’s large (and growing) subset of young people also makes the country ripe for a cultural renaissance. As of July 2026, more than half of Uzbekistan’s 39 million people are under 30. “You see it often in countries like Uzbekistan or Saudi Arabia, where there is a young population that they drive you,” Umerova says. “You meet people and see they’re creating things from nothing, and that’s really what motivated me to be in the art world.”

Much of Umerova’s desire to strengthen her country’s cultural ecosystem stems from her own childhood in Tashkent. With an aunt who was an artist and grandparents who collected antiquities, she grew up surrounded by the arts, though her first dream was to become an architect. Her father tried to steer her elsewhere, worried that she would be crowded out of such a male-dominated field: “He didn’t want me to end up behind the scenes of these great projects, like a secretary at a big firm.” Nevertheless, Umerova’s passion for architecture has come full circle, from her UNESCO advocacy to her partnerships with firms like the Paris-based Studio KO—which designed the CCA Tashkent—and Japanese starchitect Tadao Ando, who is currently at work on the new National Museum of Uzbekistan, scheduled to open in the capital in 2028.

Umerova received much of her education in the United Kingdom, discovering in Europe what she hoped to create at home. “I’d never seen museums like the Pompidou, which was so extravagant to me at the time,” she recalls. “I was inspired not just by the arts but the buildings that house them. I felt like I would love to do something like that in my own country.” Back in Tashkent, she served as a senior curator at the Art Gallery of Uzbekistan from 2008 to 2017, the year she started the ACDF.

The majority of Umerova’s roughly 50-person team at the ACDF has lived or studied abroad. “I think we have the stigma of not hearing or seeing our country on the map, but now the younger population really wants to own that they’re from this country,” she says. She believes it has become a “collective ambition” to strengthen the country’s arts-education infrastructure from a young age. (In service of that goal, the ACDF was behind the renovation of the Nationwide Children’s Library, which has operated out of a 19th-century mansion in Tashkent since 1965.)

This demanded, in part, a reframing of what even constituted art. “In Uzbekistan, there are a lot of artisans, but they just see the traditional way of getting their work to people, for example, through fairs. They don’t really see themselves as artists. They only see themselves as makers.” She traces that sensibility back to the fall of the Soviet Union, when, “for all Central Asian countries, it was about self-identity,” and “everything which was contemporary or modern was seen as foreign.”

While Umerova acknowledges the value of promoting Uzbek crafts, from woodcarving to papermaking, she sees no reason why contemporary art and design can’t also thrive. In 2022, the ACDF formed a partnership with the Michelangelo Foundation, marking the first time a Central Asian country joined its Homo Faber Guide, a curated digital platform spotlighting master artisans and ateliers around the globe. Uzbekistan also sent its first representatives to the Venice Architecture Biennale and the Venice Art Biennale in 2021 and 2022, respectively.

A testament to Uzbekistan’s success in Venice is London-based Uzbek artist Aziza Kadyri, who combines suzani, traditional hand-embroidered textiles, with AI. She participated in the Uzbekistan National Pavilion at the 2024 Venice Art Biennale, and last year she was commissioned for the Bukhara Biennial. (Visitors to the Victoria & Albert Museum in London can see a site-specific installation in which Kadyri responds to the museum’s historic suzani collection until January 10, 2027.)

Venice’s Biennale model has long inspired Umerova. “It’s like a living city with art,” she says, emphasizing the convenience of having most sites within walking distance of each other. With restoration at the heart of the Bukhara Biennial (the Historic Centre of Bukhara was added to the World Heritage List in 1993), Umerova conceived a master plan to ensure that the art and the impending tourist boom would not harm the city, the most complete medieval city in Central Asia. The inaugural edition saw local artists, such as ceramicist Abdulvahid Bukhoriy and metal engraver Jurabek Sidikov, transform a prayer room in a restored historic madrasa (a center of Islamic education), while participating international artists ranged from Egyptian multidisciplinary artist Wael Shawky to British sculptor Antony Gormley.

Of course, Uzbeks can now get their contemporary art fix year-round at the CCA Tashkent. Curated by Sara Raza, who specializes in postcolonial and post-Soviet art, the debut exhibition, “Hikmah”—Uzbek for “wisdom”—brings together works by artists near and far. The painstaking restoration of the building, originally constructed in 1912 as a diesel station, has also made a powerful statement about the potential of adaptive reuse.

Exterior of the Centre for Contemporary Arts Tashkent (CCA Tashkent).

The CCA Tashkent, Uzbekistan’s first permanent contemporary art museum, is housed in a former diesel station.

Conscious that modern buildings are often considered less precious than ancient sites—and therefore more vulnerable to being torn down—Umerova spent five years researching and applying for Tashkent’s Soviet modernist buildings to be granted UNESCO status. She also advocated for about 160 mosaic panels adorning residential and public buildings in Tashkent and other areas to be added to the National List of Real Estate Properties of Tangible Cultural Heritage in 2024. After an earthquake destroyed much of Tashkent in 1966, these mosaic designs were embedded into new constructions, becoming a distinctive part of Uzbekistan’s Soviet-era architectural landscape. Under this heritage status, the mosaics are protected from being dismantled, painted over, or covered with advertising.

Several more museums are underway in Uzbekistan, most notably the new National Museum, Tadao Ando’s first major project in Central Asia. The architect tells Vogue via email that he hopes the 40,000-square-meter institution “will become a spiritual anchor for its people and serve as a vibrant cultural center.” Its collection, currently housed in the State Museum of Arts (which will become a state museum of architecture—fitting, as it was one of the 10 buildings to earn UNESCO protection this year), is especially important to Umerova, as she often visited it with her parents and on school trips. The ACDF is also working on the Jadids’ Legacy Museum in Bukhara and the Silk Road Museum in Samarkand. They are projected to open in 2027 and 2029–30, respectively.

A rendering of the National Museum of Uzbekistan.

Umerova’s efforts have also reached American institutions. New York’s Solomon R. Guggenheim Museum loaned a monumental aerial sculpture by Nadia Kaabi-Linke, a Tunisian-Ukrainian artist based in Berlin, for the CCA Tashkent’s inaugural exhibition; and Umerova teases a major collaboration with the Smithsonian in 2029 that will have been 10 years in the making.

“Gayane Umerova is leading the cultural strategy for Uzbekistan with an uncommon combination of inspiring vision, executive follow-through, and a fine sense of what art means for human thriving and cohesion as well as social and economic development,” Dr. Mariët Westermann, director and CEO of the Solomon R. Guggenheim Foundation, told Vogue via email of Umerova’s “broad-spectrum” approach.

Umerova’s hope is that Uzbekistan will not just be a place more travelers want to visit, but one that they keep coming back to. “We want people to see our country on repeat,” she says. “Not just once in a lifetime.”

#6784. 11 Best Cowboy Boots in 2026, According to GQ Editors

You might think that cowboy boots are a privilege reserved for ranch hands and folks who can actually ride a horse. But with a little chutzpah, the best cowboy boots are just as adept on a saddle as they are on the subway. Is that so surprising? Before they blossomed into their fashionable forms, much of menswear’s most riffed on garments were rooted in function: cargo pants, work jackets, even suits. With enough time, they were adopted by civilians and adapted to their second lives as symbols of self-expression and creativity, not just job uniforms. Cowboy boots are no different.

In terms of styling, the classic move for western boots is to lean into their rougher side. Toss them on with some well-fitting jeans (preferably vintage boot cuts) and with a snap shirt. And that's still a great approach. But if you open up your mind and step outside of the saloon, the possibilities are practically endless. A slick pair of exotic leather cowboy boots matched with a ‘70s-inspired suit screams filthy rich oil tycoon if not Alessandro Michele-era Gucci. Or, why not mix up a preppy pair of chinos with a grungy graphic tee and some roughed-up suede ropers? The point is, you don’t have to pretend to be a cowboy to wear their boots. So treat them like any other pair of shoes in your closet and wear them however you want. Hell, we’d go so far as to call them an essential pair of boots for every wardrobe.

And if you’re having trouble, well, that’s why you’re here. We’ve wrangled together the best cowboy boots west—and east—of the Mississippi, from the budget-friendly to the ostentatious and every vibe in-between.

The good folks at Tecovas are living, breathing proof that direct-to-consumer brands aren’t all operated by algorithms tasked with solving imaginary problems. The DTC upstart might be the youngest of the bootmakers on this list, but it's quickly endeared itself to budget-strapped swashbucklers with a hankering for democratically-priced kicks.

Take, for example, the Cartwright, a handsome riff on a classic western silhouette made in Leon, Mexico, a bona fide cobbling capital of the world with over a century of expertise to draw on. It boasts hand-laid cording at the shaft, a resoleable Goodyear-welt construction, and a slew of colors and leathers to choose from, including genuine cowhide and goat leather. They’re not the cheapest boots on this list, but the relatively approachable price belies the craftsmanship involved. For $375, they’re one small step up from our favorite budget cowboy boots—but one giant leap in overall quality.

You can think of roper boots as the chilled-out version of the average western boot. Originally designed for calf roping, roper boots feature a shorter shaft and a shorter, squared off heel. These details made it easier for cowboys to dismount their horse and run after the calves they roped. Coincidentally, they also make roper boots easier to walk in which is why some people prefer them over other cowboy boots for everyday wear.

Our pick for the best roper boots currently goes to Red Wing. (Perhaps no surprise, given the brand also makes our favorite trend-proof boots.) Part of Red Wing's Heritage line, they're crafted with premium S.B. Foot Tanning Company leather, and feature Goodyear welt construction so you can have them resoled and keep ‘em going for a long time. Plus, if you’re just dipping your toes into the cowboy boot world, the minimalist uppers forgo the decorative western stitching which makes them easier to style.

If you’re after that western look but need a pair of boots that can handle the demands of a jobsite, a lot of guys will tell you to look to Ariat. That’s because they manage to blend classic cowboy style with modern-day features, and a palatable price point in perfect proportion. While they do offer a range of work boots in lace-up styles, their Groundbreaker boots are a standout with classic cowboy boot looks.

They’re made of full-grain leather, include a supportive and comfortable insole, and meet ASTM standards for slip resistance and electrical safety. Because it’s among Ariat’s most popular styles, it’s also available in a wide range of colorways, toe shapes, and even steel toe versions. Some more traditional cowboys will poo-poo the fact that these aren’t made stateside, but if you’re on a budget, you’ll get more than what you pay for.

Cowboy boots aren’t all rough-and-tumble affairs. In fact, the right pair can easily strut the red carpet or waltz into a fancy restaurant with panache. You’ll want a pair with leather that shines up well and in a darker colorway. For date nights and more formal affairs, these Tony Lamas will impress thanks to the exotic lizard skin uppers and low-key embroidery. They’re the kind of boots that can play well for the in-laws with a tailored suit or play a rock-’n’-roll star with some vintage jeans and a tight white tee.

These days, most boot brands walk in the shadows of one brand: Lucchese. They’ve been outfitting American presidents, high-profile celebs, and the Dallas Cowboy Cheerleaders for decades and it’s all thanks to their commitment to high-quality craftsmanship. Though any pair of Lucchese’s would earn you more than a few approving head nods, the most coveted are the boots that they make in their Texas workshop.

Using a range of full-grain leathers and exotic leathers like caiman alligator and pirarucu fish skin, each pair is crafted by master bootmakers using time-honored techniques. The Gavin, in their iconic Black Cherry, is a picture-perfect style featuring smooth Santini goat leather uppers with intricate embellishments, welted construction, and lemonwood pegs—made the old-fashioned way, basically. They’ll cost you a pretty penny, of course, but they'll last pretty much forever, and the drip you’ll get in return is priceless.

From Ralph Lauren’s western-inspired sub-label, these RRL boots meld high-end materials with old-world craftsmanship and feature American-tanned leather uppers, Goodyear-welted construction, and vintage-style rubber soles—all crafted in a multi-generational Texas bootmaking factory.

Frye is another American brand with proven boot quality chops. (In fact, it claims to be America's oldest continually-operating boot company.) Its Roper boot has all the same strengths as Red Wing's, and comes in a non-roughout leather.

We don't typically include the same designers twice on one list, but Tecovas are that good. The Johnny is for anyone who wants a softer, slightly more charming boot than the eternal Cartwright up top.

For fellas whose style skews a little more regular-guy than rodeo, these lower-profile, lower-priced Ropers have a Goodyear welt and Vibram outsole. Bang for your, ahem, buck.

Wythe's playing-card-inspired boots feature all the bells and whistles, including premium leather inside and out, hand-applied details, lemonwood pegs, and second-to-none construction all-around. While some brands tap the expert bootmakers in Léon, Mexico to pump out high-quality mass-appeal boots, Wythe tapped these artisans to really let them cook.

All of Yuketen's shoes are perfect, which is a big part of the reason Drake's now carries them. And while they're best known for their moccasin styles, the brand makes an exceptionally handsome cowboy boot too. Really, they're just showing off now.

The classic move for western boots is to lean into their rougher side. Toss them on with well-fitting jeans (preferably vintage bootcuts) and a snap-button shirt. But open up your mind and step outside of the saloon and the possibilities are practically endless. A slick pair of exotic leather cowboy boots matched with a '70s-inspired suit screams filthy-rich oil tycoon—if not Alessandro Michele-era Gucci. Or why not mix up a preppy pair of chinos with a grungy graphic tee and some roughed-up suede ropers? The point is, you don’t have to pretend to be a cowboy to wear their boots (though you certainly can). So treat them like any other pair of shoes in your closet and wear them however you want. Hell, we’d go so far as to call them an essential pair of boots for every wardrobe.

Peter Middleton knows a thing or two about the lifestyle having cut his teeth at Ralph Lauren and used it as a springboard for his own western-inspired brand, Wythe. “To me the beauty and shape of the Cowboy Boot (and a lot of the original design intent) lies in its curves and that’s what makes a great boot SEXY. There isn't a lot of men's footwear that has these dramatic curves, leather, and a high heel but the right cowboy boot has them all.”

He says that good cowboy boots should be made with natural leathers that age well with wear, mould to the wearer’s feet, and can be recrafted repeatedly. His non-negotiables include full leather construction from the insole to the outsole, linings, uppers, and heels. Look for Goodyear welted construction which will allow you to have the boots resoled and extend their life. The toe bug (the fancy stitching at the toe), should sit right where your foot flexes to help cover up the crease that comes with each step. Preferably, this would be corded by hand, of course.

Price points will vary, and you can get some well-made boots for around $300. The really great stuff will often be twice that much. Middleton says, “the best thing to do is wait and save up until you can buy the version you actually want because its impossible to cut corners and still deliver the product you want.” So if you can be patient, save up. If you can’t, try buying secondhand. But if you simply can’t wait, you’ll do well enough with plenty of the mid-tier brands until you’re ready to upgrade.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you.

#6782. This Golden-Hour Honolulu Wedding Shared the Same View as the Couple’s First Date

A tropical storm warning couldn’t stop Mele Hamasaki and Daniel Croix Henderson from going on their first date. “I think we both had a special feeling about each other and were just way too excited to postpone,” Mele says of their May 6, 2021 meeting. “The connection was instantaneous and life-altering.” Sitting at Tantalus Lookout in Honolulu, the two discussed nature, their creative processes, and more. “As we both had hoped, the storm never came,” says Mele. “Instead, we were blessed with a stunning sunset and an empty park entirely for us!”

“All these years later, I look at the life we’ve built together—our individual and shared dreams coming into focus, the films we’ve co-created, the communities we’ve rooted in, and the curiosity we still bring to one another—and realize our first date was a microcosm of everything that followed,” adds Daniel, an actor and filmmaker.

On New Year's Day in 2025, Mele and Daniel returned to that very same spot to watch the sunset over a picnic. Daniel had an engagement ring on hand, ready to propose. “I kept checking my pocket over and over again on the day for the ring, realizing I’d never held an object so precious to me,” he remembers. “I’ll never forget the moment when she said ‘yes!’ Tears welled up in our eyes and I felt like I’d entered a portal.” Says Mele, “At that point, I knew I was going to spend the rest of my life with Daniel but I’ve always found the ritual and ceremony of making a life-long commitment to a person and their family really profound and courageous.” When they returned to their home, Daniel coordinated with Mele’s mom and aunts to have the space adorned with flowers and vines from her “Popo’s” house. Adds Mele, “Always the romantic, this guy!”

When the bride and groom chose where to tie the knot, they decided to maintain the same view as when they first met. A friend’s family offered their historic Honolulu home, which provided a dramatic backdrop of the city and ocean. “We live in town and have the most pilina (connection) as a couple to the ridges and waters of Honolulu,” explains Mele, who grew up on Oʻahu. “So having the wedding on the south side of the island was very important to us. Our intention for having it on the peak of the Lion’s Gate Portal was to fill the world with as much aloha as we could!” Notes Daniel, “Our friend’s family opening their home to our wedding was an act of kindness and generosity that we’ll always remember.”

As a designer and producer in her day-to-day life, Mele put her skills to work when planning the wedding. “As soon as we reframed wedding planning as pre-production, everything locked into place for us,” she says. “We hired ‘department heads,’ scheduled fittings and rehearsals, managed the budget, and sent out call sheets to our guests. I used my design skills to collage and mock up everything I could think of to ensure details were visualized!” The bride also partnered with wedding planner Karisa Ramolete Hayashi of Say I Do By Karisa to direct the show. “Karisa grew up right down the street from the venue, which felt very aligned,” says the bride. “It was important for us to work with someone who was connected to Hawaiʻi in the same way we are.”

The couple also called on their community to bring their celebration to life. “I was so moved by how many friends contributed their creative gifts and energy toward preparation,” reflects the groom. “Our family members and friends from my bachelor party came to the house before the wedding several times to help prepare. It took an island of love, creativity, and generosity to bring this all together.”

That community also guided the couple as they sought out their wardrobes for the weekend. The bride kicked off the wedding weekend in an ivory look by Hawaiian designer Rocket Ahuna. She first connected with the designer when attending his debut show on the island. “He ended up designing an entire capsule wardrobe for me which I got to wear on my bachelorette in Kauaʻi and throughout the wedding weekend!” Mele says.

Mele admits she struggled to find a ceremony dress for a full year and almost wore a vintage dress she didn’t truly love. “Luckily, my very bossy cousin Aja Dang demanded I keep trying and reminded me that I should never settle,” says Mele. “My instinct told me to check out Loho Bride in West Hollywood one more time before it was too late. Aja and my friend Helix came with me and encouraged me to try on the Calico Grace Gown from Bon Bride.” The gown featured raw stitching details, a bleached calico fabric—“perfect for the humidity of Honolulu summers”—and draping that lent a sexier touch to the shape. “This was the first dress that finally felt truly me,” the bride says.

To accessorize, Mele paired the dress with a pleated handkerchief veil from Danielle Frankel, “which brought the whole look together and made me feel like an ethereal queen!” The bride’s friend Karen of Jos Mundo gifted her a custom pair of bridal shoes that were a take on the brand’s Marikina Mule. “It was so fun to go through the design process with her,” Mele remembers. “It was the first time she had her team emboss initials inside the shoe—a customization they will now be offering to all brides!” As for beauty, Mele worked with Risa Hoshino for makeup, and Jonah Dela-Cruz to craft two hair looks for the night. She says, “He understood the assignment!”

Daniel knew he “had to look sharp” for his wedding day. “I wanted to wear something with enough formality to honor the moment, while also having a free-flowing, playful, and creative twist that felt danceable and like myself,” he says. The groom looked back through family photos for inspiration as well. “My stylist Henna Koskinen and I spoke a lot about these influences, and when I tried on the Dries Van Noten suit, I felt a special energy—and everything felt right,” he says. He paired his suit with a textured Cult Gaia shirt and Saint Laurent loafers.

For his second look, Daniel worked with Isabel Sicat, designer of Toqa, to create an ensemble filled with small talismans that connected him with the ocean. “Isabel’s team did extraordinary work—every shell, bead, and charm was hand-sewn into the raw silk shantung dyed tuxedo-inspired trouser and tailcoat-like shirt,” he explains. “The cowrie shell adornment was of particular significance to me—an amulet of protection which connects generations of my ancestors. Found in West Africa and along the coasts of Georgia and the Carolinas, [it’s] known as a symbol of prosperity, protection, and power.”

When Mele’s original plan for a second look fell through, Sicat was able to craft her a complementary look to wear as she danced the hula later in the evening. “Without hesitation, she immediately stepped up to the plate and hustled to create a custom heat-treated top and pleated wrap skirt that perfectly matched Daniel’s look,” says Mele. “It was a dream to wear.”

The wedding weekend kicked off with a welcome picnic in Kapiʻolani Park. Mele and Daniel produced a public art show in the same spot with artist Noe Tanigawa a year before that commemorated the 80th anniversary of the bombing of Hiroshima and Nagasaki. “Reflecting on this project one year later reminded us of the delicacy of peace and our responsibility to live in harmony with the natural world, with our relationships, and with ourselves,” she says. The welcome event was full of connection and crafts. “At each table we had a setup to make lei with string and needle, which nourished the many days ahead—adorning our ancestral altar and decorating a lot of the areas of the wedding,” shares Daniel. “Shoutout to Karisa’s aunty for the pounds of puakenikeni and crown flower from her yard!”

The next day, the couple came together with their friends and family at their ceremony overlooking Honolulu. Mele’s mom officiated, Daniel’s mom shared a passage, and their fathers served as ring bearers and also performed a water unity ceremony. “When we were up there between the Lion’s Gate Portal and pillars Ren of Renko Floral designed, it felt otherworldly but also very quotidian—like it was just us, holding hands in the park like we always do,” says Mele. Daniel remembers the entire ceremony as deeply moving and memorable. He shares that he felt, “Elated, floating, vibing, teary, hyperpresent—witnessing Mele in these moments was truly the most beautiful sight, and holding hands with her through it all was deeply grounding.” The newlyweds remember being filled with joy (and a bit of surprise at impact) as their loved ones showered rice over them.

The rest of the evening was filled with connection, happiness, and plenty of music. After speeches, the bride’s father recited a poem as the groom’s father played bass, Hawaiian music legend Brother Noland played his hits, the bride danced hula, and the groom sang “Knocks Me Off My Feet" with their band Hawaiian Soul. “What felt the most special was watching Mele dance hula,” says Daniel. “She danced as a kid but stepped away from it for over a decade, and only recently returned to the practice. It was special to witness her magic and talent in this way, and I’ll never forget her glow!” Says Mele, “It was a total portal of love, high vibration, and celebration!”

The after-party was held indoors in a space transformed by artist Bradley Capello into a “pink jewel box” filled with mirrors, disco balls, and a large Pegge Hopper cutout. “Dancing the night away on the lauhala mats that my mom and her lauhala friends wove and resting on the Tutuvi pillows they sewed was a truly magical experience,” shares Mele. “You could feel the aloha through your feet and tush!” A low-key beach day followed, giving friends and family time to say goodbye. “Soaking in salt water was the perfect way to process everything,” says Daniel.

Both the bride and groom share that they don’t take their wedding or their love for granted. “I think celebrating love and choosing love is a superpower,” says Mele.

#6775. The Deeper Meaning Behind Kate Middleton’s Remembrance Day Earrings

Kate Middleton’s jewelry choices often carry a significance beyond their sparkle. Across this year’s Remembrance commemorations, the Princess of Wales paid subtle tributes to two of the most significant women in the royal family’s history: Queen Elizabeth II and Princess Diana. And the personal stories behind her chosen earrings offered a poignant reminder of how royal jewels are passed between generations.

On Sunday morning, Kate joined senior members of the royal family for the National Service of Remembrance at London’s Cenotaph, where King Charles III and Prince William laid wreaths in honor of those who have lost their lives in military service. Watching from a balcony alongside Queen Camilla, the Princess wore a sharply tailored black Catherine Walker coat (a new addition to her wardrobe, from a long-favored designer that Diana also loved), with a double-breasted silhouette, velvet buttons, and a high velvet collar, topped with an asymmetric wide-brimmed hat by Jane Taylor. But it was the familiar pearl-and-diamond earrings that carried a particularly personal significance.

The Collingwood Pearl and Diamond Drop Earrings once belonged to her late mother-in-law, Princess Diana. Given to Diana by London jewelers Collingwood ahead of her wedding to the then-Prince Charles in 1981, the earrings feature diamond-studded settings from which large, lustrous pearls are suspended. They became a mainstay of Diana's jewelry collection, accompanying her to glittering state banquets and royal engagements. In the years since, Kate has made the heirlooms part of her own wardrobe, continuing their story as the current Princess of Wales and wearing them on multiple royal engagements.

There was further symbolism in Sunday’s accessories. Kate fastened three red poppies to her coat alongside the HMS Glasgow Brooch, a sterling silver piece specially commissioned by Glasgow jeweler James Porter & Son in 2025 to mark her role as sponsor of the Royal Navy warship. Its design incorporates the ship’s crest, the Scottish thistle, and a depiction of St. Kentigern, Glasgow's patron saint. The three poppies, have been linked to Kate’s own family history: three brothers of her great-grandmother Olive were killed during the First World War, although the Princess has never publicly confirmed the connection.

The previous evening, Kate had reached for a different pair of royal heirlooms. Attending the Royal British Legion’s Festival of Remembrance at the Royal Albert Hall with Prince William, she wore Queen Elizabeth II’s Diamond Orbital Pendant Earrings, also known as the Queen’s Diamond Frame Earrings. The intricate jewels feature a floral diamond cluster at the ear and a cascade of graduating diamonds enclosed within a glittering frame. While their precise origins remain unknown, Elizabeth wore them on several occasions, including the State Opening of Parliament in 2012.

Kate Middleton first wore the earrings publicly in 2016 and has since chosen them for significant moments, including Princess Eugenie’s wedding in 2018 and her own official 40th birthday portraits in 2022. For Saturday’s ceremony, she paired them with a black-and-white pleated polka-dot dress by Alessandra Rich—another sentimental wardrobe repeat, having previously worn it to Prince Philip’s memorial service at Westminster Abbey in March 2022. A black suede Ba&Sh belt, pointed Alessandra Rich slingbacks, a velvet Jimmy Choo clutch, and her Irish Guards Regimental Brooch finished the ensemble.

Across two consecutive days, the Princess’s choices traced a line through three generations of royal women. From Elizabeth’s glittering diamonds to Diana’s unmistakable pearls, these were jewels carrying memories as well as history. And at a time of national reflection, Kate’s wardrobe offered its own quiet tribute to those who came before her.

#6773. 18 Best Raincoats for Men 2026, According to GQ Editors

Decades ago, your only options for rainy weather coverage were some kind of trench coat, a coarse wool overcoat, or, if you really needed to be out in it, a thick rubber sack that kept you dry but didn’t breathe. Thankfully, material science has advanced.

There are, of course, umbrellas? And we won’t stop you looking into those. But ask anyone from the Pacific Northwest, and they’ll tell you their rain jacket does all that work with ease. That’s because raincoats aren’t an accessory to your outfit: they’re the main focus. A good raincoat can turn your sad ensemble into head-turning Paparazzi fodder, or at the very least, keep you dry and stylish when it’s wet out.

Still not convinced? We figured—so we tested the best rain jackets and coats for men on the market right now, from the tried-and-true natural-fabric mainstays to the buzzy, newfangled riffs, and everything in between. Here they are.

Arc'teryx's Beta LT jacket is the pinnacle of no-BS rain defense, beloved by wannabe Everest climbers, GQ Recommends editors, and meme-savvy TikTokers alike. Its custom Gore-Tex fabrication will keep you from getting soaked during the harshest of downpours, while its breathable pores work overtime to let water vapor (a.k.a. sweat) escape. That sleek, streamlined silhouette also lends itself naturally to a range of pairings, from straight-fit jeans and sneakers to a full-on “Scaling Kilimanjaro” kit—cargos, backpack, hiking boots—perfect for all of your wildcard outdoor activities.

It’s an investment, but hundreds of guys were showering in theirs for a reason: If you need top-tier weather protection and long-term durability—and are willing to invest—this is still the best rain jacket for serious outdoor use. That said, most dudes chasing the clout that Arc’teryx affords won’t need to max out its capabilities. Still, it’s good to know that if you’re ever caught in a downpour that won’t let up, the Beta’s the one waterproof jacket that won’t leave you high and, uh, wet.

When it comes to outdoor gear that keeps up with Mother Nature, Patagonia’s among the first to come to mind. The OG gorpcore label has more than its fair share of bangers for every climate from the iconic Baggies shorts to its range of plush fleece jackets, and when it comes to rainy weather, there’s the Torrentshell jacket. It’s a canonical piece of kit for Patagonia and has kept customers dry and fly for years.

The latest version features a three-layer shell system that maximizes water-repellency and breathability with a 100% recycled nylon face fabric. It features toasty microfleece-lined handwarmer pockets, welted zippers for water protection (plus pit zips for ventilation), and the entire thing can pack down into itself. So pack it up, throw it in your bag, and bust it out whenever the weather turns out to be worse than what the weatherman says. With a feature set like that, you’d expect to pay through the nose, but we’re thrilled to say that it doesn’t even break $200.

The advancements in rain-wicking technology tickles our lizard brains as much as watching a rock-crushing machine turn a boulder into pebbles. But as much as we appreciate multi-layered DWR-coated materials and heat-sealed seams give us joy, we also have a great reverence and appreciation for rain coats of yore. Enter the waxed jacket, a time-tested fabric used by sailors off the British coast since the 1800s.

If you’re gonna get a waxed jacket, you’d best get a Barbour, the reigning champ of waxed jackets. Barbour has more options and collaborations than ever, so it can be tricky to get your head around what’s what but for our money, the classic Beaufort is the Goldilocks of wax jackets. Where the Bedale is shorter and styles like the Border run down to the mid-thigh, the Beaufort is just right but still long enough to layer over your sport coats. Whichever you choose, don’t forget to re-wax at home after a few seasons. Guys seem to be nervous to do it, but it’s easy, and everytime I’ve re-waxed mine, people compliment me on how great it looks. The reward for your grunt work is a jacket you'll keep for decades to come.

This season, J.Press has unearthed a Rare Barbour: the Burghley. It hasn’t been in the brand’s regular rotation for a number of years, earning it a following among collectors in places like Japan. But now it’s back in all its sweeping, full-length glory.

Originally designed to extend past the knees (and over the saddle and tops of riding boots) the Burghley protects the rider from rain and thorns. It’s a dramatic silhouette that will set you apart from all the cropped Transport and Bedales out there. Sure, it has equestrian heritage, but you needn’t be keeping a stable to wear one. It’s a great alternative to a trench, at home in the city as well as the country. And yes, it’s still made in England.

The perfect trench coat does exist, and Burberry, inventor of the style's signature gabardine fabric in 1879, cracked the code on it long ago. In the nearly 150 years since, surprisingly little has changed about the silhouette, and for good reason. The classic fit means it's roomy enough to layer over your slouchiest Armani-inspired suiting, while the belted design gives it instant sophistication. Plus, the lining and under-collar check pattern is unmistakably Burberry—an investment-worthy flex since the coat was invented.

Its WWI heritage is well-documented—British officers wore it in the trenches, using details like the shoulder flap for rifle recoil and belt rings for canteens and grenades serving real military functions. Today, it’s less about battlefield utility and more about how that water-resistant cotton gabardine will keep you dry in light city rain. I like the black, but the classic tan is always an option for you too.

Another entry from Vancouver—what can we say, they know a few things about rain up there. Indie label James Coward sources fabric from mills known for their quality and considered production practices, then makes its garments locally in its workshop. This coat is cut from the brand’s durable, waterproof ‘Mastershield cloth, a dense weave that offers protection from the elements. A generous silhouette and roomy raglan sleeves give it natural gravitas in gloomy weather, all while keeping the look relaxed and easy to layer.

Whereas the Arc’teryx Beta is the brand’s versatile all-mountain shell, the Alpha SV is built for technical climbing and severe weather. A helmet-compatible hood, harness-ready chest pockets and pit zips make it the top dog of storm shells.

HAVEN puts a GORPcore spin on the refined, city-ready trench made from three-layer GORE-TEX PRO, a more rugged fabric typically found in shells for climbing and backcountry skiing. It’s serious storm gear with a city sensibility, ready to make you feel invincible during a rainy commute.

The wrap coat is Lemaire’s runaway hit. But this Ventile-cotton style is a rainy-day flex: densely woven, breathable cotton that swells when wet to help keep wind and rain out, without synthetic fibres or coatings. Chic.

Menswear can get a little obsessive about fabric specs and provenance. But when a jacket is this cool, you get why. 100 percent Giza cotton, woven at maximum density in Saitama, Japan and originally developed for the British Air Force and later recommissioned by Japan’s Coast Guard. Yeah, that’s the good stuff.

Prep never left, but J.Press is certainly on its way back. The label’s updated take on a classic hooded parka keeps the familiar military shape sans PFAS with natural Ventile cotton. Choose classic army green, or eye-popping orange.

Haider Ackermann's take on Canada Goose is coming into focus. The brand’s Snow Goose sub-label is more Milan than Manitoba, trading logo-heavy designs for cool colours and modern silhouettes that go well beyond down parkas.

Making a good rain jacket might not take a degree in nuclear science, but Outdoor Research’s founder was actually a nuclear physicist turned avid mountaineer. This stripped-back shell sheds weight and packs small without skimping on protection. Stash it and forget it.

Marmot, meanwhile didn’t pull back on features to shave weight. This soft, quiet waterproof shell is built for movement, with an “Angel-Wing” design that lets you reach overhead without the hem riding up and putting your backside on display.

Reigning Champ has also gone well beyond sweats and tees. This workhorse of a rain jacket is more downtown than summit in style but still meets the moment with GORE-TEX construction and a very digestible price.

Italian fabric. Fifth-generation Portuguese construction. New York City design. That’s the mix behind Knickerbocker’s full-length trench. Its tightly woven, water-repellent fabric helps keep rain out all with the kind of easy, downtown cool Knickerbocker does so well.

Uniqlo’s even more affordable sister brand, GU, delivers a lot of jacket for the money The throwback piping and punchy colours nod to the ’90s, while the water- and wind-resistant fabric makes it practical, too.

A waxed Harrington probably isn’t for a downpour unless your trousers are waxed, too, but it’s ideal for breezy, drizzly, and sunny fall days alike. And, aye, the waxed fabric comes straight from Scotland. No messing about.

Who wants to drag a sopping wet umbrella around only to forget it at the restaurant or in the back of a cab? When you’ve got a trench coat with your cell and headphones in the pockets, it’s almost impossible to lose. Through light mists, heavy rain, and even torrential downpours, the best rain coats are, ironically, the drippiest too. They’ll prevent you from showing up to the function like a poor wet dog and instead make your arrival a warm, dry welcome.

Trench coats that aren’t the only coats vying to shield you from the rain, though. Gorpcore’s popularity has given us waves of tech-y jackets, parkas, and anoraks all wrought from futuristic multi-layered materials that balance breathability with waterproof ability. The result is a cementing of this 21st century sportswear into must-have menswear. And let’s not forget the workwear-adjacent waxed jackets born from sailing the Scottish seas in 15th-century Scotland. With their rugged good looks and a lust for patina, they’ve become stylish staples when the weather’s all doom and gloom.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6771. A Visit With Six Women Farmers Across the Americas

Women make up nearly half of the agricultural workforce in developing countries, yet they account for fewer than one in five of the world’s agricultural landholders. As rising food prices, climate volatility, and food security concerns put farming back at the center of public debate, the United Nations designated 2026 as the International Year of the Woman Farmer, bringing renewed attention to a workforce that remains central to food systems while often excluded from land ownership, financing, and decision-making.

Between January and August 2026, I spent two weeks living with each of six women farmers. In the Galápagos, María Espinoza grows food without chemicals on islands that still import most of what they eat. In Guatemala, Aurelia Beb Tec cultivates cacao on land returned to her Q’eqchi’ community through a reparations process after decades of dispossession and war. In Chiapas, Yumara Salas Ramírez became the first woman to serve as secretary of her coffee cooperative. In Haiti, Lucianie Vil, a 70-year-old Madan Sara, carries her harvest by mule to the border market of Anse-à-Pitre. In Arkansas, Samantha Noble coordinates poultry production at a ranch devoted to regenerative farming. And in Honduras, Milexy Rodríguez lost 15 cows in 40 days when drought dried up her lagoon, rebuilding by drilling a well and diversifying.

The resulting photographs follow the rhythm of their days and the pressures shaping food production where they live. They also show what these women have built: cooperatives, savings groups, a coffee brand of their own, knowledge passed on to their daughters and neighbors.

María Espinoza, 54, lives in the highlands of San Cristóbal Island, in the Galápagos. She is up at 5 a.m. to feed her animals before heading into the fields, where she grows fruit and vegetables on a plot she slowly reclaimed from invasive blackberry. She lives alone but the farm sets the rhythm of her day, keeps her company, and puts food on the table.

Fresh water on the island is scarce and depends on El Junco lagoon, while pests and food shipped in from the mainland make farming hard. María uses no chemicals, makes her own organic fertilizers, and, since 2023, has trained in protecting soil and crops without pesticides.

María Espinoza gets dressed in her bedroom before heading out to work on her farm.

Espinoza pushes a wheelbarrow loaded with freshly harvested produce. Back home, she will wash and prepare it to take to market the next day.

Espinoza tends her stall at the agroecological market organized by Heifer, where she sells her produce alongside other farmers every Wednesday.

Yumara Salas Ramírez, 30, lives in Santa Rita, a community in the Sierra Madre de Chiapas, among coffee farms and dense forest at the edge of the El Triunfo Biosphere Reserve. A single mother to her daughter Ximena, she works at the Sierra Azul Cooperative’s collection center, where she checks coffee quality and is learning to cup. She was the first woman to serve as the cooperative’s secretary and is part of Café 89, the coffee brand of the cooperative’s women producers, created to make their work visible. Chiapas grows 41% of Mexico’s coffee, yet women hold only about a quarter of Mexico’s ejido land rights. Yumara speaks openly about the machismo that still holds women back, and about a region hit by drug-trafficking violence, climate change, and pests such as the coffee berry borer. Still, she chose to stay.

Yumara Salas Ramírez picks coffee during the harvest on the hillsides of Santa Rita, Chiapas.

Salas Ramírez rakes the coffee at the processing mill to ensure it dries evenly.

Salas Ramírez and Anayanci Domínguez Vazquez smell the cups during a cupping session at the Sierra Azul Cooperative.

Aurelia Beb Tec, 44, grows cacao on five manzanas (about eight and a half acres) in Pinares, a Maya Q’eqchi’ village in Alta Verapaz. Every Wednesday she walks the slopes with her daughters Brenda, Mayra, and Mirna, picking the ripe pods and selling the harvest to the local association. She planted every tree herself. At home she roasts the beans on a clay comal, grinds them on stone, and makes hot chocolate, while her daughters learn to make bars with cardamom and milk.

For decades the Q’eqchi’ of Pinares were dispossessed by landowners and forced into servitude; then came repression and civil war. After the peace accords, a reparations program brought land, seeds, and support, and cacao proved the best crop. In 2014 the community founded APIDIP (Asociación del Pueblo Indígena para el Desarrollo Integral Productivo de la Aldea Pinares), which today has 156 members and exports fair trade, organic cacao.

Aurelia Beb Tec walks among the cacao trees in the rain, searching for ripe pods during the harvest.

Beb Tec and her daughter Brenda sort cacao in their kitchen.

Lucianie Vil, 70, is a Madan Sara, one of the women traders who travel Haiti’s roads and markets to support their families and feed their communities. She lives in Ma Rouge, a scattered village in the mountains of southeastern Haiti. Every Wednesday she walks up to her plot in Thiotte, where she harvests pineapples, oranges, passion fruit, and coffee with her husband and grandchildren, then carries her harvest down the mountain by mule and sells it at the border market of Anse-à-Pitre on Fridays. More than half of Haiti’s population now faces food insecurity, and rural trade depends on routes increasingly cut off by violence and on harvests threatened by drought. Lucianie started selling at 13 and raised seven children on what the land gave her. Like the bird the Madan Sara are named after, she never stops moving.

On a narrow mountain trail, Lucianie Vil walks ahead of a mule loaded with fruit and vegetables on the three- hour journey from Romain to her home in Ma Rouge.

Vil clears a path with her machete through the undergrowth on her plot in Romain.

Vil counts the day’s earnings with a fellow vendor at the market in Anse-à-Pitre. She reports that a day at the market brings in around $150 in sales, of which she keeps roughly $100 once transport and other costs are paid.

Before dawn at Heifer Ranch, Samantha Noble already has her boots on. She checks the mobile chicken coops, looks over the birds, and plans with her team which paddock to move that day. Born in Minnesota in 1993, she grew up around animals and ended up studying animal science.

A professor told her about Heifer International and she came to the ranch as a volunteer, first guiding school visits, then rotating among livestock species. Poultry has been her trade ever since, and today she is the ranch’s poultry production coordinator. Heifer Ranch, about 40 miles west of Little Rock, is a living laboratory for regenerative livestock farming, where planned grazing, holistic management, and animal health are researched and demonstrated. Her goal is to help other producers save time, improve their farms, and find a better balance between work and life.

Morning fog over the ranch roads in Perryville, Arkansas.

Noble, the ranch’s poultry production coordinator, walks through one of the mobile coops built on the pasture.

Noble takes two of her children to a swimming lesson.

Milexy Rodríguez, 46, lives in El Nance, a rural community in Olanchito, in the department of Yoro. Over the years, she built up a cattle farm that supported her family. But in 2023 the lagoon her herd depended on dried up, and in just 40 days she lost 15 cows. To stop relying on rain alone, she drilled a well and began to diversify, and today she raises cattle, grows chili peppers, and keeps a small apiary with her daughter. Honduras is facing a new water emergency, and producers in Yoro report a sharp drop in the milk reaching collection centers. Rural women also face barriers to credit and resources. Milexy belongs to a community savings group and shares what she has learned as a community leader and trainer.

Milexy Rodríguez, in a blue shirt and hat, and her daughter Nayeli, in white, at a cattle fair held one evening around the CREL, the community milk collection center, in a neighboring village. Organized by local ranching families, these gatherings bring neighbors together and offer a break from the daily rhythm of work on the ranch.

Rodríguez milks one of her cows on the ranch. Today she gets fewer than 10 liters a day, far below what she produced before losing her herd in the 2023 drought.

A calf feeds from its mother on Rodríguez’s ranch.

Milexy Rodríguez rides home on her motorcycle after delivering her milk to the El Edén collection center.

The project was produced with support from Heifer International.

#6767. Men Are Outspending Women on Botox. Here’s What They’re Asking For

Guys are going to increasingly wild lengths to achieve their version of the idealized man. Looksmaxxing, as we’ve come to know it, has included surgical procedures to reshape their eyes, taking a hammer to their face to create a more chiseled jawline, and breaking their legs to gain a couple inches. But an OG in cosmetic procedures is seeing a resurgence in men, too.

Chances are you’re familiar with Botox: localized injections to smoothen wrinkles in common areas like the forehead, the eyes, or around the mouth. Long viewed as a treatment limited to the ladies, it’s increasingly becoming another form of male upkeep. Now we’re learning that men are outspending women on the popular procedure. According to an October 2026 report by Guidepoint Qsight, a medical aesthetics data and intelligence provider, men spent an average of $529 per neurotoxin visit in Q3 of 2026, compared with $455 for women. (The figures include not just Botox but also competitors like Daxxify and Xeomin.)

The potential explanation is simple: Botox is an easy way to look a little younger, more well-rested, and even less angry or severe. (As a built-in bonus, it’s virtually painless—especially compared to the barbaric looksmaxxing trends circulating as of late.) According to a 2025 survey of 2,900 aesthetic plastic surgeons, men accounted for over 15% of all Botox cases that year. It was also their top non-surgical treatment by a landslide.

The growing cohort of men opting into “Brotox” believe it’s money well spent. Nancy Samolitis, MD, a board-certified dermatologist in Los Angeles and co-founder of facile, has been treating men for years but has recently seen an uptick in male clientele, aged anywhere from 35 to 70. These days, “they’re more interested in skincare and more open to procedures,” she says. Many also book their first treatment nudged by their spouse or significant other who are existing patients. Others come in simply wanting to look better but not knowing what treatment to get. In many cases, Botox fits the bill.

While the uninitiated might think the injections make guys look surprised or less masculine, good work can be undetectable. Still you, just fresher.

Kyle Waldrop, a 37-year-old product designer in Los Angeles, first started getting Botox three years ago. “I’ve always been good about keeping up with a skincare routine and taking care of myself, so Botox felt like a natural extension of what I was already doing,” he says. His goal isn’t to change his face but to slow down wrinkles before they become obvious. He gets Botox for his forehead lines and crow’s feet, and recently started treating his neck lines, too.

But it’s not always about looking younger. Men are also using Botox in less obvious places. David Kim, MD, board-certified dermatologist and the founder of Soho Dermatology in New York City, treats the chin for more projection, as well as the underarms to combat excess sweating. Bell Yoo, NP, a board-certified nurse practitioner and the founder of Mimo Aesthetics in West Hollywood, adds that she injects the masseter (and sometimes temporalis) muscles for guys who struggle with jaw clenching or teeth grinding.

Aesthetically, men usually aren’t looking for the same thing from Botox as women. For starters, many want to soften their lines without erasing them. Preserving some natural movement is key. “My female patients often love that smooth, ‘glass skin’ appearance, whereas some of my male patients think it looks too waxy or oily,” Yoo says. Men also tend to opt out of a Botox brow lift, which many women request. “Men would much rather maintain a slightly heavier, lower-set brow than end up with an overly arched or ‘Spock’ brow,” Yoo says. Per Samolitis, this requires different injection points and doses tailored for guys. Kim, meanwhile, is careful not to overdo the crow’s feet. “It looks more natural when men have lines around the eyes, and it makes their face look better overall,” he says. These techniques quell the fears that guys have about looking less masculine or overdone. For most, the ultimate goal is to look relaxed and rejuvenated—not tight and taut. “When it’s done well and with the right amount, you look like yourself, just a bit more rested,” Waldrop says. “I still have all my natural movement and expressions, which is important to me.”

All that subtlety comes at a price. Men may end up paying more than women to treat the same areas, but the higher price tag is actually expected: Men often have larger, stronger facial muscles, which require more units to achieve their desired results, Yoo says.

While more men are embracing Botox, some remain tight-lipped about it. Yoo says that most of her male Botox patients are pretty discreet. “I think there’s still some stigma around men receiving cosmetic treatments, and many would rather keep it to themselves,” she says. Waldrop, however, is an open book. “I don’t really see it as something worth hiding or feeling that weird about,” he says. “Guys are already spending money on skincare, haircuts, hair transplants, working out, and all sorts of things to look and feel better, so I don’t really see why Botox should be treated any differently.”

For Waldrop, Botox is less a dramatic intervention than another item on his grooming checklist. He doesn’t see it as something that needs to be announced or apologized for. At the end of the day, “it’s really not that deep,” he says.

#6765. All of the Best Looks From the 2026 LACMA Art + Film Gala

Last night, the 2026 LACMA Art + Film Gala took place at the Los Angeles County Museum of Art in California. The night, co-chaired by actor Leonardo DiCaprio and trustee Eva Chow, brought together influential figures from the worlds of art, film, fashion, and entertainment to support the museum’s mission to make film more central to its future programming. Artist Vija Celmins and filmmaker Denis Villeneuve also received special honors.

Before the ceremony began, the red carpet saw a legion of Hollywood A-listers sport their most grand styles. Every year, the LACMA Gala—which is supported by Gucci—produces memorable (and extremely glamorous) formal attire moments, and this evening was sure no different.

Last year saw some of newly-installed creative director Demna’s first big red carpet moments, like stars Elle Fanning and Doja Cat appearing in their boldest looks by the Italian label. This year saw things amp up even more: Kim Kardashian played with her enduring love of the cut-out in a Bordeaux-toned sequin dress, while Parker Posey looked radiant in a sparkling white kaftan. Sienna Miller made the case for the LSD (the Little Silver Dress) while Rose Huntington-Whiteley went to glamorous extremes with some serious skin on show—all were care of Gucci.

And while Gucci was well represented across the evening, striking looks were also served courtesy of Valentino, Saint Laurent, and Chanel. Want to see all of the celebrities who dominated the LACMA Art + Film Gala this year? See Vogue’s roundup of the very best looks here. And be sure to vote on who you think was the very best dressed of the evening.

Nicole Kidman at the 2026 LACMA Art+Film Gala held at LACMA on November 07, 2026 in Los Angeles, California. (Photo by Gilbert Flores/Variety via Getty Images)
